Level S

1. A Daring Road to Freedom

1Playing the part of another person can be fun. Now picture a time when staying alive depends on the role you play. In an earlier time, Ellen and William Craft took on false identities. They did this to get away from slavery. Portraits show Ellen Craft and William Craft. Black enslaved people were treated as property. They had to do whatever an enslaver demanded. White people held many rights that these people did not have. Enslaved people were blocked from learning to read. They were blocked from learning to write. A person could be bought. A person could be sold. An angry enslaver might starve them. An angry enslaver might beat them. An angry enslaver might even kill them. In January 1863, the Emancipation Proclamation received President Lincoln's signature. Freedom for many enslaved people came from that order. Not every enslaved person was included. In most states that kept slavery, every enslaved person was covered by the original order. Delaware was left out. Maryland was left out. Kentucky was left out.

2Missouri was left out. People enslaved in those four states waited two more years. The two of them, William and Ellen, were Black Americans. Slavery was the life they were born into. William's birth year was 1824. His home was Macon, Georgia. The man who owned William's family liked to gamble. A lost bet was paid by selling someone from that family. William was sold at age sixteen. His skill was carpentry. The new enslaver saw that William's work was good. He told William to make things. The things brought money to the enslaver. Ellen was younger than William by two years. A white man was Ellen's father. That man held Ellen's mother in slavery. Ellen's skin was very light. Many people did not realize her mother was Black. At age eleven, Ellen was given away. The gift was to her married half sister. Ellen's work was a servant's work. Macon became the half sister's new home. Ellen traveled there with the family. She met William there.

3Love grew between William and Ellen as time passed. They wanted a life side by side. A wedding was hard for people in slavery. Permission from the enslavers was required. Some couples belonged to different enslavers. Those couples were not allowed to share a home. In 1846, Ellen and William received permission. They married in that year. For two years after the wedding, the Crafts lived in different places. Visits happened only now and then. They feared a sale could come at any time. A sale could send one of them far away. The Crafts believed escape to the North was the only path. Only there could they live as a family. Northern states let Black people live in freedom. Still, the Crafts had to leave without getting caught. They formed a clever plan. The United States had thirty-three states in 1860. Slavery was legal in fifteen states of the South. People called those the slave states. Slavery was against the law in eighteen states of the North.

4People called those the free states. In a slave state, an enslaved Black person had none of a white enslaver's rights. A free state let Black people live in freedom. Those slave states joined together as the Confederacy. The Civil War ended in defeat for the Confederates. After that defeat, the Constitution's Thirteenth Amendment ended slavery. The end of slavery covered the United States. Every state became a free state. A map for the year 1860 shows three kinds of land. One kind is free states. One kind is slave states. One kind is territories where slavery was allowed. The Crafts' plan was bold. Pennsylvania would be the goal. It was the closest free state. Ellen's light skin made a disguise possible. She would act the part of a white person. William would come along as the servant of the house. One problem still stood in the way. Ellen could not make the trip as a woman. A woman of that time was not expected to travel by herself.

5She would not travel beside an enslaved man. The plan needed one more change. Ellen would act the part of a white man. Ellen felt fear at the start. Capture would bring punishment. She said yes after the steps were clear. William's task on the night of the escape was a short haircut for Ellen. She would put on special clothes. Those clothes would hide the shape of her body. A bandage would wrap her face. Green glasses would cover her eyes. One arm would hang in a sling. The sling would complete the disguise. Ellen had never been taught to read. She had never been taught to write. Signing a name on a ticket was impossible for her. An injured arm would explain why no name was signed. The injury would also explain William's presence. He would seem to be traveling with his enslaver. If the acting held, nobody would suspect them. They picked December 21, 1848, for the departure. William had saved money for the journey.

6The pair asked their enslavers for days off at Christmas. Enslavers often allowed a few free days. The days were meant for family celebrations. The Crafts could slip away in that gap. Days would pass before anyone missed them. When the morning came, William cut Ellen's hair. He helped her dress in the costume. They spoke a prayer. Then they walked into town. There they bought tickets for the train. In those years, travel kept people apart by race. The rules placed them in different train cars. They waited there for the trip to begin. A map traces the Crafts' escape route. The first stretch is marked December 21 to December 25, 1848. The map includes Macon, Georgia. It includes South Carolina. It includes North Carolina. It includes Washington, D.C. It includes Maryland. It includes Pennsylvania. It includes Boston, Massachusetts. A later mark says December 1850. That part shows the Atlantic Ocean. It shows Liverpool. It shows England. The United States appears on the map as well.

7All at once, William noticed a familiar person. The person stood on the train platform. He owned the shop where William worked. He had come to search for the Crafts. William slid low in his seat. Fear made him shake. The man came up to William's car. Right then, the train bell rang. The man walked away. William breathed out in relief. He had stayed safe. Ellen faced a scare of her own. A passenger took the seat beside her. She went still. The man was a close friend of her enslaver. He had known Ellen for many years. She kept her body from moving. She kept her eyes down. He did not recognize her. She acted as if she could not hear. She hoped to avoid any talk. Talk could reveal who she was. The pair reached Savannah, Georgia, without being caught. A steamer ship then carried them to South Carolina. Trouble met them there. The clerk refused to sign their tickets. At that moment, a boat captain walked past.

8He had captained their first ship. He agreed to put his name on the papers. The last test waited in Maryland. Some slave states touched the border of free states. Patrols watched those borders. The patrols searched for people who had escaped. Maryland's patrol stopped the Crafts. The patrol refused to let them continue. One officer at last felt sorry for them. He allowed the Crafts to board. They came into Pennsylvania without capture. The date was Christmas morning in 1848. Friends were there to meet them. Shelter was there too. Next, the Crafts made a home in Boston, Massachusetts. William got work. Ellen got work too. For a time, their life felt good. The calm did not hold. A new law arrived in 1850. Under that law, hiding an escapee was a crime. Danger suddenly reached people who lived in free states. How did the Crafts find their way after Philadelphia? They followed the Underground Railroad. That name stood for a web of escape routes.

9It also stood for a web of safe houses. The people who ran it helped escapees reach freedom. Routes crossed the free states of the North. The routes continued all the way to Canada. Helpers looked for Black people arriving from the South. New arrivals received a place to stay. They received food. They received directions toward freedom. Two bounty hunters came to Boston. Their search was for the Crafts. Friends hid the couple. Living in fear wore them down. They left for England in December 1850. During 1850, the Fugitive Slave Act won passage in Congress. A helper of an escapee could be fined $1,000. In those days, that fine was a large sum. Returning an enslaved person brought a reward. People called the reward a bounty. The act picked up the name Bloodhound Law. Hunters used bloodhound dogs to follow escapees. The act was law in free states. It was law in slave states too. Plenty of free states paid the act no mind.

10They would not chase escapees. They would not chase the helpers either. A poster warned Black people who lived in Boston. It said they could be seized with no fair trial. The warning pointed to the Fugitive Slave Act. The Crafts made a home in London. Five children were born to them. Their talks against slavery took them around England. In 1860, William put their escape into a book. William and Ellen came back to the United States in 1870. By that year, the law forbade slavery. Georgia became home again. They opened two schools. The students were people who had lived in slavery. Fire destroyed both schools. They opened one more school. White people made them shut that school. In 1890, they moved into a daughter's home. That home was in South Carolina. They stayed there until death. They gained the thing they had wanted most. They brought up a family. Slavery never split that family apart. They spent the rest of life side by side.

2. A Holiday for Ireland's Saint

1Chicago, Illinois, marks Saint Patrick's Day in a bright way. People dye the Chicago River green. In Bucharest, Romania, people dance in a traditional Irish style. March 17 brings people together in many parts of the world. The day is Saint Patrick's Day. The holiday honors the people of Ireland. It also honors Ireland's culture. Towns and cities hold parades. The parades have music. The parades have dancing. Many people wear green. Many people eat Irish foods.

2At the start, the day was for Saint Patrick. He is the patron saint of Ireland. Saint Patrick was not always a saint. He was not always called Patrick. He was not even Irish. His name was Maewyn Succat. A Catholic family was the family of his birth. His birthplace was Britain. The year was about 385 or 390. At age sixteen, Irish invaders captured him. They took him to Ireland.

3For six years he had to care for sheep there. Those years were hard. He found comfort in his religion during that time. He later wrote about a dream. Escape by ship was the order in that dream. He did escape. Then he went back to his family in Britain. His writings tell of another dream, this one in Britain. That dream told him to go back to Ireland.

4The goal was to help the Irish people. Maewyn then spent many years in training. He became a priest. After that, he became a bishop. He received the Latin name Patricius. Patrick is another form of that name. At last he went back to Ireland. He set up schools. He set up churches. He helped people who were in need. He became well known. Because of Patrick, many people in Ireland became Catholic.

5Many tales grew from Patrick's years in Ireland. One tale says he chased every snake off the island. That tale cannot be true. No snakes lived in Ireland during those years. Patrick died on March 17. The year was about 460. Many years later, the Catholic Church gave him a title. He became the patron saint of Ireland. The day Patrick died began as a feast day. That feast day was in Ireland.

6Across hundreds of years, large numbers of Irish people settled in other lands. They carried their customs with them. Saint Patrick's Day was one of those customs. Corned beef with cabbage is a traditional Irish food for the day. Soda bread is another food for the day. Irish stew is another. In Toronto, Canada, some people dance to mark the day. Towns and cities around the world hold celebrations. Parades are part of the day.

7Irish food is part of the day. Green clothes are part of the day too. Green appears on Ireland's flag. Some people believe green clothing brings good luck. Some people in England wear the same colors that are on the Irish flag. The Empire State Building stands in New York City. It glows green on Saint Patrick's Day. The Sydney Opera House stands in Australia. It glows green that day too.

8Ireland uses the shamrock as its symbol. Many people believe the shamrock is a kind of clover. A plant other than clover is what some people suspect it is. People often decorate for the day with a shamrock. Japanese students march in a parade in Tokyo, Japan. The parade is for Saint Patrick's Day. A real man stands behind this saint's name. He matters to the history of Ireland.

9Passing years have changed the holiday that carries his name. People in many countries now join the celebration. On this day, anyone can be a little Irish. In some Christian churches, a bishop ranks as a high official. A bishop is responsible for a set area. A bishop may also be responsible for a group of churches. Catholic means linked to the Roman Catholic Church. That church is a branch of Christianity.

10Culture means the ideas of a certain group of people. It also means that group's customs. Customs are traditional ways of doing something. They belong to a culture. Ireland is an island in northwestern Europe. The Republic of Ireland is on that island. Northern Ireland is on that island too. A patron saint is a holy person. People see that saint as a special protector of a person or a group.

3. A Lasting Rule Book for a Nation

1This book asks what the United States Constitution is. It tells how people created that set of rules. It looks at what the rules contain. It shows how the rules can change. It explains why the guide has lasted. A list of hard words comes at the end. Visitors in Washington, D.C., stop to look at the Constitution. The United States Constitution is a major set of rules for the country. Those rules lay out the way the government works. They also help guard the rights of every American. British troops stopped fighting at Yorktown in 1781. That win set the United States on a path away from British rule. In 1776, thirteen American colonies felt Great Britain had treated them unfairly. United States of America became the name those colonies took.

2They fought a war against Great Britain in order to be free. By 1783, the war had ended with a win for the new country. After that win, the states needed to build a new government. They did not want a king to rule them again. George Washington led the meetings that produced the Constitution. He led those meetings before he became president. The year 1787 brought a meeting of important people from every state. Talk at that meeting was about the new government. One task was choosing the parts of that government. They also had to decide how it should work. They shared ideas through the whole summer. Then they wrote the Constitution. The states approved the Constitution. After that approval, it became the law.

3Voters then chose George Washington. He became the nation's first president. Someone asked Benjamin Franklin a question after the Constitution was written. The question was what kind of government the United States would have. He said it would be a republic, if the people could keep it. Democracy is the system the United States uses. It is not a direct democracy. In a direct democracy, the people vote on the laws themselves. A republic is the form that system takes. In a republic, people vote for leaders who stand for them. Those leaders then cast votes so laws can pass. A chart can compare the two paths. In one path, people vote and the result is a law. In the other path, people vote for lawmakers.

4The lawmakers then vote. Three separate branches of government came out of the Constitution. The legislative branch makes the laws. The executive branch carries the laws out. The judicial branch decides how the laws can be used. It also decides how the laws should be used. The Supreme Court belongs to the judicial branch. Other federal courts belong there too. The president belongs to the executive branch. The vice president belongs there too. The cabinet belongs there as well. The Senate belongs to the legislative branch. The House of Representatives belongs there too. The Constitution has seven parts. Those parts are called articles. The first three articles tell how the United States government should work. Under that plan, the government has three branches. People also call the branches parts.

5One part is the executive branch. Another part is the legislative branch. The last part is the judicial branch. The Constitution places the president at the head of the executive branch. The president is also the top leader of the nation. Command of the armed forces sits with the president too. Only a person born in the United States may run for president. That person must be at least thirty-five years old. George Washington served as the first president. Thomas Jefferson served as the third president. Early presidents helped form ideas about how a president should act. They also helped form ideas about how much power a president should hold. Leaders of the United States gather inside the House of Representatives. People also call the legislative branch Congress.

6Congress is the body that makes the nation's laws. Congress has two parts. One part is the Senate. The other part is the House of Representatives. A state where more people live gets more representatives. Every state gets two senators. The number of senators does not change with the size of the state. Congress holds many powers. Raising taxes is one of those powers. Sending the country to war is another power. Nine justices serve on the Supreme Court of this country. A justice is a judge. The judicial branch watches over the courts of the United States. No court in the country stands above the Supreme Court. In cases before the courts, this court has the last say. It also decides if a new law follows the Constitution.

7Power is divided among the three branches. That division keeps the government from growing too strong. Power is also divided between the central government and the states. A change to the Constitution can be suggested in two ways. Two-thirds of the states can suggest a change at a special meeting. Two-thirds of the members of the House and the Senate can suggest a change. A change can be approved in two ways as well. Three-fourths of the state lawmaking groups can approve a change. Three-fourths of the states can approve a change at a special meeting. Many people were unhappy when the Constitution first became law. They believed it fell short on people's freedoms. They also believed it fell short on people's rights.

8When they need a change, Americans may change the Constitution. Each of those changes is called an amendment. In 1963, thousands of Americans gathered in a peaceful crowd. Martin Luther King Jr. was the speaker they gathered to hear. The first ten amendments passed soon after the Constitution was written. People know those ten amendments as the Bill of Rights. The First Amendment lets people share news with others. It also lets people share ideas with others. It lets people meet in groups that stay peaceful. People may practice any religion. People may also practice no religion. The law guards both choices. Two amendments deal with the courts. These amendments guard certain rights. Those rights belong to people who may have broken the law.

9Another amendment limits searches of homes. Police need a good reason before they search a home. More amendments were added as the years passed. One amendment brought slavery to an end. Further amendments gave African Americans the right to vote. Further amendments gave women the right to vote as well. The year 1915 shows women marching through the streets of New York. They marched so they could gain the right to vote. Women gained that right in 1920. In that year, the country approved the Nineteenth Amendment. Some people carry copies of the Constitution that fit in a pocket. Not every American agrees with all of the Constitution. The country has still been well served by it. The Constitution is not perfect. More than two hundred years have passed.

10The Constitution is still in use. People hope it will keep serving the United States for many years. A constitution is the written set of rules and ideas behind the laws of the United States. A court is a place where a case of law is heard. Elected means chosen by vote for a place in government. To protect is to keep a person or thing safe from harm or danger. Representatives are people picked to speak, vote, or act for someone. They may act for one person or for a group. The Senate is one of the two lawmaking houses of Congress. The Senate holds two representatives for each state. A body like the Senate can also exist at the state level.

4. A Park Where Lava Builds Land

1This book calls the park a volcano wonderland. It tells how volcanoes formed the park. It tells an amazing history. It tells about wildlife at the edge of the volcanoes. It tells visitors to enjoy the park and watch their step. A word list comes at the end. A map names two volcanoes, Mauna Loa and Kilauea. The map shows the park border and the island of Hawaii. The map shows the Atlantic Ocean. The map also shows the Pacific Ocean. A note says two famous volcanoes are in the park. Every day, lava seeps from a volcano on the island of Hawaii. That volcano sits on the edge of the ocean.

2Cool lava hardens and becomes rock. The new rock makes the island a little bigger. Hawaii Volcanoes National Park is the home of this volcano. Every year, the park has over one million visitors. A national park is land that is kept protected. A volcano has a beginning deep inside Earth. Melted rock comes out through a crack. The crack is in Earth's surface. When the melted rock reaches the surface, it cools. Then it becomes solid rock. As time passes, the rock piles higher. One possible shape of the piled rock is a mountain. The rock may even form an island.

3This is how the islands of Hawaii formed. Hawaii Volcanoes National Park holds two volcanoes. They are two of the most amazing volcanoes in the world. A drawing of the inside of a volcano shows lava. It shows the volcano itself. It shows melted rock, which is called magma. Earth's most active volcanoes include one volcano inside the park. Scientists think it began forming a very long time ago. Formation began in a span from 300,000 years ago to 600,000 years ago. Island form came only at a much later time. That later time was 50,000 to 100,000 years ago.

4Lava leaves one park volcano in a flowing river. A group of people poses for a photo. The year 1924 is when the photo was taken at a park volcano. In 1790, an eruption killed dozens of people. Then, in 1924, more eruptions came. Those eruptions threw huge rocks around the area. In 1959, one volcano sent lava high into the air. The stream of lava rose 1,900 feet. That height is 579 meters. Twice the Eiffel Tower's height is about how high the stream went. Another volcano stands nearby. Earth's biggest mountains include this one. Half of Hawaii island is covered by it.

5Eruptions from this volcano have happened again and again. Those eruptions have come since 1843. Far away, a snowcapped volcano can be seen. It is one of the largest volcanoes on Earth. An eruption appears in a picture made in the 1870s. The eruption is one of the park's volcanoes. Earthquakes followed an eruption in the year 1868. The earthquakes lasted four days. They destroyed nearly forty buildings. One volcano has a crater at the top. That crater holds a lava lake that is active. Hundreds of years of visits have been made to the volcanoes. That long story is an amazing history.

6The 1870s picture lets us see lava leaving the volcano. The earthquakes of 1868 shook the land for those four days. Nearly forty buildings did not survive the shaking. The lava lake at the top is still a sign of an active volcano. Visits by people go back hundreds of years. People wanted to protect the volcanoes. They also wanted to protect the land around the volcanoes. In 1916, the area received the name of national park. President Woodrow Wilson made the naming official. This area became national park number thirteen for the country. The date was August 1, 1916. Hawaii Volcanoes National Park covers 520 square miles.

7That area is 1,347 square kilometers. The park has volcanoes. It also has lava tubes that flow to the sea. Big ground tunnels are what lava tubes are. Their large size lets people walk inside them. The Thurston Lava Tube is open for visitors to walk through. That tube is in the park. The park's size of 520 square miles holds both rock and tunnels. Walking through a lava tube shows how big the tunnels are. The park is home to many living things. One is the caterpillar of the green grappler moth. One is the bird called the nene.

8One is the Hawaiian happy-face spider. One is the bird called the apapane. Protected animals in the hundreds live inside Hawaii Volcanoes National Park. Turtles live here. It is home to bats. It is home to insects. It is home to many types of birds. Many plants under protection are also in the park. It has many protected trees. Only Hawaii has almost every one of these plants. For years, many plants have grown in the volcanoes and beside them. One plant is the mamane. One plant is the ohia lehua. One plant is the tree fern. One plant is the silversword.

9These animals and plants live at the volcanoes' edge. The nene, the apapane, and the happy-face spider share that edge. The mamane, the ohia lehua, the tree fern, and the silversword grow there too. The changing land can be watched by visitors with their own eyes. They see that change at Hawaii Volcanoes National Park. The park is a wonderful place to visit. You can drive along a volcano. You can hike more than 150 miles of trails. That distance is 241 kilometers. You can bike along the volcano's paths. You can camp in the rainforest. Knowing the moment of an eruption is almost impossible.

10However, parts of the park close at certain times. They close when eruptions are possible. Long quiet does not happen in this special park. Active means in a state of activity. Erupted means shot out rock, lava, and ash. That happens from an active volcano. Lava is melted, liquid rock. An opening in the surface of Earth is where it comes out. A national park is an area of land. The government protects it for its history and its natural beauty. Protected means guarded from danger. Lots of rain falls on this kind of forest. It has many plants and animals.

5. A Penny Tells Its Story

1Hi. I will introduce myself. I am a coin of the United States. Friends call me Penny. Pfennig, a word from German, gave me my name. You say that word FEN-nig. The word means coin. Ten groups of ten pennies add up to 1 dollar. Count twenty-five pennies four times, and you also get 1 dollar. A pocket can hide coins that match my look. Each one is worth one cent. A cent is 1/100 of a dollar. You need 100 of us, me and my friends, to make one dollar.

2My story starts at the birth of the United States. In 1792, the U.S. government created the U.S. Mint. A mint is the factory that makes coins. I was one of the first coins that government made. I am the one-cent coin. In 1787, my friend Ben Franklin had an idea for my face. Across the years, my look changed more than once. For a time, I showed Lady Liberty.

3She wore a headdress of Native American style. That picture sat on my obverse. Obverse means my face. The other side is my reverse. Most people say heads and tails. In 1909, I received an important new look. America chose to honor President Abraham Lincoln. His picture went on my face. My reverse showed wheat. You could see the stalks, which are the stems. Some people called me the wheat penny. Check the pennies in your pocket.

4An old wheat penny might be there. In 1959, the Lincoln Memorial went on my back. People build a memorial, either a building or a statue, so a person will be remembered. Look very closely at my reverse. Inside the memorial, Lincoln appears again as a teeny-tiny statue. Workers make every penny at the U.S. Mint. The mint begins by asking an artist to draw. One drawing is for the front. One drawing is for the back. After the pictures look perfect, carving comes next.

5The carving is a sculpture called a relief. A machine copies a model of that relief. The machine cuts the picture into a hard stamp made of steel. In 2009, the U.S. Mint marked a birthday. It was 100 years since the Lincoln penny began. People first used the Lincoln penny in 1909. The year 2009 was also 200 years after Abraham Lincoln was born. Every coin begins as a blank that has been cut.

6A blank is a flat piece of metal in the shape of a coin. A blank is plain. It has no pictures. It has no words. Long ago, a penny blank was 100 percent copper. Today the blank is copper mixed with zinc. Workers heat the cut blanks until the metal is soft. The blanks then pass through a washer. They pass through a dryer next. Next, a machine raises a rim on the blank. Each blank gets that rim along its outer edge.

7Look closely at a penny in your hand. A raised edge circles it. Next, each blank is struck. The hard stamp carries the artist's relief. The stamp slams down on the blank. The hit presses pictures into the metal. It presses words into the metal too. A worker then checks the pennies with a magnifying glass. Finished pennies are counted. They go into bags. The bags go to banks across the United States.

8The U.S. Mint keeps a web page for children. The page can teach you more about money. One mint is in Denver. The other mint is in Philadelphia. Together they make every coin of the United States. That includes me. Each year they make billions of pennies. Can you guess the cost of making one penny? The cost is 1.4 cents. Making me costs more than I am worth. That is my story. I have been here a long time.

9Some people think I do not matter. Gather a bunch of us, though, and we can do a lot. Finding me is good luck. I hope you find many of my friends. Save a few of us for a rainy day. Here are word meanings from the book. A blank is a flat, round piece of metal with nothing on it yet. It becomes a coin. A cent is a coin worth one penny. It equals 1/100 of a dollar.

10A headdress is special clothing worn on the head. People wear one at a festival or on another special day. Money is made in a factory called a mint. The obverse is the heads side, or the face, of a coin. A relief is a shallow carving on a flat surface. The reverse is the tails side, or the back, of a coin. Struck means a stamp has pressed pictures or words into the metal.

6. A Shelter Animal Makes a Great Pet

1Dogs sit in a shelter and hope a person will choose them. Shelter animals are the subject of these pages. A fresh start for those animals comes first. Many kinds of animals you might pick come next. How shelters guard a pet's health comes after that. Advice for families is another part. Services a shelter can give follow the advice. Saving a life is one more big idea. A list of useful words closes the pages. Buzz the dog has a gentle manner. Four is his age. Scratches on his ears please him. A family home is something Buzz still lacks. A cage is his spot for now. That cage stands inside an animal shelter. Animals stay in a shelter only for a time.

2Buzz is hoping a family will take him in. Shyness describes the cat named Michelangelo. Two is his age. His eyes look pretty. Blue is their color. Care grew too hard for the person who owned him. This cat now lives where Buzz lives. Buzz and Michelangelo are two animals in a giant count. Shelters across the United States hold more than six million animals. Pets reach those shelters when good homes run short. Choosing at a shelter is the step for a family that wants a pet. One small building is packed tight with cats. A stray dog digs through street trash in search of food. If you are hunting for a pet, begin at a shelter. That is the top spot to look.

3Animals inside need homes that will treat them well. Being lost is why some of them are there. Never having lived in a home is true for others. Some owners did a poor job of training. Those pets may be given to a shelter. Other owners can no longer manage the care. Adoption gives a shelter pet another try at life. That new life can be a happy one. Health can be part of the new life too. Many cats live at shelters. Many dogs live there too. Other animals are often ready for a new home. Rabbits may be among the choices. Birds may be among the choices. Guinea pigs may be waiting. Ferrets may be waiting. Turtles may be waiting.

4Pictures show turtles that shelters keep. Pictures also show ferrets. A healthy pet is a likely find at a shelter. Mixed breeds are most of the animals there. A mixed breed comes from parents of unlike types. Breeders often raise animals that are purebred. Selling those animals is their aim. Studies find a health gap between the groups. The mixed-breed group is, in general, the healthier one. A veterinarian examines each animal at the shelter. Vet is the short name people use. Shots are part of what the vet gives. The shots support ongoing health. A photo shows a rabbit during an exam. The vet is looking into its ear. A counselor speaks with a family that just chose a puppy.

5People in that job guide visitors at a shelter. Their help is one reason a shelter is a strong place to seek a pet. Matching a home with a fitting animal is the work. Time is a question they raise. Can the family share enough hours with a pet? A yard is another question. A fence lets a dog run and play. Does the home have a yard like that? Answers of this sort steer a family. The family can then choose well. A shop that sells pets may be a weak place to search. Staff there cannot always help you match a pet to your life. Lifestyle is the word for that way of living. Spare time may be something the worker lacks.

6The worker may also miss the questions that matter. A fee is paid to the shelter once the pet is picked. Services are what that money supports. Health of the pet is one goal of the services. Happiness of the pet is another goal. A vet visit is included. Obedience training is included too. An operation may be part of the fee. Babies are what that operation prevents. A microchip may go under the skin. A lost pet can be tied back to its owner by that chip. Someone searching can then locate the owner. Extra reasons to adopt from a shelter come from these services. During obedience training, an instructor works with dogs. Owners join that practice. Puppy mills supply some of the young dogs sold in shops.

7Breeders sometimes sell puppies from those mills too. A puppy mill is a business built on breeding. Good care is not what that business gives its animals. High prices are common at a pet shop. High prices are common with a breeder too. Thousands of dollars is sometimes the cost. Workers once helped pull dogs out of a place suspected to be a puppy mill. About three hundred dogs were in that rescue. A lone dog waits, hoping adoption will come. The strongest reason to choose a shelter comes last. Lives of animals are saved by that choice. Space at a shelter is limited. Money at a shelter is limited. Care of the animals depends on that money.

8A crowded shelter cannot hold every animal. A shot is then given to many of them. Life ends from that shot, without pain. Health is still true for many of those pets. A tame nature is still true for many of them. Wider adoption from shelters would shrink this loss. Fewer animals would be put to sleep. Friendship is something a shelter animal can offer. Health is likely in a pet you adopt there. Happiness is likely too. A counselor at the shelter can guide the match. A wonderful best friend can be the result. One homeless animal is saved by one adoption. Millions of animals are still without homes. Shelter pets stand as the best choice.

9Homes in America hold about seventy-eight million dogs. Homes in America hold about eighty-five million cats. In a single year, cats and dogs enter shelters in a combined count above six million. About four million of those animals are adopted. A count above nine hundred thousand is put to sleep. Fewer homeless pets is a goal worth working toward. Stopping births is an important part of that goal. A vet can perform an operation. The operation is simple. The operation is safe. Babies will not follow that operation. Dislike of the operation is a feeling you may have. Better health is a likely result afterward. A friendlier pet is a likely result too. Have the pet fixed. People who raise animals for sale are breeders.

10People who give advice are counselors. Lifestyle means a usual way of living. A person can have one. A group can have one. A culture can have one. A microchip is a very small electronic piece. Information can be stored on it. Information can be handled by it. Obedience training is a behavior program. Dogs take part. Their owners take part too. Purebred means both parents are one type. Breed is the name for that type. A shelter is a structure that offers cover. Danger is one thing it blocks. Bad weather is another. A veterinarian treats animals, not humans. Illness in those animals is part of the work. Injury in those animals is part of the work too.

7. Animals at the Extreme

1Many unusual animals seem to have a story of their own. They seem to go to an extreme. People notice them because of that. Each one is different from other kinds of animals. The difference might be an ability. It might be a special talent. Speed can make an animal stand out. Size can do it. Coloration can do it too. One animal reaches as high as a house with two floors. Another is mouse-sized. One might jump higher than the rest. One might hide inside a tiny space. One might see a longer way. One might scream with more force. This book shares odd facts about these animals. It also shares facts that are not well known. Some of the facts are just for fun. You will see what makes each animal different and special. Read on and you will meet some of these extreme animals. Biggest comes first. A giant blue whale stretches about half the length of a football field.

2No larger mammal has ever lived on Earth. That is true today. It was true in the past too. The body is so heavy that only water can hold it up. If the whale lived on land, its own weight would crush it. At birth, a baby elephant may reach 136 kilograms. That is 300 pounds. The baby keeps growing for thirty years. A typical adult elephant matches the weight of two pickup trucks. A few giant elephants reached double that weight. A grown male can stand more than fifteen feet tall. A full-grown giraffe reaches the height of a house with two floors. A long, strong neck helps make it the tallest animal on land. Long legs help too. A newborn giraffe already stands 1.8 meters tall. That height is 6 feet. Smallest comes next. A small Chihuahua seems to be mostly ears.

3This friendly dog is tiny enough to ride in a pocket. It can travel with you any place you go. Its temperament is sweet. It is also very brave. It will bark at a dog many times its own size. A bee hummingbird is so tiny that it weighs less than one teaspoon of water. Measure it from the beak tip to the tail tip. The length is under 5 centimeters. That is under 2 inches. A tarsier is a big-eyed monkey. Its body may be no bigger than a mouse. It lives in rainforests that are the hottest ones. During the warmest hours, it sleeps in a tree. At night, the tiny tarsier hunts for food. Its large eyes give excellent vision in the dark. Loudest comes next. The cry of a blue whale is also called its song. The sound matches the noise of a rocket at launch.

4The whale may sing for ten hours. Far across the ocean, others can still hear it. A howler monkey gets its name from the sound it makes. It gives a piercing howl when the day begins. It howls again when the day ends. The howl says which land belongs to the group. It also points out the best places to find food. Other monkeys can hear that howl from as far as 8 kilometers. That distance is 5 miles. Quietest comes next. Few animals stay as silent as a tall, long-legged giraffe. Shyness is not the reason. The giraffe almost never uses its voice. It sends messages with body language instead. Longest life comes next. A big tortoise lives on the Galapagos Islands. It moves slowly. It has time to cross its island home from end to end. It can live 150 years. It can even live 200 years.

5Shortest life comes next. A mayfly hatches and becomes an adult. Then it usually lives only one day. That short life is busy. One task is to find a mate. The insect does not stop to eat. It does not stop to drink. Slowest comes next. A snail will not rush, no matter the reason. A garden snail may creep across your shoe. You could nap while you wait. You could read a story while you wait. Fastest comes next. An adult ostrich can match the speed of a racehorse. The ostrich is tall and has long legs. Its walk can look awkward. No land animal on two feet runs faster. When a cheetah chases a meal, an adult can pass 105 kilometers in an hour. That speed is 65 miles in an hour. Cars on a highway travel at about that pace. A peregrine falcon can dive at the speed of a flying plane.

6It swoops toward its prey at more than 322 kilometers in an hour. That is more than 200 miles in an hour. Here are fun facts about reptiles. A snake can see very sharply. It never closes its eyes. The eyes stay open during sleep. A snake has transparent eyelids. Those lids slide down over the eyes. They keep dust off the open eyes. If a lizard loses its tail, a new tail grows in that place. Dropping the tail helps the lizard get away. It escapes an animal that is trying to catch it. At first glance, a tuatara could be mistaken for a little lizard. A third eye rests high on its head. That extra eye does not see. It works as a sensor. The sensor measures sunlight. It also measures heat. Here are fun facts about water animals. Do not lick this tongue. The tongue of a large humpback whale matches the weight of a small car.

7One kind of lungfish can last four years with no food. It can last four years with no water if it must. A crocodile can stay underwater for an hour without a breath. It has to remain very still. If it waits too long for the next breath, it can drown. The mudskipper is a fish with an athletic body. It can climb a tree. It is still a fish. On land, though, it moves faster than it swims. The halibut is flat and shaped like a diamond. It is the largest flatfish. To our eyes, it looks strange. Both eyes sit on one side of its head. Here are fun facts about mammals. Each zebra wears its own stripe pattern. Because of that, every zebra is a little different. To people, most zebras look the same. Other zebras can tell them apart.

8A kangaroo can lean backward. It balances on its big tail. At the same moment, all four feet can punch and kick. A hippopotamus is large. It looks awkward. A person would lose a race against it. On a short run, its speed is 48 to 64 kilometers each hour. That speed is 30 to 40 miles in an hour. A camel can shut its nose. The shut nose blocks blowing sand in the desert. A twitch of the nose can cool the air the camel breathes. Here are fun facts about insects. A butterfly stands on the food it will eat. Tiny taste buds in its feet work as sensors. The sensors tell whether that spot is good to eat. A cricket hears with ears on its knees. It turns those knees toward a chirp. That is how it hears another cricket. An ant notices smells as well as a dog does.

9Ants share news by giving off special smells. One smell shows where food is. Another smell tells the group to flee danger. Here are fun facts about birds. An eagle grows very large. Even so, the bird weighs very little. Its bones are hollow. The hollow bones let an eagle reach high altitudes without getting tired. The bones are so light that the feathers weigh more than they do. A tiny hummingbird is fast. It is also mobile, so it can shift direction quickly. This bird can fly to the side, the way a helicopter can. The bird can fly up. The bird can fly down. It can even fly backward. An ostrich eye is larger than the ostrich's brain. The big eyes help it spot objects that are far off. Its tall body helps with that too. A picture can set an ostrich eye next to an ostrich brain.

10A penguin is a bird. Swimming is easy for it. Walking is easy too. It cannot fly. The bones inside its wings are heavy. Those bones are solid. Solid wing bones help it steer while it swims. The same bones are too heavy for flight. A flamingo looks bright pink because of pigment in its food. Pigment is color. Long legs let it wade far into the water. Out there, it searches for food. If it ate green leaves, would it turn green? These extreme animals stand out like famous stars among other animals. People notice them wherever they go. You may already know some of them. You may never have seen the others. You may never have heard of some of them. This book shows that extreme animals come in many sizes. They come in many shapes too. Which other animals seem extreme to you?

8. Animals Just Found

1A world map marks where new animals turned up. The map shows Europe. The map shows Asia. The map shows Africa. The map shows North America. The map shows South America. It shows Australia. The map shows the Atlantic Ocean. The map shows the Pacific Ocean. The map shows the Indian Ocean. The lesula monkey was found in the Democratic Republic of Congo. The Caquetá titi monkey was found in Colombia. The Matang narrow-mouthed frog was found on Borneo. The long-nosed tree frog was found in New Guinea. The blossom bat was found in New Guinea too. Goodman's mouse lemur was found in Madagascar. Sazima's tarantula was found in Brazil. The troll-haired planthopper was found in Brazil's Amazon Rainforest. The Gorgon's head starfish was found in the Atlantic Ocean. The walking bamboo shark was found in Indonesia. The olinguito was found in Ecuador.

2The olinguito was also found in Colombia. The Cambodian tailorbird was found in Cambodia. Animals have scientific names. One jellyfish is called Tamoya ohboya. A contest was used to pick that name. The winner expected most people to shout oh boy when they saw this jellyfish. A hunt for a new animal species could start far away. Remote corners of the globe are good places to look. A single year can bring more than 15,000 new animal species. That number is 1 percent of the species already known. The known list already holds more than 1.5 million species. Around the globe, species are being lost at a record pace. For that reason, a new species is an important discovery. In 2007, scientists found a new monkey in the forests of Central Africa.

3The lesula has large eyes. It is shy. It is quiet. Scientists first met this monkey as someone's pet. In 2010, calls led scientists to the Caquetá titi monkey. Its calls gave it away. The Amazon jungle is home for this monkey. A bushy red beard is its best-known feature. Other titi monkeys have a white bar on the forehead. The Caquetá does not. Its babies purr like cats. The Caquetá titi monkeys still living today number only around 250. That small number means the species is endangered. Pitcher plants are where this Matang narrow-mouthed frog puts its eggs. Those plants hold a liquid. The tadpoles grow in that liquid. A pea matches this frog in size. The 2010 discovery happened on Borneo, which is an island. These frogs were not new to the eyes of scientists. They believed these frogs were youngsters from some other species.

4Later, the calling of the frogs reached them. Only adult frogs make calls. The tiny frogs had to be full-grown. Spike-nosed tree frog is another name for this long-nosed frog. A scientist discovered it in 2010. New Guinea's Foja Mountains are where it lives. People have called that area the Lost World. People know this tree frog by its long nose. Some people call it the Pinocchio frog. A call from the male sends his nose upward. The nose drops again once the call is done. No one knows why the nose moves. The blossom bat drinks nectar from flowers. That feeding also pollinates the flower. The flower can then make seeds. Those seeds can grow new plants. The blossom bat was another find on the 2010 Foja Mountains trip.

5A long tongue lets it drink nectar from flowers. Researchers came upon Goodman's mouse lemurs during a Madagascar trip in 2005. These lemurs are not much bigger than mice. At night they jump around in the trees. Empty nests that birds have left are sometimes their beds by day. Lemurs live only in Madagascar. Sazima's tarantulas live only in the tabletop mountains of Brazil. Those mountains have a climate unlike the land around them. Scientists think animals with backbones are a small share of life. Those animals are called vertebrates. Only 3 percent of all species are vertebrates. The other 97 percent have no backbone. Animals without backbones are called invertebrates. A special beauty belongs to Sazima's tarantula. The body is dark blue. It looks nearly ready to glow. This arachnid has a limited habitat. High mountains in Brazil are the only place it lives.

6Planthoppers pass through stages as they grow. Most insects grow that way too. This young planthopper is just starting to grow wings. A new kind of planthopper was discovered in 2013. The find was in South America. The animal looks strange. Its wild hair is not hair at all. Scientists think the hair breaks off when an enemy attacks. The planthopper can then jump to safety. That is what they think, but they are not sure. The Gorgon's head starfish takes its name from the Gorgons. The Gorgons come from Greek mythology. Hundreds of snakes covered the heads of those creatures. The snakes took the place of hair. Scientists found this starfish in 2010. The find was in the Atlantic Ocean. The Gorgon's head is a type of basket star.

7Five arms split off from its body. The tips on a set of five arms can reach five thousand. The tips help it find food that floats past. Walking along the ocean bottom is another use for the arms. It uses its arms to protect itself. The walking bamboo shark does not match other bamboo sharks in pattern. Its stripes are different. Its spots are different. No one found this shark until 2013. Humans are safe around it, as they are around most sharks. The sea by the coast of Indonesia is where it lives. The colors on its back did not match other sharks. That difference made scientists notice it. Other sharks do not have those back colors. It uses its fins to push along the ocean floor. It pushes along while it searches for food.

8The wiggle makes the shark look as if it is walking. Olinguitos have smaller faces than olingos. Their faces are rounder too. Their tails are shorter than olingo tails. The name sounds like oh-lin-GEE-toh. The wrong idea lasted among scientists for more than a hundred years. They thought the olinguito was another species. In 2013, they learned they had been wrong. Night is when this olinguito leaps from tree to tree. No relative in the raccoon family is smaller than it is. South America is where people can come across it. People may meet an unknown species for years. They mistake it for a species they already know. This often happens because the two look the same on the outside. Scientists call these cryptic species. The species look distinct only when the genetic code is studied.

9DNA technology is being used more and more. Reports of separate new species are rising for that reason. Finding a Cambodian tailorbird is not easy. It lives in dense brush. Researchers found it in 2009. The tests placed this tailorbird in a species of its own. The bird's song was studied as well. All tailorbirds sing. No two species of tailorbird sound the same. The Cambodian tailorbird's song is different from all the rest. From 2000 to 2009, finds were counted by type. Scientists discovered 88,598 insects in those years. They discovered 23,604 plants. They discovered 12,751 arachnids. They discovered 11,984 fungi. They discovered 7,070 crustaceans. They discovered 5,949 mollusks. They discovered 4,417 bacteria. They discovered 3,587 fish. They discovered 18,351 living things of other kinds. Scientists keep finding new species. Each find can still amaze people. Experts agree that many more species are still hidden.

10Dying out is a real risk for many species that were just found. Once they are found, people can work to save the places they live. Saving those homes can save animals we already know. It can also save animals we do not know yet. An arachnid is a living thing with two body segments. An arachnid has eight legs. Spiders are arachnids. Scorpions are arachnids. Endangered means in danger of dying out completely. Habitat means the natural home of a plant. It can also mean the natural home of an animal. Remote means distant. Remote can also mean isolated. Scientists are people who study science. A scientist may study more than one field. Physically similar living things can belong to one species. Members of a species can reproduce.

9. Animals That Stay Together

1Animal groups have playful names. Caterpillars in a group are called an army. Crocodiles in a group are called a bask. Emus in a group are called a mob. Rhinos in a group are called a crash. A group of giraffes is called a tower. People call a group of crows a murder. Whales gather in pods. Bees gather in swarms. Fish gather in schools. Each special name points to one important truth. Many animals spend life in a group. Some animals do well with no group at all. A tiger can live alone. A mole can live alone. A sloth can live alone. Why do some animals join up? How does each animal fit inside the group? Parents and their young make a small family. For some animals, that family is a mother raising babies. Skunks live this way. Cheetahs live this way. Orangutans live this way. Grizzly bears live this way. For other animals, both parents raise the young. Penguins do this. Parrots do this. Foxes do this. Finches do this. A pair of cranes may dance.

2Close bonds are common in a small family. A mother cheetah grooms her babies. The grooming is done with the tongue. Baby bears play with one another. They play with their parents too. Crane parents dance as a pair. Seabirds use calls. Fox families pounce, and they play. Fox kits grow close through that play. The bonds push family members to care for one another. The bonds also push them to protect one another. An albatross pair shares the work. Each bird takes a turn with the egg. Care of the young is the main job of a small family. Some families stay together only until the young can manage alone. A mother moose keeps her calf beside her. The pair stays close for about a year. The grown calf goes off on its own. After that, a moose lives a solitary life. Other families last much longer. A pair of albatrosses stays together for a whole life. An albatross is a large seabird. More than fifty years of life is possible for this bird.

3A bond that long is a big promise. Some animals gain nothing from life in a group. Bears are one example. Moose are another example. Certain wildcats are another example. A solitary animal lives alone. The exception is the time spent raising young. Alone, they avoid a contest with their own kind. The contest could be over a mate. The contest could be over territory. Helpless young are common in animals that form small families. Birds and mammals make up most of that group. Parents of birds must carry food to the babies. The feeding lasts until the young can fly. Young mammals drink milk from their mothers. A few animals outside those groups form small families too. A mother crocodile guards her babies. Hungry fish are one danger she watches for. Birds are another danger. A clown fish father cares for eggs in his own way. He fans fresh water across the eggs. A small family helps the young grow big. It helps them grow healthy. It helps them gain skills. The skills let them survive once they are on their own.

4A songbird can be shown with hatchlings. Some newborns can live without help from the start. Other newborns need a family. This young crocodile is one that needs care. A family of animals may be small. A family may be large. Some animals live in a band. A band is an extended family. Mothers and fathers are in the band. The young are in it. Grandparents, aunts, uncles, and cousins are in it. Other relatives are in it too. Some bands include animals that are not kin. Those animals are usually mates of someone in the family. On rare occasions, a band adopts an outsider with no group. Life in a band is the pattern for lions. Wolves live in bands. Whales live in bands. Dolphins live in bands. Monkeys live in bands. Chimps live in bands. Elephants live in bands. A band shares the work of raising young. Some adults watch the little ones. Other adults go out for food. Brothers and sisters play together. Cousins and other young animals play too.

5An elephant band is often females only. At times, male elephants join the family group. A lion group is called a pride. Males in a pride are often far fewer than females. A band also shares the search for food. Wolves are known for skilled teamwork on a hunt. Lions are known for that teamwork too. Dolphins are known for it too. The teamwork is sophisticated. Prey is what the hunt is for. An elephant matriarch leads the family. She is often the oldest female. She guides them to food in far-off places. She guides them to water. The rest follow in a single line. Many bands also guard a territory as a team. Band life has many moving parts. Often one animal, or two, acts as leader. In some groups the lead passes in a calm way. An elephant band passes it from a parent to the young. Chimps can be competitive. Monkeys can be competitive too. Mates can be the thing they compete for. Territory can be the thing they compete for.

6A follower may try to take the lead. Teeth may be flashed. The flash is a challenge. Followers may join with other followers. They may even fight the leader. Bands are home to some of Earth's smartest animals. Dolphins are part of that set. Chimps are part of it. Elephants are part of it. Some scientists offer a reason. High intelligence may help these animals. It may help them keep track of hard relationships in the group. A band works best when the members get along. More support gives a leader more power. Grooming makes the bonds stronger. Play makes them stronger too. Resting together does the same. Sound is one way they communicate. Scent is another way. Movement is another way. An elephant may rumble. An elephant may trumpet. Both sounds let it express itself. Wolves leave marks of scent. Wild dogs leave those marks too. A scent mark claims territory. It also helps the animals know one another. Bands and other large groups rely on behaviors of this kind.

7The behaviors help the group cooperate. Wolves in a pack lick one another. The lick is a way to communicate. The lick is also a way to bond. Caribou travel as one herd. Groups bigger than bands have also learned to work as a team. Picture the savanna in Africa. Animals that graze move over the land. They fill the view as far as sight can reach. A herd of mammals can be a huge group. A flock of birds can be a huge group. A school of fish can be a huge group. People generally call all of these groups herds. Big numbers help them stay safe from predators. A lone zebra is an easy target for lions. A crowd is much harder to attack. A crowd on the run is harder still. The lions struggle to single out one animal. Some herds and flocks are built from small families. Penguin colonies are one case. In a colony, thousands of parents raise chicks. A bigger group also means more watchers. One gazelle may notice a cheetah.

8It gives the alarm with a honking call. The rest of the herd then has a chance to flee. For these reasons, many animals raise young inside a herd. The big group helps keep the babies safe. Herd members often call to one another. An alarm call warns that a predator is near. Other calls help the group stay together. A herd does not build bonds. A family does build bonds. A band does build bonds. In a herd, self comes first for every animal. One member may watch for predators while the rest eat. A springbok can be that watcher. In a society, every member works for the group. The member does not work only for itself. It is already amazing that a herd can cooperate. A society is a different kind of group. Thousands of individuals may live in one society. Hundreds of thousands may live in one. A society is organized. A herd is not organized in that way. The whole group is the reason members labor side by side. Very few animals form a society.

9Nearly all that do are insects. Ants form societies. Termites form societies. Bees form societies. Some wasps form societies. One rare member of a society is not an insect. That animal is the naked mole rat. Queens live among naked mole rats. Workers live among them too. Some scientists also place humans in the class of animal societies. Humans differ from other societies in one way. A human can work with someone who is not a relative. A soldier ant has large jaws. It has a large body. The nest stays safer because of those features. By contrast, a worker ant's body is much smaller. Every member of a society has a job. Workers collect food. Nurses look after the young. Soldiers keep the group safe. Body shape is not the same for every worker insect. The body shape helps the job get done. One queen is usually the one who lays every egg. For that reason, the members are all related. An insect society can do astonishing things. Leaf-cutter ants cut leaves into pieces.

10Grass-cutter ants cut leaves too. They carry the pieces into the nest. The leaves fertilize farms of fungus. Bees make cells of wax. The cells store a food that is special to them. That food is honey. Ants lay trails of scent. A trail leads others to food. A trail also leads the food back to the nest. Termites raise mounds. A mound can stand over 5 meters high. That is 17 feet. Next to a termite, the mound is huge. The mounds are like giant skyscrapers. All of this is possible for one reason. Every member works with the others. Animal groups are not all the same. Each kind of group helps its members survive. Many sorts of animals live with others. Tiny termites do. Enormous elephants do. A group helps with raising young. A group helps with protecting young. A group helps members find food. A group can even build something together. The sizes of groups differ. The styles of groups differ. Survival is the help that every group gives.

10. Bhutan Measures What Makes People Glad

1This book starts with an introduction. Then it tells how growth and happiness fit together. Then it tells about four pillars of happiness. Then it tells how happiness is measured. Then it looks at happy people around the world. A word list comes at the end. What is happiness? People do not all give happiness the same meaning. Contentment is how people in Bhutan, said boo-TAHN, define happiness. Happy citizens are what the government of Bhutan hopes for. Money ranks lower than happiness in the government's thinking. That is why the government asks people questions every few years. The questions check how happy the people are. Life for all people can get better because of this information. The country is called the Kingdom of Happiness. Bhutan's place is in the Himalaya Mountains. The native language is Dzongkha, said ZOHNG-kah. Many people there also speak English. For centuries, bows and arrows were used to defend Bhutan's borders. Archery holds the title of national sport today. Bhutan has a king. Bhutan also has leaders who are elected.

2A map shows Bhutan near China. The map also shows Nepal, India, and Bangladesh. The map marks Thimphu. The map shows the great range of the Himalaya. It also shows the lesser range of the Himalaya. Bhutan's landscape changes from south to north. The south has low plains. The north has the towering Himalaya Mountains. Bhutan held its first happiness interviews in the year 2007. The plan to track happiness was much older than those interviews. Bhutan crowned a new king during 1972. King Wangchuck's age then was only sixteen. Love for his country filled the young king. He wanted the country to grow. He wanted the country to be successful. The Tiger's Nest Monastery holds tight to a cliff. That cliff rises thousands of feet over Bhutan's Paro Valley. King Wangchuck knew how other countries measure success. They measure success by money alone. Costs of goods that are made get added together. They also add up the cost of goods they sell.

3Bhutan, the king felt, should not follow that money-only way. Most citizens followed Buddhism. Happiness, in their belief, is an important step on the way to a good life. Only a small piece of that life was money. King Wangchuck's wish was a happy country. Only then would the country succeed. The plan for Bhutan came from him. The plan is called Gross National Happiness, or GNH. Four key ideas about happiness sat inside the plan. Those ideas would help Bhutan's growth. Bhutan's government believes happiness is based on four ideas. The ideas are called pillars. One pillar is government rule. One pillar is social and work matters. One pillar is culture and traditions. One pillar is nature and the environment. Before making new laws, the king and his government looked at all four pillars. The king's full name is King Jigme Singye Wangchuck. The measure for Gross National Happiness uses things Bhutanese people value. They value good governing. They value wise handling of money. They value growth of society.

4Keeping the Bhutanese way of life strongly supported is something they value. They value care of the natural world. In the beginning, the four pillars only worked as guides. There was no real survey. Bhutan was a quiet country. It was protected from the modern world. Its people led simple lives. They were mostly content. People dressed in Bhutanese clothing. Each day they sat in quiet with their own thoughts. They enjoyed nature. Families spent time together. After that, change started in a slow way. You can see giant prayer wheels everywhere in Bhutan. Gross National Happiness schools include morning meditation in the day. In 1999, Bhutan let television into the country for the first time. That same year, Bhutan let the Internet in for the first time. Citizens of Bhutan started learning about lands past their borders. People outside also started learning about Bhutan. Its natural beauty attracted people. People were drawn to its peaceful lifestyle. Many people came to visit. The past still held one foot of the quiet kingdom.

5It also had one foot in the modern world. Bhutanese children watched television. They played video games. People began using cell phones. Bhutan's leaders became worried. Their fear was that traditions might disappear. They were afraid the happiness of Bhutan's people would be lost too. Bhutan does not have any stoplights. A single stoplight was once installed in Thimphu, the capital. It was removed. Citizens judged a stoplight to be too modern. A traffic officer guides cars through the downtown streets. A group of young monks gathers around a cell phone. They are at a monastery in Paro, Bhutan. The government chose to find a method for tracking happiness. The four pillars were not exact enough by themselves. Planners created nine areas, called domains. The domains were based on the four pillars. Smaller categories were made from the domains. Every domain and every category went into the Gross National Happiness Index. The index let happiness be seen in a fuller way. Survey questions on Gross National Happiness were written by the government next.

6How people lived was the topic of the survey. Each answer had a number. The numbers were added up. The total was a happiness score for each person. The nine domains have these names. One domain is well-being. One domain is living standards. One domain is health. One domain is time use. One domain is environment. One domain is community vitality. One domain is education. One domain is cultural resilience, which means toughness. One domain is good governance. These domains come from the GNH Centre Bhutan. A test of the survey was run by the government in 2007. By 2010, the survey could be given in every part of Bhutan. The teams went out to villages in the mountains. They traveled to valley farms. They traveled to cities. More than seven thousand people gave information about their lives. Those people were part of a country of over seven hundred thousand. The GNH Index was used to study this information.

7The highest scores belonged to the happiest people. The survey found that women were among the least happy. Farmers were among the least happy. Uneducated people were among the least happy. Senior citizens were among the least happy. Improving health care was a step the government started. It began to improve daily life. One more survey came in 2015. The rise in citizen happiness was 1.8 percent. Prayer flags are often seen in every part of Bhutan. They stand for peace. They stand for balance. The survey left out one group living in Bhutan. That group is the Lhotshampa, said loh-TSUHM-puh. Their first arrival from Nepal was as farmers. They came in the nineteenth century. The Lhotshampa wore different clothes. They had different ways of living. They were not Buddhists. New rules for every citizen came from the Bhutanese government in the 1980s. Citizens had to wear Bhutanese clothing. They had to speak Dzongka. They had to practice Buddhism. Tens of thousands of Lhotshampa departed Bhutan during the 1990s.

8Many hope to return someday. One Lhotshampa woman is shown holding her child. Her job is on a road project in Thimphu, Bhutan. There were some areas where happiness dropped. People did less volunteer work. Traditions mattered to fewer people than before. Those weak areas will be improved by working with communities. They may hold festivals. The festivals would include national traditions. The aim for someday is a population at 100 percent happiness. Word of Bhutan's GNH Index reached other countries. Some countries track how happy their citizens are with an index of their own. In 2007, Thailand made a national happiness index in the same spirit as Bhutan. South Korea developed tools based on Bhutan's GNH. Great Britain developed tools based on Bhutan's GNH too. A big company ran a happiness survey during 2009. The survey was of people in the United States. A 2016 report lists the ten happiest countries. The first is Denmark. The second is Switzerland. The third is Iceland. The fourth is Norway.

9The fifth is Finland. The sixth is Canada. The seventh is the Netherlands. The eighth is New Zealand. The ninth is Australia. The tenth is Sweden. The list comes from the World Happiness Report 2016. The same ten countries have stayed at the top every year. Their order may have changed. The United States ranked 13th. Bhutan ranked 84th. An average score for the country is what each number shows. Citizens gave quality of life a rank from 0 up to 10. Personal happiness matters more than money inside Bhutan. In 2011, the United Nations did a study of world happiness. The United Nations is an organization of countries around the world. In 2012, the United Nations released the first World Happiness Report. A happiness score set each country's place in the report. A special day was named by the United Nations in that same year. March 20 became the International Day of Happiness. Why was March 20 chosen? On that date, daylight and night last the same length.

10This day stands for a belief of the Bhutanese king. He believed balance was the main key for a nation's happiness. Analyzed means examined closely. Balance means the parts are equal with one another. The parts are in the right proportion to one another. Balance can also mean harmony or equilibrium. Border lines separate one country or area from the next. Buddhists are followers of Buddhism. Buddhism rests on teachings from Siddhartha Gautama. Those teachings began in ancient India. Festivals are celebrations. A festival can also be an organized series of related events. An index is a number used as a measure of something. An index can also display a rise or a fall in a level. A survey is a way of collecting information about something. The method is questions asked of a group of people. Tracking means watching the progress of something. Tracking can also mean following. Traditions are beliefs or customs passed down from year to year. They are also passed from generation to generation.

11. Buyers, Sellers, and the Price

1In the COVID-19 pandemic, customers hunted for toilet paper. They also hunted for paper towels. Shelves in the stores were often bare. This book asks who decides a price. It shows supply and demand in action. It shows how a balance can form. It shows how feelings change supply and demand. It asks what part you play. A list of hard words comes at the end. Have you wanted to buy a thing and then walked away? Was the cost too high? Maybe the thing was new. It might have been a shirt. A character from your favorite movie might have been on it. You might have returned after the shirt went on sale. Did you buy the shirt that day? Probably you did not. A shirt from a newer movie would appeal more. Some scientists say a shopping trip can change the brain. The change can bring a feeling of excitement. The change can bring a feeling of happiness.

2Why does each thing cost what it costs? A choice to buy is an exchange with the person who sells. Any place where sellers and buyers meet to trade is a market. In a market, buyers hand money over for goods. A toy is one kind of good. Buyers also hand money over for services. A haircut is one kind of service. Prices of goods and services can stand far apart. The same sort of thing can sell for a low price. It can also sell for a high price. Sellers put up signs in large letters. They use bright colors on those signs. The signs grab attention. They pull customers into a store. They pull customers onto a website too. Economists try to explain prices. They study buying. They study selling. They study wealth. The law of supply and demand is the tool they use. The law tells how costs of goods and services shift in the market. The law also tells why those costs shift.

3Supply means how much of a good or a service exists. Demand means how much of it people want. Supply and demand set the price of every thing. The price of one product is not always the same. Popularity can change it. Location can change it. Season can change it. Prices move often. They move because supply and demand never stay still. The link also runs the other way. A new price can change supply. A new price can change demand. Did the movie on your shirt do poorly? Did that movie do very well? How popular the movie is can change the price of goods tied to it. Supply, demand, and price never stop moving. Each one sways the others from one moment to the next. Buyers like you help set what goods cost. You help set what services cost as well. A nearby restaurant offers a lunch deal on chicken sandwiches.

4The deal lets one price cover two sandwiches. In this deal, the sandwich price went down. The lower price made more people want the sandwich. Look at the deal from the seller's side. Why offer it? Maybe too many chicken patties were on hand. The kitchen may have needed a fast sale. Otherwise the patties might spoil. That situation is a surplus. A surplus here means more patties than buyers wanted. A surplus often pushes prices down. When the COVID-19 pandemic started in 2020, toilet paper became far more costly. Stores sold out fast. Some shoppers stored up large piles of it. Other shoppers could not find any. A normal item for the home had become scarce. In economics, scarcity is the reverse of abundance. Abundance means plenty of something is on hand. Sellers generally want the highest price the market will bear. They hope to earn a profit. A profit comes from selling for more than the cost to make the item.

5Buyers want to spend as little as they can. The pull between those wishes helps keep supply and demand in balance. Price an item too high, and demand often drops. Think again about the chicken sandwiches. Suppose one sandwich cost one hundred dollars. Most people would leave and eat lunch somewhere else. Want for that sandwich would fall. The fall would come as the price went up. People will pay more for popcorn inside a movie theater. They will pay less for popcorn at a supermarket. Products and services keep shifting in supply and in demand. Together, those shifts move the price. Hold supply steady and raise demand, and the price goes up. Hold supply steady and lower demand, and the price goes down. Raise supply and hold demand steady, and the price goes down. Lower supply and hold demand steady, and the price goes up. Price an item too low, and demand is likely to leap.

6Suppose each chicken sandwich cost one dollar. You would likely buy all you could eat. You might buy extra ones for friends. The restaurant would empty its supply very fast. Over a long time, neither side gains from either extreme. Shoppers will go far for a strong sale. Some will stand in line through the night. Some will wait even longer than that. If the sandwiches cost too much, fewer people want them. The restaurant earns nothing. The customer eats somewhere else. If the sandwiches cost too little, the kitchen may run out. Lots of customers will probably feel let down. The restaurant may fail to earn enough. It may not cover what the sandwiches cost to make. Picking a price for one chicken sandwich can look easy. It is not easy. The restaurant has to weigh what it pays for ingredients. It has to weigh what it pays the workers. It has to weigh the cost of keeping the doors open.

7Energy bills are part of that cost. Rent is part of that cost. It also has to watch the prices at other places. It has to judge what customers will agree to pay. Balancing every one of these factors is hard. Because of that, prices can fluctuate. When a holiday nears, watch the sale of certain goods. Candy is a clear example. Before the big day, holiday candy usually costs much more. After the day, the price is lower. Supply and demand help explain the gap. A chart can picture supply and demand for the chicken sandwiches. High demand sits on one side of the chart. Low supply and low demand sit on another side. The market equilibrium price is the meeting point. Buyers and sellers both gain when supply and demand sit nearer to even. People call that even point market equilibrium. In the sandwich case, equilibrium is a price with two results. Every sandwich finds a buyer.

8The seller does not run out. Customers will buy exactly the number of sandwiches the restaurant has. They do not want more than that number. They do not want fewer than that number. Now picture a concert tour. A band goes on tour every summer. Each year, more people like the band. Ticket supply stays fixed if the shows stay in the same stadium.
