Only so many seats fit in that stadium. The seat count does not change. The equilibrium price of a ticket will likely climb. The climb comes as the band grows more popular. At the same time, more people want tickets. When a trend loses fans, supply and demand can swing hard. Fidget spinners are one trend that lost fans. Each person likes different things. Each person values different things. Those differences shape what people buy. That is why prices keep moving under supply and demand. How a buyer sees a good also matters.

9How a buyer sees a service also matters. People often pay extra for what they view as valuable. They often pay extra for what they view as cool. Every buyer runs those numbers in a personal way. Personality is one guide. A budget is another guide. Shoe prices can spread across a wide range. What the buyer prefers drives much of that range. Plain sneakers usually cost much less than another kind. The costlier pair carries a famous athlete's name. Some buyers find worth in that athlete's endorsement. They will spend more because of the famous name. Other buyers hunt for the lowest shoe price they can find. No two buyers are the same. Values and personal situation shape each choice. One well-known line of athlete shoes has been for sale since 1985. A buyer has real power over supply and demand. You and the people you know enter markets all the time. It happens in ordinary days. Pause and list goods your family buys often.

10List the services too. The next time one of those prices rises, notice your feeling. Notice your feeling if the price falls. Did the new price change how much you wanted the item? What would cause that shift? A purchase puts you inside a push and a pull. The push and the pull are supply and demand. Demand is the need or the wish for a good or a service. An endorsement is support or approval for a product or a service. Sometimes the supporter is paid for that support. Fixed means something cannot be changed. Fluctuate means to change often. Influencing means having an effect on people or things. Merchandise means items people buy or sell. A product is something made, grown, or produced, most often for sale. Supply is how much of something is ready for sale or use. A surplus is what remains after the need has been met.

12. Dogs That Guard Little Penguins

1A loud bark from the Maremmas drives red foxes off. Eudy and Tula are out on patrol. Their watch is over the little penguins of Middle Island. Middle Island lies off the coast of Australia. These Maremma dogs are shaggy. Their coats are white. Each year, penguins come to the island to breed. The dogs watch over those penguins. The smell of the dogs scares red foxes away. The booming bark scares the foxes too. Those foxes nearly made the little penguins vanish. The island they use is small. It is rocky. About five and a half fields made for American football would cover Middle Island. During breeding season, little penguins come ashore each night.

2No other penguin is smaller. This bird stands only 30 centimeters tall, or 12 inches. It lives throughout New Zealand. It also lives in southern Australia. Some people call it the little blue penguin. Some people call it the fairy penguin. In breeding season, these birds use burrows dug in sand. After the eggs are laid, the adults wait for them to hatch. Once the eggs hatch, the adults fish in the daytime. At dusk they return to the burrows. They come back to feed the babies. A chick can leave the nest at about eight weeks old. For years, families grew up on Middle Island. The island is close to the city of Warrnambool.

3Then, in the 1990s, foxes started to show up on the island. Summer tides there are very low. At those times, a fox can reach the island on foot. More foxes meant more penguins eaten. Kristie King is the person who tracks the island penguins. She says fox attacks had nearly wiped out the island's penguins by 2005. Only four penguins were spotted that year. Australia got the red fox from British people in the 1830s. It seemed the little penguin's days on Middle Island might be over. A local chicken farmer had a strong idea. Alan Marsh, called Swampy, said Maremmas could guard the penguins. He already used the dogs to guard his chickens.

4Farm animals are what Maremmas were bred to protect. Chickens are a usual animal for them to guard. Sheep are a usual animal for them too. The dogs keep away predators such as wolves. They also keep away foxes. Eudy and Tula treat the boardwalk as the place where patrol starts. Italy is the home country of the Maremma sheepdog. People bred it to guard farm animals. In Australia, the same breed protects other animals as well. Guardian dog is the title the Middle Island Maremma Project uses. The name fits because they do not guard sheep. Neighbors and conservationists joined to test the idea. The Warrnambool City Council helped start the project. The Warrnambool Coastcare Landcare Group helped too.

5Together they created the Middle Island Maremma Project. To test Marsh's idea, one of his dogs came to the island. The tryout lasted a few weeks. These hero dogs proved to be the right answer. The dogs arrived in 2006. Since then, red foxes have made no known attacks on the penguins. King says the penguin count has been able to grow. By 2016, the island held almost two hundred penguins. Vince Haberfield also helps with the penguin counts. Volunteers weigh adult birds and young birds. They measure them too. Training for penguin work starts while a Maremma is young. Trainers begin by letting puppies meet chickens slowly. Over time, the dogs learn that the chickens belong there.

6After that lesson, the dogs protect the birds. At about two months old, the dogs meet the penguins. Volunteer Peter Abbott explains how the dogs see them. To the dogs, the penguins are chooks in dinner suits. Chooks means chickens. Trainers stay on the island with the dogs while the dogs learn. They learn the area. They grow comfortable with the penguins. Protection begins when the dogs accept that the penguins belong there. For one breeding season, trainers take the dogs to the island and back. Only after that season do the dogs stay on their own. Volunteers are the people who bring the dogs over. Every few days, the dogs leave the island to rest.

7Volunteers take good care of them. A wooden deck holds the trained dogs during breeding season. The deck has shade. The deck has food. The deck has water. From the deck, the dogs watch the penguins. Two dogs stay on the island in every breeding season. The main handler of these dogs is Phil Root. Off-duty hours are when he looks after the dogs. Maremmas are cute. They are fluffy. Even so, they usually are not good pets. They like to herd other animals. They like to protect other animals. Humans are among the animals they try to protect. That habit can make them a handful.

8Handler Phil Root is pictured with Eudy and Tula. Root says the volunteer work is an honor. The shared aim is to protect the Middle Island penguins. Caring for the Maremmas is a great responsibility, he says. The Warrnambool community truly loves these dogs. People stop to take photos. Their talk is about the pride they feel for the project. The dogs need a break from time to time. Harsh weather is normal on the island. A couple of days each week, the dogs leave to rest. A family takes them in when breeding season is finished. That family lives on a farm. A cove cuts into Middle Island. At Phillip Island, Australia, a worker measures penguins.

9About two hundred volunteers give time to keep the penguins safe. They give money too. In breeding season, volunteers count the penguins that arrive each night. They also check burrows. They look for penguins in those burrows. They look for chicks too. Abbott says each chick is recorded. Workers monitor the chicks for weight gain. They also monitor feather development. Other volunteers care for the island. They teach visitors about the project. Each day the dogs get a break so they can leave the boardwalk. Helping animals takes hard work. It also takes lots of support. Sometimes it takes one person's bright idea. The Middle Island Maremma Project shows what people can do together.

10Joined together, people can make a difference. To breed is to make a baby. It can also mean coming together to reproduce. Burrows are animal homes dug in the ground. Conservationists work to protect the environment. Their care includes animals, plants, and natural resources. To monitor is to observe how something is going. Patrol means moving in an area while keeping watch. The goal of a patrol is to keep the area safe. Penguins are aquatic birds. They are flightless. They live in the Southern Hemisphere. Population means every member of a single species inside one area. Predators hunt other animals. They eat those animals to survive. A volunteer offers help without being paid.

13. Fair Courts for the Whole Country

1This book begins with rule by laws. Then it ties the Constitution to the judicial branch. Next it shows how the federal courts work. Then it looks at the figure called Lady Justice. A list of hard words comes at the end. A judge may strike a gavel so the court pays attention. Americans do not bow to a king. Americans do not bow to a queen. No single leader tells us what we may say. No single leader tells us what we may do. Laws are what rule us. The laws are not perfect. The laws protect us. The laws help see that everyone gets fair treatment. People do not always obey the laws. People do not always agree on what is fair.

2Courts of law punish people who break laws. Courts also settle disagreements. One part of the United States government is the judicial branch. It keeps watch over the courts and the legal system. It also protects our rights. Lawyers for both sides of a case speak in front of a jury. A jury is a group of people chosen to hear a case. In 1787, American leaders make plans for the Constitution. Those leaders adopted the nation's Constitution in the year 1788. The Constitution was a system of laws for running the new country. The system has changed as time has passed. People still use the system today. The Constitution divides the government into three branches, or parts. The president leads the executive branch.

3That branch handles most government business. It also handles government departments. The Senate and the House of Representatives make up the legislative branch. That branch makes the laws. It stands for citizens inside the government. The judicial branch keeps watch on the courts and the legal system. A chart lays out the three branches. The executive side shows the president, the vice president, and the cabinet. The legislative side shows the Senate and the House of Representatives. The judicial side shows the Supreme Court and other federal courts. Sometimes the branches cooperate. At other times, the branches oppose one another. One section of the Constitution describes the judicial branch. That section explains how the branch is organized. The courts of the country deal with federal laws that people break.

4The courts also help judge whether new laws match the ideas in the Constitution. People sometimes clash over what the Constitution means. They also clash over what the Constitution allows. The first three parts of the Constitution describe the three branches. Those parts divide the branches' power in an even way. Each of the three branches can stop the other two. That power to stop is called a check. Checks and balances are a key piece of the Constitution. They help block any one branch from gaining too much power. Picking the judges is how the executive branch checks the judicial branch. The judicial branch checks the executive branch as well.

5It makes sure orders from the president follow the Constitution. The legislative branch can take judges out of office. That power is how it checks the judicial branch. The judicial branch makes sure laws passed by Congress follow the Constitution. Another check on the executive branch comes from the legislative branch. It holds control of the budget. It can take a president out of office. A check on the legislative branch can come from the executive branch. It can veto a bill and keep that bill from becoming law. Congress can vote a veto down if enough members agree. Cases can climb to the Supreme Court along more than one path. The Supreme Court of the United States sits at the top.

6A highest state court of appeals sits below that top court on one path. Thirteen federal courts of appeals sit on the federal path. Ninety-four federal district courts sit under those appeals courts. State appeals courts sit on the state path. Local trial courts sit under the state appeals courts. A case may move up from lower federal courts until it reaches the Supreme Court. A case may also arrive from a state court. That happens when the state case deals with federal law. Lower federal courts across the country form part of the judicial branch. The Supreme Court in Washington, D.C., forms the other part. Judges are the people who run every one of these courts. Most court cases in the United States begin in a lower court.

7The side that loses may ask a higher federal court to look again. A close study of the case comes next at the higher court. It looks for mistakes made by the first court. It might decide that the first court was right. After a loss there, that side may ask the Supreme Court for a review. In the higher federal courts, three judges hear a case together. They decide if they share the lower court's view. They may decide that they do not share that view. The Supreme Court ranks as the top court in the country. Justices choose which cases are important enough for a review. When they accept a case, they study it with care.

8Then they vote. The vote names the side that wins. The choice they make is final. A decision from this court can help shape the government. It can also help shape daily life for all Americans. Each year, about 7,500 cases arrive with a request for a hearing. In a usual year, it works on fewer than 150 of those cases. Nine justices serve on the Supreme Court. One of the nine is the chief justice. John Marshall served from 1801 to 1835. He was chief justice for a longer time than any other person in the court's history. Earl Warren served from 1953 to 1969. While he led the court, it worked toward equal rights for Black people.

9Thurgood Marshall served from 1967 to 1991. He was the first Black justice on the Supreme Court. Sandra Day O'Connor served from 1981 to 2006. She was the first woman on the highest court in the nation. Sonia Sotomayor began to serve in 2009. Her service continues. Before her, no Hispanic justice had served on the Supreme Court. Lady Justice showed up first in art from Rome. This figure, Lady Justice, stands for the judicial branch. She carries a set of scales. She carries a sword. A blindfold covers her eyes and shows that she is fair. She stands ready to defend justice. The judicial branch runs the justice system of the United States. The people who wrote the Constitution knew a sound justice system was important.

10Their new country needed that system. Peaceful paths for settling disagreements are part of what the system offers. Above all, it helps guard the rights of Americans. Visitors may sit inside the courtroom of the Supreme Court. From those seats they can hear the judges discuss cases. The Constitution is the written set of rules and principles behind the laws. A disagreement is a time when people hold different opinions. Federal describes a central government that shares power with states or regions. Judicial describes the branch that checks laws against the Constitution. Justice means the quality of being fair and honest. Justice also means the act of being fair and honest. Legal means allowed by the law.

14. Fast Fliers by the Water

1A southern hawker dragonfly is one kind shown here. Picture yourself walking past a lake. Or picture a walk past a stream. Suddenly a buzz rushes by. Something big shoots past your head. It is shiny. It is green. It is bigger than a bumblebee. It is faster than a butterfly. That flier is an amazing dragonfly. A dragonfly is an insect that flies. Its body is long. It has two pairs of wings. Its colors include every color of the rainbow. Some are bright red. Some are sunny orange. Some are metallic blue. Some are green. Some are gold. Colorful stripes mark the wings of some dragonflies. Colorful stripes mark the bodies of others. Some dragonflies even change color as time passes.

2A large head is one mark of a dragonfly. A long, skinny body is another mark. Two pairs of wings are a third mark. People often mix up dragonflies and damselflies. The two kinds of insects look alike. You can tell them apart after they land. A damselfly that is not flying folds its wings together. It holds those wings above its back. A dragonfly's wings cannot fold that way. A dragonfly keeps each wing straight out beside its body. They keep them that way all the time. Water is needed at every stage of a dragonfly's life cycle. Adults lay eggs in water. They may also lay eggs on plants near water.

3The young are called nymphs. After hatching, nymphs live in water. That water life lasts one year or more. Some dragonflies lay eggs in rotting wood under the water. Some can lay hundreds of eggs at one time. Nymphs do not look like adult dragonflies. Gills sit inside their bodies. Those gills let them breathe underwater. Dull green coloring helps them hide. Brown coloring helps them hide too. Fish are predators they must hide from. Frogs are predators they must hide from too. To get away, a nymph squirts a strong stream of water. The water shoots out through its gills. The squirt sends the nymph forward at high speed. A nymph's skin does not grow when the nymph grows.

4Each time it needs to grow, it sheds its whole outer layer. Readiness for adult life sends the nymph crawling out of the water. The adult then comes out of that last shed layer. Dragonfly eyesight is amazing. About thirty thousand tiny sections make up each eye. No insect sees with larger eyes than an adult dragonfly. They can see in almost every direction at the same time. That eyesight helps them catch prey. They can pick out one target inside a swarm of thousands. Up close, the eyes look like window screens. Scientists want to know where a swarm is headed. They glue miniature radio transmitters onto dragonfly bodies. Then they follow the insects with cars.

5They also follow them with an airplane. The chase can cross the country. Adult dragonflies are among the fastest flying insects in the world. They can reach one hundred body lengths each second. A large dragonfly can go faster than 22 miles an hour, or 36 kilometers. When one jumps to high speed, it speeds up hard. Even the fastest race cars on Earth pick up speed more slowly. Male dragonflies defend their homes. The rivals they face are other males. If a male spots an intruder, the two put on a show. The show is a high-speed flight display, not a harmful fight. The contest lets them show their speed. It also lets them show their size.

6They can do this without getting hurt. Most flying insects do not control each wing on its own. Dragonflies do. They can hover the way helicopters do. They can swoop and dive the way fighter jets do. They can fly forward. They can fly backward. They can even fly upside down. These fliers are quick. They are nimble. Scientists study this flight to learn how to improve airplane designs. They hope the same study will improve helicopter designs. They even hope to build a robotic dragonfly. The robot could go where a visit is too tricky for humans. It could also go where a visit is too dangerous. A dragonfly can make a meal out of a damselfly.

7An adult dragonfly is a predator that flies at high speed. Few hunters in the animal world succeed more often. Scientists say a catch happens on more than nine tries out of every ten. A lion catches dinner on only one try out of four. A dragonfly can bite fast. The bite is powerful. It surprises mosquitoes by coming from behind. The attack also comes from below. It surprises other small insects the same way. It often eats the catch without landing. Strong flying lets dragonflies avoid most predators. Dragonflies are food that many animals seek. Birds are among them. Lizards are among them. Frogs are among them. Spiders are among them.

8Other dragonflies are among them too. Few of those hunters can catch these fast insects. Humans eat dragonflies as well. People in many places eat them as a special food. Sellers put them on sticks in a thin row. They look like skinny corn dogs. The beak of an adult bee-eater grips a dragonfly. Scientists say swarms of dragonflies are hard to photograph. Huge groups of dragonflies come together at times. Those groups are called swarms. A swarm may form so the insects can feed. The prey is a large number of insects in one place. A swarm may also form for a move south. The move comes as the weather cools. Birds travel south for the winter in that same way.

9Japan got a park for dragonflies in 1985. No dragonfly park had existed in the world before that. Since then, more dragonfly parks have opened in Europe. Parks have opened in the United States as well. Dragonflies are amazing creatures. People in many lands are beginning to notice how special they are. Singapore's park for dragonflies places large statues out in the lake. The statues are shaped like dragonflies. Nearly three thousand kinds of dragonflies live in the world. They turn up in almost every kind of habitat. Look at a stream near you. Look at a pond. Look at a lake. Try to find these fancy fliers on your own.

10Gills are the organs many water animals use for breathing. Fish use gills. A habitat is the natural home of a plant. It is also the natural home of an animal. Hovering means hanging in a single spot in the air. Independently means without relying on something else to work. Insects are small animals with six legs. An insect has three body parts. An insect usually has two sets of wings. A life cycle is the set of changes in a living thing's life. A pair is a set of two. Prey is an animal that a predator hunts and eats. To shed is to drop skin. An animal can also shed feathers, hair, or antlers.

15. Fire on the Fifth of November

1A picture shows masks of Guy Fawkes. This book asks what Bonfire Night is. It asks who Guy Fawkes was. It tells about the Gunpowder Plot. It tells how the plotters were caught. It asks whether there was another conspiracy. It tells the legacy of the plot. It ends with a conclusion and a word list. An old nursery rhyme from the United Kingdom says to keep a date in mind. The date is the fifth of November. The rhyme speaks of gunpowder. It speaks of treason. It speaks of a plot. The rhyme says there is no reason for that gunpowder treason to be forgotten. People call the rhyme traditional. Every year on November 5, people in the United Kingdom gather. They watch fireworks. They attend bonfires. A dummy sits on top of the fire. The dummy is called a Guy. People cheer when the Guy goes up in flames. This day is Guy Fawkes Day. It is also called Bonfire Night. What is celebrated is a day from the year 1605. On that day, the king of England survived a plot.

2His government survived the plot too. The plot was a plan to kill them. The Bonfire Night celebration in Lewes, England, is the biggest in the world. Birth came to Guy Fawkes in 1570. The country of his birth was England. The ruler in those years was Queen Elizabeth I. At that time in Europe, religion divided many countries. War divided many Catholic countries from many Protestant countries. During Elizabeth's reign, the Catholic religion was illegal in England. A fine was the punishment for Catholics caught at their religion. Some were killed. Guido was the nickname used by Guy Fawkes. Guy's grandparents were Catholic. His parents were Protestant. After the death of Guy's father, his mother took a new husband. Her new husband was a Catholic. At school, Guy made friends with people who were Catholic. Guy joined the Catholic religion during his teen years. He became angry about violence in England. The violence was aimed at Catholics. England was left behind once Fawkes was old enough. He joined the Catholic Spanish army. His time as a soldier lasted twelve years. Using gunpowder was a skill he picked up.

3He learned how to make explosives. Death came to Queen Elizabeth I in 1603. The next ruler was King James I. He was a Protestant king. He passed tough laws against the Catholic religion. Loyalty to England's king remained among many English Catholics. So they worshipped in secret. Meetings with other Englishmen happened for Fawkes inside the Spanish army. They were also angry. Their anger was about the king's cruelty against Catholics. Robert Catesby stood at the head of a group of men in April 1604. The group made a plot to kill King James I. An invitation to join was given to Fawkes by the plotters. His skill at making explosives was what they required. Fawkes agreed. He became part of the group. A picture shows the Gunpowder Plotters. Robert Catesby led them. He is second from the right. In May 1604, a meeting took place in London. The London meeting brought Fawkes together with Catesby and the others. They swore an oath. They would never reveal their plan. Women were part of the wider movement.

4Anne Vaux was one of those women. Men alone took part in this particular plot. Their plan was to blow up the Parliament buildings. The king would be there. His whole government was going to be inside as well. The plotters had a new ruler in mind. They would make Princess Elizabeth queen. She was King James's daughter. Her age was only nine. They had a plan for how to raise her. Control of her reign, they believed, would follow if she was raised Catholic. Robert Catesby lived very near the target. His house was just across the river from Parliament. Thirty-six barrels of gunpowder were purchased by the plotters. They stored the barrels in his house. One of the group was Thomas Percy. He got a job as a royal bodyguard. A house close to Parliament became the place he lived. Renting a cellar was also something he could do. The cellar was beneath the buildings of Parliament. The group used a rowboat at night. They took the gunpowder across the river. The trip started at Robert Catesby's house.

5They hid the gunpowder in the cellar beneath Parliament. Guy Fawkes pretended to be a servant. He pretended to serve Thomas Percy. The disguise let him enter the Parliament buildings easily. Guard duty over the gunpowder belonged to him. The gunpowder was hidden in the basement. The opening of Parliament had a date. The date was November 5, 1605. The plotters were ready. Some people think the gunpowder had a problem. Some people believe the gunpowder Guy Fawkes had was too old to explode. Lord Monteagle, a Catholic nobleman, received a secret letter. His name was Lord Monteagle. The king had his loyalty. The letter warned Monteagle to stay away. It told him not to attend Parliament on November 5. The reason was that something bad would happen. The letter was taken by Monteagle to the assistant of the king. News of the letter led the king to give an order. A search of the Parliament buildings was the order he gave his men. Part of the letter is shown in a picture. The search party found firewood. The firewood was in the dark cellar.

6They did another search. This time they found Fawkes. They found the fuses he was carrying. They found the matches he was carrying. They looked under the firewood. They discovered the barrels of gunpowder. Guy Fawkes was caught. He explained his aim to the guards who served the king. Blowing up the king had been his aim. The government was also a target he had hoped to blow up. His only regret was the failure of the plot. He insisted on one claim. He said that no one else had helped him. Fawkes was put in prison. The prison was the Tower of London. Confession of the names came by November 9. Names of the other group members were what he confessed. News reached the other plotters on the morning of November 5. They heard that Fawkes was in prison. They left London. A revolt against the government was what they tried to begin. November 8 was the day the king's men reached them. Robert Catesby was killed in a gunfight. Three others were killed in that gunfight too.

7The rebellion had failed. A picture shows the Tower of London. Eight plotters were still alive. Death was the sentence from the English courts for each of them. The crime was treason. In January 1606, four of them were executed. Guy Fawkes was one of the four. Three others died with him. Their punishment was a warning. The warning was for anyone who might try the same act. Later, King James I praised Guy Fawkes. The praise was for his loyalty to his cause. Some people believe the king's own ministers played a part. They think the ministers might have helped the plotters. The warning letter may have been the writing of one royal minister. Perhaps the government already knew two facts. Renting of the basement had already been done by the plotters. They had smuggled the gunpowder inside. The stopped plot might have served as a tool. It could make Catholics look bad. It could create support for the unpopular king. This telling of the story has no proof behind it. Relief came to people once the Gunpowder Plot was stopped.

8The English people felt that relief. Their king was safe. A picture shows King James I. Parliament took an official step. It made November 5 a day of celebration. This changed how people saw an unpopular king. He became someone to celebrate instead. The holiday had an earlier name. It was first known as Gunpowder Treason Day. In the seventeenth century, people marked the day in two ways. They rang church bells. They lit bonfires. During this time in England, bonfires already had dummies. People burned dummies on those fires. They believed the burning would drive away evil spirits. On November 5, the dummy changed. A Guy Fawkes dummy was what people started to burn on the bonfire. Modern art shows Guy Fawkes with fireworks in his hands. Parliament stands in the background of that art. Bonfire Night has special traditions in some parts of Britain. Lewes is a town in the south of England. There, people wear costumes. They carry torches through the streets. In some towns, the dummy is not Guy Fawkes. People burn dummies of famous people. Those people are unpopular with the public today.

9In the nineteenth century, the holiday grew louder. Big firework displays began. Parades began too. People marched to the place of the displays. Bonfires and fireworks were held there. Now, local organizations put on the bonfires. Guy Fawkes Day looks back at a failed plan. The Gunpowder Plot failed to kill the king of England. The celebration marks that failure. More than four hundred years have passed since these events. The night is still enjoyed by many people in the United Kingdom. They enjoy bonfires on November 5. They enjoy fireworks on November 5. Another view of Guy Fawkes is held by some people. To them, he was someone who defended his own beliefs. Once a year, a search takes place. Guards of Parliament search its buildings once each year. They search before the official opening. They make sure no plotters are hiding in the basements. This search is a traditional ceremony. The hidden gunpowder had a huge weight. It weighed 2,500 kilograms. That weight is 5,512 pounds. If it had exploded, the blast would have moved outward.

10The reach would have been about 490 meters. That distance is 536 yards. A picture shows the guards getting ready. They prepare for their yearly search of Parliament. Guy Fawkes has an island named after him. The island is in the Galapagos Islands. The country is Ecuador. The island is called Isla Guy Fawkes. Bonfires are large fires built outside. They are built for celebrations. A dummy is a figure. It is a likeness or model of someone. Executed means killed. The killing is especially a punishment for committing a crime. Loyal means faithful. A loyal person is faithful to a person, a government, an institution, or a cause. Lawmaking for the United Kingdom is the work of Parliament. The House of Commons is one part. The House of Lords is the other part. A plot is a plan or a scheme. A rebellion is a fight against authority or power. It is an uprising. A revolt is a fight against a government or an authority. Treason is a crime of betrayal. It is disloyalty against one's government or ruler.

16. Forests That Have Grown Very Old

1Old-growth forest appears in four places around the world. Japan holds Shirakami-Sanchi. The United States holds Great Smoky Mountains National Park. The United States also holds two parks that share a story. One park is called Sequoia. The other is called Kings Canyon. Both are national parks. Russia holds the Western Caucasus. Age is the first question in this book. The United States comes next. Japan comes after that. Russia follows Japan. A close and a word list finish the pages. Old-growth forests do not share one look. A single kind of tree fills some of them. Many kinds of trees fill others. Great height marks some of the trees. A short size marks others.

2Difference is the first thing a visitor notices. Sameness is harder to see at a glance. A map can show all four forests at once. Each one is old growth. Each one sits far from the others. A visitor would not mix them up. Tree type can change from forest to forest. Height can change too. Age can change too. Later pages walk through those differences one forest at a time. Difference is so wide that one definition fails. Scientists do not share a single meaning for old-growth forest. Common traits still show up in many of them. Age is the trait many scientists require. 150 years is the least they accept. Every tree need not reach that age.

3A glance upward can make a person dizzy. Levels divide a forest. Tall trees form the canopy. Pines stand in that level. Maples stand there. Oaks stand there. Smaller trees form the understory. Bushes form it too. Grasses lie on the forest floor. Ferns lie there. Logs lie there. More than one layer should make up the canopy. The top layer is the tallest trees. Holes open in the canopy when trees fall. Sunlight slips through the holes. Young trees can rise in that light. Food for many living things comes from dead trees. Shelter for many living things comes from them too. Special plants live in these forests.

4Special animals live in them too. Nowhere else can those living things survive. Study of the forests teaches a great deal. Care of the forests teaches a great deal too. National parks of many kinds fill California. Old-growth forest is part of some of them. Sequoia is one park with that forest. Kings Canyon is another. Coniferous trees form most of the old growth there. Giant sequoias rise in this place. The world's largest tree is one of those giants. Life for a sequoia can run past 3,000 years. One famous tree sets the size record. People call it the General Sherman Tree. No other tree on the planet is larger. A Pacific fisher lives in the park as well.

5Porcupine is the meal it likes best. Old-growth forest is the only home it uses. Protection is the goal of some states because of that limit. Towering trees are not in every old-growth forest. Harsh sites exist. Winds there are wild. Winds there are icy. Short growth is the result. Twisted growth is the result. Pines can take that form. Spruces can take that form. Other coniferous trees can take it too. Krummholz is the name people use. The word is German. Crooked wood is what the word means. Height is only about 6 feet. The metric height is 1.8 m. Size can fool a visitor. Age for some of them is one thousand years.

6Autumn color is bright at Great Smoky Mountains National Park. This park lies far from California. North Carolina holds part of it. Tennessee holds part of it. Only a small share of the park is old growth. Deciduous trees are most of what grows in these forests. Leaves on those trees change with the season. That change is why autumn looks so bright. The Carolina northern flying squirrel lives here. Endangered is its status. Real flight is not its skill. A glide is what it does instead. Skin stretches from the front paws. Skin stretches from the back paws. The animal spreads that skin and glides.

7Cutting of other forests put these squirrels in danger. Those forests were other places they could live. People did the cutting. Loss of those homes is why the danger grew. A visitor in autumn sees color in the canopy. The squirrel is part of the same forest picture. A Japanese beech pushes upward toward the sun. Shirakami-Sanchi in Japan is where it stands. East Asia has almost no old-growth forest left. This one is among the last. The area itself is small. Northern mountains hold it. Forest of this kind once spread over most of Japan's north. A rare serow makes the forest its home. The animal looks like a goat. It survives in very few spots.

8This forest is one spot. Japan still has two further small pockets for it. Black woodpeckers share the forest. Japan counts those birds as endangered. A photo can show the serow. A photo can show the woodpeckers. Both animals live in this old forest. The beech in the picture reaches upward. A fir lies fallen in the Caucasus of Russia. Old growth is the forest around it. Other plants can grow with help from a fallen fir like this. A fir like this can nurse those plants along. The Western Caucasus holds many forests. Coniferous trees are most of those forests. Plant life there is highly varied. Animal life there is highly varied too.

9Snow leopards may still walk these woods. Some scientists think the number is only a few. Endangered describes the cats. Prints of paws are found at times. Other clues are found as well. The wisent has been brought into the forest again. Endangered describes the wisent too. A wisent is a European bison. The return means the forest holds them again. Hundreds of years is a possible age for old-growth forest. Thousands of years is another possible age. Protection covers many of these places. Danger still hangs over some. Damage can come from humans. Destruction can come from humans. Return may never happen once the forest is gone. Logging is blocked by law in some old-growth forests.

10Other forests are left out of those laws. Argument continues among people. The question is how much protection to give. Canopy means the treetop zone. Leaves there form a thick layer. Coniferous points to a plant group. Evergreen trees and shrubs are most of the group. Cones are what they grow. Needles are one leaf shape. Scales are another leaf shape. Deciduous plants drop leaves in the fall. New leaves return in the spring. Endangered means dying out is a real risk. Old-growth points to forests of great age. Protect means to guard. Harm is what the guard stands against. Danger is what it stands against too.

17. Four Steps of a Monarch

1A picture of the monarch life cycle shows four forms. One form is the egg. One form is the caterpillar. One form is the pupa. One form is the adult butterfly. A monarch passes through these four stages while it lives. The body changes at every stage as the insect develops. A young caterpillar breaks free when it hatches from the egg. A female monarch usually places a single egg under a leaf. The egg has an oval shape. She sets it on the leaf's lower side. She might produce several hundred eggs before her life ends.

2Inside the tiny egg, a caterpillar grows. Larva is another name for that caterpillar. Hatching takes anywhere from three days to twelve days. Caterpillars never drink. Food gives them every bit of water they need. The hungry caterpillar keeps eating almost without a stop. Growth calls for a large supply of food. The caterpillar's own eggshell is its first meal. Other eggs on that same leaf are eaten next. Afterward it eats the leaf beneath its body. The caterpillar's body gets big at a fast pace.

3The skin does not get bigger at that same pace. Old skin splits open. The old skin then peels off. Molting is the name for this shedding. This stage of the life cycle includes many molts. An instar is the stretch of time between one molt and the next. Sticky silk comes from the caterpillar. The silk keeps the old skin from slipping away. A wiggle lets the caterpillar come free of the skin. Leaves of the milkweed plant are the only food a monarch caterpillar eats.

4Bright black covers the caterpillar. Bright yellow covers it too. Those colors help the caterpillar stay safe. They warn other animals of a bad result. An animal that eats the caterpillar will get sick. Skin hidden under the old layer starts out soft. That new skin turns hard before long. The caterpillar goes through four molts, or as many as six. Each shed skin is eaten. This quick growth lasts from two weeks to four weeks. Eating more lets the caterpillar gain more size after every molt.

5Striped skin opens down the caterpillar's back. The opening works like a zipper. Bright green skin shows once the old skin is gone. A fully grown caterpillar hangs head-down from a branch. It is preparing for its greatest change. One final molt takes place. Bright green is the color of the skin this time. The skin looks much like a leaf. The green coloring helps it hide from predators. Predators may think it is only one more leaf. The green skin grows hard around the body.

6Predators are kept away while the change happens inside. The caterpillar is now a pupa. Chrysalis is the other name for a pupa. Protection inside the shell lasts from one week to two weeks. In that time the body is transformed completely. The butterfly draws air inside. Its body swells because of the air. The shell can break once the body is larger. The butterfly then climbs free. An adult comes out of the shell wearing wet wings. Those wings lie folded tight on a damp body.

7The butterfly hangs head-down from a branch. Blood is forced out into the wings. The wings extend because of that blood. Hanging head-down is how the butterfly rests. Strength returns during the rest. Flight will not be possible if it skips this hang. Blood that pumps into the wings makes them unfold. The wings then spread wide. Most monarch wings measure about 4 inches from one tip to the other. About 10 centimeters is the same measure. The hang goes on for several hours.

8Delicate wings dry under the sun. They dry in the air as well. Two pairs of wings belong to a butterfly. Scales are what cover the wings. The front pair often shows a pattern a little unlike the back pair. Even so, the colors on both pairs are often much the same. A butterfly at rest holds the wings up. Nectar is the drink of a monarch. This sweet liquid is made by plants. The tongue is long, and it has the shape of a tube.

9Nectar travels in through that tongue. Once flying is possible, the butterfly leaves on a search. A mate is one thing it looks for. Food is the other thing it looks for. A butterfly does not feed on leaves, though a caterpillar does. The long tongue sips nectar out of flowers. Adults keep their energy for flight. They also keep energy for laying eggs. They do not spend the energy on getting larger. A female is usually larger than a male. Wing stripes on a female are usually wider.

10Migration waits for the season's final monarchs. Migrate means to move on to another place. Late summer is when they head toward a warmer land. These few butterflies live on through the winter. They can come back home after the winter. A mate can be found at that time. A monarch usually lives from two weeks to six weeks. Egg laying starts once a mate has been found. Then the butterfly's life cycle begins again. Eggs laid in her lifetime may number from 400 to 500.

18. Frederick Douglass Finds a Free Life

1Frederick Douglass appears in an 1866 picture. Bread traded for lessons opens his story. Birth into slavery comes next. Escape follows. Writing and speaking come after the escape. A land of the free is another part. Useful words close the book. Eight was the age of Frederick Douglass. A loaf of bread was in his hands. The back door was his way out. Errands were the job he was sent to do. The errands took him into the neighborhood. Slavery was his condition. Food at his house was more than some neighborhood boys had. Trade was his answer. Bread went to those boys. An education was what he asked in return. That learning mattered more to him than the extra food. The 1820s blocked school skills for enslaved people. Reading was against the law. Writing was against the law. A trick helped Frederick. Showing off was the trick. Letters scratched in the dirt were his display. Other letters then came from the poor white boys. The alphabet was his before long. A new trade started soon after. Food bought him lessons. Reading was one lesson.

2Writing was another. Lunch left his hands every day. Learning was the reason he gave it up. Slavery was the world he was born into. February was the month. The year was 1817 or 1818. His birthday stayed unknown to him. White was the race of his father. The man's name stayed unknown. Harriet Bailey was his mother. Grandmother's care is what raised him. Betsey Bailey was that grandmother. Work took him away at age eight. The Auld family was the household. Baltimore, Maryland was the city. Help came from Mrs. Auld at the start. The alphabet was what she helped him learn. Her husband then made her stop. An educated enslaved person would want freedom. That was his claim. Secret reading of the newspaper built his skill. One word stayed hard for a while. Abolition was that word. Blame fell on abolition when an enslaved person ran off. Blame fell on it when an enslaver was displeased. A dictionary was where he searched. Ending something was all the book said. The thing being ended was still a mystery. News in 1831 cleared the fog.

3The abolition movement was in the paper. People working to stop slavery were in it too. Understanding arrived. White people were among those who wanted slavery stopped. Freedom for Black people existed in some states. That fact reached him too. Escape became his dream. Later writing returned to the power of reading. A person who learns to read, he said, stays free from that time on. City life had been his for years. Fifteen brought a change of work. A farm was the new place. Field hand was the job. Edward Covey was the man in charge. Beatings were Covey's tool. Breaking a person's spirit was the aim. Six months of those beatings fell on Frederick. Fighting back came on one day. Nearly two hours was the length of the fight. Covey never tried a beating again after that. Freedom stayed in Frederick's dreams. Literacy stayed in his work. A debate club took him in. Anna Murray was the person he met there. A free Black woman was who she was. Escape plans were made by the two of them. Savings from Anna were sent to him.

4A train ticket was what the money could buy. A sailor's uniform was sewn by her. That uniform was what he wore. A passport of a free man was in his hands. A train carried him to New York. The trip stayed under twenty-four hours. Only a small margin remained before that full day. Later words described that day. More living happened in it, he wrote, than in a full year. The year he meant was one year of life under slavery. Audiences heard him years afterward. He called himself a thief in front of them. He called himself a robber too. His own head, he said, had been stolen from the man who claimed to own him. His limbs had been stolen too. His body had been stolen too. He ran, carrying all of it away. True freedom did not arrive with the escape. A free state could be reached. Life there could look like a free life. Seizure was still possible. A return to a slave state was still possible. Anna was sent for. Marriage came in 1838.

5Capture was still a risk. Slavery could have taken him again. A new last name hid the couple. Douglass was the name they chose. New Bedford, Massachusetts became their home. Five children were born to them. The 1800s saw a secret group take shape. Help for people who had escaped was its work. The Underground Railroad became the network's name. Food waited at some stops. Clothing waited at some stops. Shelter waited at some stops. Safe houses was one name for the stops. Stations was another name. Feet carried many of the travelers. Spacing of the stops was the helpers' aim. Conductors was one name for the helpers. Stationmasters was another name. Fifteen miles was the gap they tried to keep. The metric gap is 24 km. Safe houses sheltered Douglass right after he escaped. A stationmaster's role came later, and it was his. Rochester, New York was where he did that work. Fugitives got help from him. Fugitives got help from Anna. Canada was where those people were sent. Ripley, Ohio had a safe house. Intelligence was the excuse enslavers used. Freedom, they said, asked for more smarts than enslaved people had.

6Antislavery meetings were where Douglass spoke. Each talk proved the excuse false. Skill with words created a new doubt. Some white listeners refused to believe slavery had ever held him. Proof of that past had to be written down. An autobiography was the form he chose. The title was Narrative of the Life of Frederick Douglass. Publication came in 1845. Fakeness was the critics' charge against the book. Fakeness was their charge against him too. Writing that strong, they said, was beyond any enslaved person. Success of the book was huge anyway. A great mind, readers learned, could belong to an enslaved person. Danger sat inside the details. Arrest could have followed those details. Capture was what he meant to avoid. Leaving the country was the step he took. Two years was the length of the speaking tour. Electrifying is the word for how he spoke. England treated him as a star. Money was raised by fans there. Purchase of his freedom was the use of that money. A return to the United States came in 1848.

7A newspaper was founded by him. Speeches poured from his pen. Thousands of them were written. Editorials were written too. An end to slavery was the call in that writing. Showing slavery plainly was his stated reason. Destruction of slavery, he wrote, follows from showing it. A monster living in darkness was his picture of slavery. Truth's light, he said, kills that monster. One famous speech carries a question as its name. The name is What to the Slave Is the Fourth of July? Surprise was the effect on his listeners. Questions were his method. Independence Day was the subject. Meaning of that day for enslaved people was what he asked. Meaning for people no longer enslaved was what he asked too. National independence, he asked, had what to do with him. The people he spoke for were part of that question. Political freedom was one great idea he named. Natural justice was another. Were those ideas, he asked, truly given to his people? Fame by the Civil War was at its peak. By the Civil War, fame put him above every other Black man.

8The United States was that country. Advice to President Lincoln was his role in 1863. The Emancipation Proclamation was the subject. Most enslaved people were freed by that order. A link to another speech is what some historians see. Lincoln's Gettysburg Address is that speech. They think Douglass helped inspire it. Black soldiers were another cause he pressed. He wanted Lincoln to let them fight. The North was the side he named. Two sons of Douglass joined when the chance came. Guards from one unit appear in a photo. Unit 107th United States Colored Troops is in the photo. The Civil War is when they served. Douglass stands with President Lincoln in another picture. Death of Lincoln brought a memorial speech from Douglass. A standing ovation came from the crowd. A widow's gift followed. Mary Todd Lincoln was her name. A walking stick was the gift. It had been Lincoln's favorite. Appreciation was the reason she gave it. The house of Douglass still holds that stick. Slavery's end owed much to his writing. It owed much to his speaking. The Thirteenth Amendment was the legal end.

9Passage came after the Civil War closed in 1865. Three years later, another amendment arrived. Citizenship for formerly enslaved people came from the Fourteenth Amendment. The year 1870 brought the Fifteenth Amendment. A vote was given to every male citizen. Black men were part of that right. Women's rights were a fight he never dropped. Death was the day he stopped. The year 1920 brought a further change. States ratified the Nineteenth Amendment. A vote for women was the result. A true land of the free was his hope for the United States. Black people were included in that hope. Women were included. Native Americans were included. Immigrants were included too. Joining anyone in order to do right was his rule. Joining no one in order to do wrong was his rule. Equality was the fight he kept. The rest of his life was given to it. February 20, 1895 was the day he died. A title is often given to him today. People often call him the father of a great movement. That movement is civil rights. Thought about slavery changed because of him.

10Thought about race changed because of him. Words he left still stir Americans. A motto was among those words. Right, the motto says, belongs to no one sex. Truth, the motto says, has no color. Douglass appears in a photo from the 1870s. Abolition means putting an end to something. Making slavery illegal is one use of the word. An amendment changes a document or a law. An addition counts too. An autobiography is a life story that is true. The person in the story is the writer. Civil rights cover law, social life, and money. Freedom for every citizen is what they lock in. Equality for every citizen is what they lock in. Editorials are pieces in a newspaper or a magazine. The publisher's view is what they give. Electrifying describes a burst of excitement. Enthusiasm is part of that feeling. Enslaved means slavery was the person's condition. Another person owned them or forced their labor. Inspire means to push a person toward action. Literacy means a person can read. It also means a person can write.

19. From a Small Lake to the Gulf

1A map traces the Mississippi from north to south. Lake Itasca sits in Minnesota. Canada lies to the north. Wisconsin is on the map. So is Minneapolis. The map shows the United States. Iowa is on the map. Mexico is on the map. Illinois is on the map. The upper part of the river is marked. St. Louis is on the map. Missouri is on the map. Kentucky is on the map. The lower part of the river is marked. Tennessee is on the map. Arkansas is on the map. Memphis is on the map. The state of Mississippi is on the map. Louisiana is on the map. New Orleans is on the map. The map names the Mississippi River states.

2The Gulf of Mexico is at the end of the route. Clouds sit over Lake Itasca in Minnesota. Rain falls on that lake. The rain mixes into clear river water. A child could wade from one bank of this river to the other. The mighty Mississippi begins as this small river. Lake Itasca is only one name for the starting lake. The Ojibwe people were the first to live in the area. They called the lake Omashkoozo-zaaga'igan. A watershed is the land that drains into a river or a lake. The Mississippi River watershed ranks fourth in size in the world. Many U.S. states lie inside it. A bit of Canada lies inside it too. The Mississippi takes in the water of many other rivers.

3One of those rivers is the Missouri. The Missouri beats the Mississippi in length. The Ohio River flows into the Mississippi as well. The moving water needs the next three months for its trip. The journey covers 2,350 miles, or 3,781 kilometers. The water passes through ten U.S. states. It flows past plants. It flows past animals. It flows past cities. It flows past towns. It flows past farmland. It carries food to people along the river. It carries other items to them too. At last the water enters the Gulf of Mexico. A lock in Minnesota can raise a boat. It can lower a boat too. The boat can then pass around the dam. The trip along the Mississippi can go on.

4After the lake, the water runs through dozens of locks and dams. Locks and dams work together. These structures can lift the water around a boat. They can also drop that water. Boats can then travel safely while water levels change. The structures also hold water back. That way boats do not hit the river bottom. In Iowa, ground corn is poured onto a barge. A barge counts as a boat. That boat is large. Its bottom is flat. It carries goods. A barge beats a line of semi trucks when the load is big. One barge carries as much as fifteen train cars. That same load would fill fifty-eight trucks. The U.S. Army Corps of Engineers gives this comparison.

5Corn rides south on the boats that use this stretch. They carry soybeans south too. A large share of this food is sent to buyers around the world. The upper river has more than fifty mammal types along it. River otters here are playful. They hunt in the water. They swim in the water. At least 260 kinds of fish live in the Mississippi River. North American fish types include this group as 25 percent of the total. The pallid sturgeon is one of those kinds. Few fish on Earth are older than this one. In 70 million years, it has hardly changed. The Missouri River joins the Mississippi. St. Louis, Missouri, lies a few miles south of where the rivers meet.

6The Missouri comes in from the west. Its water is muddy. It mixes with the clear water of the Mississippi. The Missouri passes along a nickname. That nickname is The Big Muddy. The Mississippi grows wide at this meeting too. For the next 190 miles, or 306 kilometers, wild land lines the river. Many wild animals live in those areas. Tall trees along here hold bald eagle nests. Fish from this river are what those eagles eat. The Ohio River meets the Mississippi. Illinois, Kentucky, and Missouri meet near that joining. The two rivers create three state borders there. The Ohio comes in from the east. The amount of water doubles. The river grows wider still.

7In many spots, the banks are 1.5 miles apart, or 2.4 kilometers. By the time the water reaches Louisiana, the river is very deep. In some places it is deep enough to cover the Statue of Liberty. Without her base and pedestal, the statue is 151 feet tall, or 46 meters. The greatest depth is about 200 feet, equal to 61 meters. The depth changes from year to year. The lower river has no dams. It does have a system to hold floods in. Some floods are simply too big for that system. Floods are what gave this river its fame. Those floods sometimes destroy buildings. They sometimes kill people. In 2012, a man helped a neighbor's daughter in New Orleans.

8He helped her cross a street that was flooded. Not every flood is harmful. Floods leave rich soil along the banks. That soil makes good farmland. Cotton grows well there. Grains grow well there too. More cities rise along the lower river. Boats leave from Tennessee with heavy goods. Boats leave from Louisiana too. The goods include iron. They include steel. They include rubber. They include paper. They include wood. Farm crops such as cotton travel from the area as well. Some goods turn around and travel back toward the north. Some items move farther south. New Orleans stands in Louisiana. A floating log in Louisiana supports a resting American alligator. Several types of turtles live in this part of the river.

9The American alligator lives here too. Humans swim in the river at times. In the summer of 2002, Martin Strel swam the whole river. He is a long-distance swimmer from Slovenia. The swim took sixty-eight days. A picture from space shows the Mississippi River. In that view, the river runs into the Gulf of Mexico. Past New Orleans, the river's water joins the gulf. Rain that fell at Lake Itasca has reached the ocean. The trip from the lake took three months. Reaching the ocean meant crossing the United States from end to end. Someday, some of that water may fall again as rain on Lake Itasca. The same water may make the journey once more. It may do so again after that.

10Dams are barriers that stop water from flowing. Floods are water covering land that is usually dry. Too much rain can cause a flood. A lock is a gated section of a river or a canal. Water in a lock can be raised. Water in a lock can be lowered. Ships and boats can then pass. Mammals are warm-blooded animals with backbones. They have hair or fur. They nurse their young. Their babies are born alive. The Mississippi River is a major river of the United States. Minnesota is where the flow begins. The Gulf of Mexico is where the flow ends. Shipped means goods were transported. A large boat is a common way to ship them.

20. Galileo Galilei and His Questions

1A map of Galileo's world shows Austria and Switzerland. It shows England and Germany. It shows the Atlantic Ocean twice. It shows France, Italy, and Spain. It marks the cities of Rome and Pisa. It shows the Mediterranean Sea. It shows the islands of Corsica, Sardinia, and Sicily. A scale on the map runs from 0 to 100. The scale is marked in miles and in kilometers. The parts of this book are an introduction and Galileo's childhood. Other parts tell about the student, the teacher, and the astronomer. Another part tells how he got in trouble with the Church. A later part tells about science after Galileo. The book ends with word meanings. His full name was Galileo Galilei. Think about a chandelier and the planet Venus. What do those two things share? Each one played a part in a set of discoveries. The discoveries belonged to one of history's greatest scientists. He lived five hundred years ago. Italy was the country of Galileo's birth.

2The year was 1564. Even as a boy, he was good at asking questions. Those questions got him in trouble. Still, he changed the way we understand the world. As a young man, he wanted to learn about math. He also wanted to learn about physics. Matter, energy, and motion are the three parts of physics. University was still a year away when he made an early physics discovery. The year of that discovery was 1582. He was inside a church that day. A chandelier hung from the ceiling. He watched it. Wind outside the church made the chandelier swing back and forth. He noticed something strange. The swings were not all the same width. Each swing still lasted for exactly the same time. The swinging chandelier taught Galileo a new idea. Keeping time is possible with a weight that swings back and forth. From that idea, he later worked out a way to build a clock. People today still use clocks made from his idea.

3A grandfather clock is one example. Clocks of that kind still use pendulums to keep time. Galileo did not study math at the start of university. He entered university in 1583. Becoming a doctor was the future his father chose for him. The study of medicine never interested Galileo much. In fact, he never finished those studies. Money problems made him leave university in 1585. For the next four years, he taught math. Science stayed interesting to him the whole time. In 1589, he returned to the university. This time he came back as a math teacher. He kept asking questions. He kept testing things. Falling objects were the focus of several of his tests. He wanted to see what would happen. Fame from later discoveries had not arrived yet. A famous story tells about one experiment from his teaching years. The story says he dropped two heavy balls. He dropped them from the Leaning Tower of Pisa.

4One ball had a much greater weight than the other ball. Even so, the story says they fell at the same speed. Today, it is not certain that this toss really happened. The year 1609 brought Galileo news of a spyglass. The spyglass had been invented in the Netherlands. A person who looked through it saw a change. Faraway objects looked closer. No spyglass had been seen by Galileo before that. Even so, he figured out how to build one. Soon he could make faraway objects look thirty times bigger. In other words, he had built a telescope. He began to use that telescope on the night sky. The telescope changed his life. It changed science forever. Discovery followed discovery. He saw dark spots on the surface of the Sun. The spots were there only sometimes. He discovered the four largest moons of Jupiter. Seeing a change in Venus was something no person had done before him. Shape changes were visible on the planet Venus.

5Just like the Moon, Venus sometimes looked like a full circle. At other moments, only a thin slice of light from Venus showed in the night sky. Some of the discoveries went into a book during 1610. That book made him famous. However, fame had a price. He drew the surface of the Moon. The drawing shows what he saw through his telescope in 1610. In 1992, a probe mapped the surface of the Moon. NASA sent that probe. NASA is the National Aeronautics and Space Administration. In Galileo's time, many people held one big belief. Earth, they believed, sat at the center of our solar system. They thought the other planets moved around Earth. They thought the Sun moved around Earth too. They also thought some planets moved in a special path. Venus was one of those planets. They thought Venus moved in small circles. At the same time, they thought Venus made a big circle around Earth.

6Under that old idea, Venus would look one way from Earth. From Earth, under that old idea, Venus would show only a crescent. An old drawing shows the Sun's orbit around Earth. It shows the orbit of Venus around Earth. It shows Venus as a crescent. Galileo looked through his telescope and saw something else. Seen from Earth, Venus showed many shapes. Those changing shapes suggested a new path. They suggested that Venus was moving around the Sun. They suggested that Earth was moving around the Sun too. A newer drawing shows Venus in many shapes. The shapes are full, nearly full, half, crescent, and new. That drawing places the Sun in the center. It places Earth on a path around the Sun. Trouble found Galileo because of what he learned about the solar system. His ideas did not please the Catholic Church. His ideas were different from Church teachings. For centuries the sky was taught by the Church in one set way.

7Earth as the center of our solar system was what the Church taught. In that teaching, the Sun moved around Earth. The Moon moved around Earth. The planets moved around Earth. The stars moved around Earth. The changing shape of Venus did not fit this picture. Taken together, his discoveries were big clues. The clues said the solar system moved around the Sun. The clues said it did not move around Earth. Someone else had offered this idea before he did. Still, no better proof had been found before his discoveries. Earth, the proof said, was not our solar system's center. The Church did not like the idea that Earth was not the center. At that time, the Church had a strong power. It could put people on trial for ideas. The ideas were ones that went against Church teachings. Galileo's trial before the Church took place in 1633. The trial found him guilty. He knew the discoveries told the truth.

8Lies were the words he was forced to speak, even though he did not accept them. The Church ordered him to stay in his home. The order lasted for the rest of his life. A ban also stopped him from publishing further books. A popular story says Galileo talked back to the judges. The story says he whispered four words under his breath. The words were and yet it moves. He is said to have whispered them after a forced statement. Earth, the forced statement claimed, did not travel around the Sun. The records of the trial do not include this story. The story is probably not true. Those words, and yet it moves, grew very famous anyway. People quote those words even today. Galileo kept writing his ideas anyway. His final book came out in 1638. By then, he had become blind. His health was poor. He died in 1642. The cause was natural. Earth's path was something Galileo had understood correctly, as we know now.

9The path of Earth goes around the Sun. He had a gift for asking important questions. That gift helped him make great discoveries. His time saw few discoveries greater than these. He was the first scientist to make one claim about nature. Math, he said, could be used to write down the laws of nature. Careful work with measurements put him among the first scientists to do so. He always used measurements in his experiments. In this way, he made a test easier for other scientists. Other scientists could test his ideas. Many later scientists took him as a model. Isaac Newton was one of those scientists. Albert Einstein was another. Today, people call him the father of modern science. In 1757, the Catholic Church took a book off a list. The book was Galileo's. The list was a list of banned books. The Church said in 1992 that no wrong had been done by Galileo.

10Proven facts of science are accepted by the Church today. For centuries, scientific studies have had support from the Church. A model is a person or thing viewed as an example to imitate. A chandelier is a fancy light that hangs from the ceiling. It often has branches for holding lights. Publish means to make writing available to the public. Experiments are scientific tests or trials. A solar system is a group of objects in space. Those objects orbit a star. Laws of nature are scientific truths. They are principles that explain how nature works. A telescope is an instrument. It is used to make distant objects look closer. Measurements are the act of finding a size, a quantity, or an amount. They can also be the figures, sizes, or amounts that result. A trial is a legal process in a court of law. A court trial decides whether an accused person or group is innocent or guilty.

21. George Washington Carver, Scientist of the Soil

1Carver stands in a lab in a 1940 photo. His name is George Washington Carver. Birth during slavery opens his story. Learning without a teacher comes next. Making a difference follows. Free ideas are another part. A message that lasted comes near the end. Useful words close the book. A map lists states tied to his life and his work. Iowa, Ohio, Nebraska, and Indiana appear. Illinois, Colorado, Kansas, and Kentucky appear. Missouri, Arkansas, Oklahoma, and Mississippi appear. Georgia, Alabama, Texas, and Louisiana appear. Florida appears as well. Missouri is where he grew up. Kansas is where he studied. Iowa is where he studied too. Alabama is where he worked. Fighting in the Civil War ran from 1861 to 1865.

2The fight took place inside one country. The North was one side. The South was the other side. At the start, slavery was legal in part of the country. Fifteen southern states allowed it. People called them slave states. Slavery was illegal in another part. Seventeen northern states banned it. People called them free states. Victory went to the North. Freedom reached every enslaved person in time. Missouri is the birthplace of George Washington Carver. 1864 is the year. The Civil War had not ended yet. His mother was enslaved. Slavery was therefore his condition at birth. Jim, his older brother, was born into slavery too. His father was enslaved as well. Death took the father before the baby arrived.

3Diamond, Missouri is where the Carver home stood. A photo from the 1870s shows two brothers. George is one. Jim is the older one. Freedom came to George when the Civil War closed. He remained in that same household. Susan Carver was one person there. Moses Carver was the other. They had been his enslavers. Reading was a skill Susan taught him. Writing was a skill she taught him too. Gardens pleased him more than anything. The outdoor world was his place to explore. Plants were a subject he taught himself. Animals were a subject he taught himself too. More learning meant he wanted school. Segregated schools were common in many places. Sharing a school was not allowed for Black students and white students.

4The nearby school was closed to George. Being Black was the reason. Twelve was his age when he left the Carver home. Another school was his goal. Kansas came a year after that. A 1916 photo shows a school just for Black children. Kentucky is where that school stood. College in Kansas was closed to Carver. Skin color was the barrier. High school ended for George while he was in Kansas. College there was what he tried next. Arrival did not help. The college ordered him to go. Black students were not allowed in. Quitting was not his choice. Iowa State Agricultural College became his school. Farming was the subject he mastered there.

5Finishing made him a first. No Black student had finished at that college before him. A professor's post was another first. No Black student had held that post there before him. Alabama became his home in 1896. Teaching began at the Tuskegee Institute. Forty-seven years was the length of his stay. A diploma appears in a picture. Iowa State Agricultural College is the school that granted it. Farmers in Alabama were the people he went to help in 1896. Cotton harvests shrank year after year. Problems for the farmers kept growing. Long days of work fell on children in the late 1800s. Cotton picking on plantations was their job. Soil filled Carver's hand in one photo. A worn-out field was the source of that chunk.

6Southern crops became his laboratory work. He saw the cause of the tired soil. The same crop had been planted over and over. Dead leaves were one fix he taught. Dead plants were another fix. Soil improved when farmers added them. New crops were part of the lesson too. Sweet potatoes were one choice. Peas were another choice. Peanuts were another choice. Those crops returned materials the soil had lost. Strong growth depends on those materials. Buyers were what the new crops needed. Sweet potatoes gained many new uses from Carver. The count of those uses passed one hundred. Peanuts gained many new uses too. Three hundred was the count for peanuts. Products grew out of the new uses.

7Items farmers could sell grew out of the products. Carver appears at work in a photo. Peanut uses he created reached as high as three hundred. Pavement was one use. Grease was one use. Medicines were one use. Peanut coffee was one use. Peanut mayonnaise was one use. Peanut flour was one use. Peanut milk was one use. Shoe polish was one use. Bleach was one use. Sandpaper was one use. Still more uses existed. A common story says he created peanut butter. The story is false. Peanut paste was food for the Aztecs. Central Mexico was their home. More than five hundred years have passed since they ate it. The Incas ate a paste like it.

8Peru was their home. Their use came even earlier. Creaminess marks the peanut butter people eat now. The older paste did not have that creaminess. A speech was requested of Carver in 1921. The audience was the U.S. Congress. Lawmakers of the country make up Congress. Ten minutes was the time he was offered. Some listeners did not want his voice. His being Black was their reason. Those ten minutes ended. More speaking was what they then requested. A law was the final result in Congress. Peanut farmers in the United States gained help from that law. Higher payments were required of other countries. Selling goods inside the United States was the act that cost more.

9Peanuts were among those goods. Members of Congress appear with Carver as he speaks. Fame grew large around him. Crowds came to hear the talks. Money in large amounts was within his reach. Helping people mattered more to him. Free ideas were his belief. Giving those ideas freely was his belief too. A meeting with a president happened around 1936. Franklin Delano Roosevelt was that president. A gift of money came from Carver near the end of his life. The Tuskegee Institute received 33,000 dollars. Research he had started was meant to continue because of the gift. About 450,000 dollars is what that sum would be today. Death came in 1943. Seventy-eight was his age. Respect between people was his belief about how to treat one another.

10A whole country came to respect him. Memory of his important work remains. The memory outlived him. Enslaved describes a person held in slavery. Ownership by another person was part of it. Forced work for that person was part of it. Famous means lots of people know the name. Invented means a new thing was brought into being. Design can be part of that act. Building can be part of it. A professor teaches at a college or a university. Segregated means groups were held apart. Race is one difference used to separate them. Soil is ground's top layer. Growth of plants happens there. Dirt is another name for it.

22. Getting to Know Argentina

1A boy from Argentina stands beside his llama. Argentina ranks second in size among the countries of South America. Worldwide, its size rank is eighth. The land measures 2,736,690 square kilometers. The same area is 1,056,642 square miles. The Andes Mountains stand on one side of Argentina. The Atlantic Ocean lies on the other side. No mountain chain on Earth is longer than the Andes. Five countries share a border with Argentina. Brazil is one of them. Uruguay is one of them. Paraguay is one of them. Bolivia is one of them. Chile is one of them. Argentina is split into twenty-three provinces. A government of its own belongs to each province. Laws of its own belong to each province. National laws of Argentina still bind every province. A map places Buenos Aires on the Atlantic side. The map also sets Argentina in South America, with Bolivia, Paraguay, Chile, Brazil, and Uruguay nearby. Argentina's capital is Buenos Aires. The city is also the largest.

2More than fifteen million people make up its population. Fair winds is what the name Buenos Aires means. No street anywhere is wider than the one in this city. Avenida 9 de Julio is the street's name. July 9th Avenue is the name in English. The date is the day Argentina became free from Spain. Friendly people are part of the city's fame. Many of them arrived from Europe. Others arrived from places across the world. Because of that mix, this capital looks more like a European city than any other capital in South America. An obelisk is the name of a monument in Buenos Aires. It rises in the center of that record-wide street. Soybeans are the crop farmers are shown harvesting. Argentina ranks with the biggest food-producing countries on Earth. More than forty-three million is the number of people who live there. About nine in every ten live in a large city or close to one. Latin America ranks Argentina third for the money the country makes.

3Brazil stands ahead of it. Mexico stands ahead of it too. Gold is mined in the mountains. Gold is mined in the rivers. Silver is mined in those places too. Beans are a crop farmers grow. Lemons are a crop they grow. Grapes are a crop they grow. Wheat is a crop they grow. Part of the harvest stays for use at home. Part of the harvest is sent abroad. Service jobs are the work of most people in Argentina. Automobiles are made by others. Textiles are made by others. Energy is made by others. Movie jobs are held by some people. About eighty films are made there each year. Argentina's main religion is the Catholic faith. The Muslim faith is practiced as well. The Jewish faith is practiced as well.
