The Protestant faith is practiced as well. The Mormon faith is practiced as well. A melting pot is what people call Argentina.

4Different kinds of people joined and formed a new nation. People from other countries have always been welcomed. Parents from Europe are part of many families there. Grandparents from Europe are part of many families too. Spanish is Argentina's main language. Family members from a mix of countries are common. Other languages are spoken by some people because of those family ties. Villa General Belgrano shows buildings in a European style. The district sits in the province of Córdoba. A long triangle is the shape of Argentina. Mountains cover part of the land. Deserts cover part of it. Plains cover part of it. Lakes cover part of it. Five areas make up its geography. The Andes Mountains lie between Argentina and Chile. Food comes from the Pampas. Beauty is what Patagonia is known for. Jungles and swamps fill the Littoral. A lowland in the northwest is called the Gran Chaco. Many kinds of birds live in Argentina. Many kinds of mammals live there too.

5Penguins are among the animals. Tapirs are among the animals. Howler monkeys are among the animals. Jaguars are among the animals. Iguazú Falls appears on a map of the land. The Iguazú River appears on it. The Paraná River appears on it. Mount Aconcagua appears on it. Iguazú Falls ranks among Argentina's most beautiful places. Millions of years ago is when the falls took shape. They stand among the tallest waterfalls in the world. They stand among the widest waterfalls too. Big water is what the word Iguazú means. The Andes Mountains offer another beautiful sight. Western South America is lined by this range for its full length. About 7,242 kilometers is the length of the Andes. About 4,500 miles is that same length. Seven countries are crossed by the range. It is Earth's longest chain of mountains. Iguazú Falls is counted among seven great natural wonders worldwide. Mount Aconcagua rises inside the Andes. It is the highest peak anywhere in the Americas.

6Argentina's past stretches a long way back. Trouble fills much of that past. Native peoples lived there for thousands of years with no one disturbing them. Change arrived when the Spanish invaded. The 1500s were the years when they took control. Defending the land cost many native people their lives. The year 1816 finally ended Spanish rule. Argentina then aimed to stand as its own country. Rules for the new government were written in 1853. The choice of Buenos Aires as capital came in 1880. Golden Age is the name given to the years 1880 to 1914. Money was plentiful in Argentina then. Roads were built with the money. Railroads were built with the money. Parks were built with the money. Money trouble reached the government in 1929. Banks failed in countries around the world. Army leaders then began to run Argentina. Their hold on power lasted from 1929 to 1943. Juan Perón later helped seize the government.

7Help for working people made him well known. Help for poor people made him well known too. Voters chose Juan Perón as president in 1946. People liked Juan. People liked his wife, Eva, too. Juan's power grew too large. Most followers then turned away from him. His second term as president was underway in 1955. He fled to Spain that year. Poor people liked Juan very much. They liked Eva very much too. Evita is the other name Eva is known by. She worked hard so that women could win the vote. The year 1973 was when Perón came back. He entered the race for president another time. The voters chose him. Death came the next year. Many Argentines still remember Juan and Eva Perón. They remember the pair as leaders who gave the country help. Government trouble lasted a long time after his rule. More people began to join in government during the early twenty-first century. The leaders they picked saw things the same way they did.

8The past was hard. Hope for tomorrow is what Argentina's people hold now. Festivals bring the country to life every year. Carnival is one festival. Huge floats are built for it. Color covers the floats. The floats parade along the street. Musicians take joy in the event. Dancers take joy in it. Other people take joy in it too. Some people dress as clowns for fun. Some dress as animals. Some dress as cartoon characters. Another loved event is a gaucho gathering. It is held at San Antonio de Areco. People know it as the Gaucho Festival. Gauchos display horse skills during the festival. Ten days in November are filled by the celebration. Bronco riding is included. Dancing is included. Parades are included. July 9 is another major day. It marks when Argentina split away from Spain. Food is a common part of the celebration. Parades are a common part too. A gaucho is a South American cowboy.

9Horses are a source of pride for gauchos. One saying compares a gaucho who has no horse to a man who has no feet. Empanadas differ from one region of Argentina to another. A picnic shows a family grilling meat inside an open pit. Cooking in Argentina was shaped by people who moved there from Spain. People from France shaped it too. People from Italy shaped it too. Foods from all three cultures are popular in Argentina today. Steak is often grilled over an open pit. A barbecue is another way the steak is cooked. Empanadas are popular as well. Each empanada is a small pie with a tasty filling. Meat can be the filling. Vegetables can be the filling. Cheese can be the filling. Fruit can be the filling. Italian dishes are enjoyed too. Pasta is one of those dishes. Pizza is one of those dishes. A tea called yerba maté is drunk by many people in Argentina.

10Drinkers say the tea gives them energy. They say it helps them stay alert. Night shows the buildings of Buenos Aires glowing in the port's water. Citizens of Argentina come from every part of the world. The past twenty years have brought newcomers from China. Newcomers have come from South Korea. Newcomers have come from other Asian countries too. Argentina is a modern country. Equal rights for everyone are what it believes in. It wants citizens to share in government. Protecting the land is understood to be important. History included unrest. Great hope for the future is what the people hold. A population of 43,646,358 is on record. Land area is listed as 2,736,690 square kilometers. The matching area is 1,056,642 square miles. Buenos Aires is listed as the capital city. Spanish is listed as the primary language. Roman Catholic is listed as the primary religion. The currency carries the name Argentine peso. The year of this record is 2016.

23. Gold in the Rivers of California

1A picture shows prospectors at work in California. The picture is from around 1852. This book tells how a secret was kept. It tells how miners used pans. It tells about the forty-niners. It tells about travel by ship and travel by wagon. It asks whether the chance to find gold was equal for everyone. It tells why California is called the Golden State. A word list comes last. Sutter's Mill was a sawmill that ran on water power. At the mill, logs were cut into lumber. Lumber is wood that would be sold. Work filled the morning of January 24, 1848. California was where James Marshall worked at a mill that morning. He spotted something shiny. It lay under the water in a ditch. The shiny thing was gold. Marshall showed the gold to John Sutter. Sutter was the owner of the mill. Sutter wanted the gold find kept quiet by everyone. The secret was that gold had been found. Six days after that find, California joined the United States. A war called the Mexican-American War ended with lost land. A treaty gave that land to the United States. The land makes up the state of California today. It also makes up Nevada and Utah.

2It makes up most of Arizona. It makes up half of New Mexico. It includes parts of Colorado and Wyoming. Many Native peoples made their homes on these lands. Still, Mexico had claimed ownership. After the war, Mexico gave the lands to the United States. A map marks the United States, Sutter's Mill, and San Francisco. The map shows the Pacific Ocean and the Atlantic Ocean. It shows the Rio Grande. It shows Mexico and North America. Sam Brannan owned a store on Sutter's land. He soon let out the secret of the gold. Tools for finding gold were what he sold to miners. The tools were picks, axes, pans, and shovels. He sold those tools at very high prices. John Sutter had moved to California from Switzerland. Mexico controlled California at that time. The Mexican government made a land deal with Sutter. The deal let him control a large amount of land. That land actually belonged to Native peoples. Some Native people were put into an army that belonged to Sutter. He enslaved many others. Those enslaved people had to work the land. California's gold was unlike gold in most places. It was not locked deep under the ground.

3Placer mining was one way to get it. Riverbed gold was sifted out by the miners. They used a shallow, flat pan. Prospectors stood in rivers. They shoveled gravel into the pan. They took out the large rocks. Then they carefully swirled the pan. Dirt spilled over the edges with the water. Gravel spilled over too. Shiny gold flakes remained on the bottom of the pan. At the time, most miners found only a little gold. The gold was worth between 10 and 15 dollars in today's money. A prospector is a person who moves to a chosen spot. The prospect, or possibility, is that gold may be found there. Gold fever took hold of many people. Gold fever means greed and excitement. The discovery of gold brought that feeling on. Many workers then earned under one dollar for a day's work. In 1849, a huge crowd joined the search. Around ninety thousand people came. People called them forty-niners. People traveled from Mexico. People traveled from countries in Central America. People traveled from countries in South America. Enslavers brought some Black people who were held in slavery. Free Black people also came. A picture from 1852 shows a gold miner. The miner shovels gravel in California.

4Months could pass on the journey to the gold mines. The trip was not cheap. Travel to the mines had to be paid for. They had to pay for food during travel. Quick money was the plan for many forty-niners. Then they planned to return home. Others were ready for adventure. People already in California were among the first to arrive. People from Oregon were among the first. People from Mexico were among the first too. They came to the rivers near Sutter's Mill. Only pack animals had to be taken by the locals. Immigrants needed more time. They arrived by boat. Asia, Australia, and South America were where they came from. Ships were the choice for about half of the forty-niners. Crossing Native peoples' lands was what they hoped to avoid. A ship from the East Coast took five to eight months. The trip had to go around South America's tip. Then it went up the coast to California. The cheapest ticket was 75 dollars. That price is about 2,300 dollars today. The journey was difficult. People got sick. People were bored. Posters advertised the ships. Forty-niners could sail from New York City to San Francisco on those ships.

5A map shows the routes the forty-niners took. It marks North America and South America. It marks Saint Louis, Missouri, and New York City. It marks the Atlantic Ocean and the Pacific Ocean. It marks Sacramento, San Francisco, and Panama. It marks Cape Horn. One route went over land and took five to eight months. The Panama route took one month. That short time worked only if a ship was waiting on the Pacific side. The Cape Horn route took six to eight months. A scale on the map counts 0, 500, 1,000, 1,500, and 2,000. The scale is shown in kilometers and in miles. Some passengers cut through Panama. They traveled across 60 miles of jungle. That distance is 97 kilometers. Then they got on another ship. Bad weather made the shortcut difficult. Wild animals made it difficult too. Many people fell ill. Many people died. A person who made it across still had to wait. Waiting for a ship on the far side could take weeks. It might take months. Crossing the United States by wagon took about the same amount of time. Much of the land on that path still belonged to Native peoples. The land lay between the East Coast forty-niners and California.

6Theft from Native communities was common among settlers heading west. They often attacked those communities too. People on the move appear in a drawing made around 1900. They traveled together in a group of wagons. That group was called a train. Most people left from Saint Louis, Missouri. At the time, it was the most western city in the United States. Wagons followed the California Trail. The trail ran 2,100 miles. That distance is 3,380 kilometers. The trail crossed the flat prairie. In one day, a wagon could cover about 15 miles. That distance is 24 kilometers. Rivers greeted the travelers. Mountains, cliffs, and deserts greeted them too. So did rain, wind, and snow. Keeping the oxen healthy was difficult. Keeping the wagons working was difficult too. Even worse dangers came from sicknesses. Hunger was another danger. Accidents were another danger. Life did not get easier for those who made it to California. Giving up was hard for the miners. Gold might be waiting in the very next pan of gravel. A photo from around 1880 shows a group of California gold miners. They stand together for the picture. Filled hills of prospectors brought harsher treatment of other people.

7Other groups were treated badly by white miners. Native peoples in California belonged to many different groups. Forty-niner attacks pushed Native peoples away from their land. The year 1850 is when California turned into a state. Even more Native land was taken by the government after statehood. The killing took thousands of Native peoples. Thousands more died of sickness. New laws let settlers arrest Native people. The arrest was for breaking the law. Settlers then forced them to pay fines. The fines were paid by working on ranches. A picture from around 1850 shows Chinese miners. They are searching for gold in California. White prospectors hoped for the smallest amount of competition. A tax was used to make miners from other countries leave California. The force was a tax. Miners from China were among the people who were taxed. A picture shows gold miners in their camp. The camp was at El Dorado, California, around 1850. Finding gold was not easy. Even people who found gold often lost it. Robbers were common. Buying goods took a lot of money. Some early prospectors got rich. Deeper gold was a different job. Deeper gold was mined mostly by large companies. Those companies were the most successful.

8Forty-niners in 1849 San Francisco could buy what they wanted. They could buy it only if they could pay. One can of peas cost 6 dollars in 1849. In 2021 money, that can would cost 206 dollars. One egg cost 1 dollar in 1849. In 2021 money, that egg would cost 34 dollars. One breakfast cost 43 dollars in 1849. In 2021 money, that breakfast would cost 1,479 dollars. One pound of coffee cost 3 dollars in 1849. A pound is 0.45 kilograms. In 2021 money, that coffee would cost 103 dollars. One mule cost 360 dollars in 1849. In 2021 money, that mule would cost 12,384 dollars. One pair of boots cost 96 dollars in 1849. In 2021 money, those boots would cost 3,302 dollars. The 2021 numbers show the old prices after inflation. Edward Dolnick listed these prices in a book. Edward Dolnick's book gives these prices. The short title is The Rush. The rest of the title is America's Fevered Quest for Fortune. The years on the book are 1848 to 1853. Some people got rich without mining. They got rich by selling goods to the miners. Sam Brannan was among the earliest millionaires in the United States.

9Levi Strauss sold pants. The pants were tough enough to handle the work. Those pants were blue jeans. Women started businesses. Some cooked. Some ran boarding houses. Some washed clothes. Some of the earliest jeans offered for sale appear in a 1905 ad. Mary Ellen Pleasant was a businesswoman and a Black American. During the Gold Rush, she worked as a cook. She also worked as a housekeeper. While she worked, she listened. She heard what businesspeople were talking about. She became a millionaire. She bought and sold buildings. She bought and sold businesses. One picture shows San Francisco in 1851. Another picture shows San Francisco today. White settlers gained success from the Gold Rush. It brought success to the government of the United States. It brought success to rich people. Harm also came from the Gold Rush, and it came in many ways. Around three hundred thousand settlers were brought in. Most of those settlers were white. They came to lands in California that had been Native lands. The number of Native peoples who had died was more than one hundred thousand. That number of deaths was counted twenty years after the rush started. Permanent change came to the western United States because of the Gold Rush.

10No other state today has as many people as California. The other forty-nine states each have fewer people. Nearly two centuries have passed since the Gold Rush. Its nickname is still a true fit for California. The nickname is the Golden State. Boarding houses are houses where people paid a fee. The fee let them stay and eat meals. A ditch is a low, narrow area. A road or a field often has this low area along its side. Enslavers are people who put others in slavery. They also keep others in slavery. Being owned, or being forced to work, is the state called slavery. Gravel is a loose mixture of rock fragments. Greed is a selfish and intense desire. The desire is for more of something than is needed. A new country is the place immigrants come to live. They come especially for the purpose of settling there. Settlers are people who make a new, permanent home. That home is on a frontier. This kind of path can make the trip shorter. It can also be an easier way. Taxing means collecting a fee. The fee pays for government services, operations, or functions.

24. Harriet Tubman and the Hidden Road to Freedom

1Contents. Born into Slavery. Dreams of Escape. Leading Others to Freedom. How She Did It. The End of Slavery. Word Meanings. People held in slavery were sold at auctions. Those auctions were humiliating. Born into Slavery. Kidnappers took her great-grandmother well before Harriet Tubman was born. They seized her from her home in Africa. They packed her into a small wooden ship. Hundreds of other Africans were packed in too. The ship carried her to the United States. Traders had enslaved her. When she arrived, those traders sold her. The buyer owned a large farm. That kind of farm is called a plantation. Harriet's great-grandmother was now human property. She had to give the name master to the man who owned her. All her children were enslaved. All her grandchildren were enslaved too. Enslavers forced them into hard labor. Sometimes the punishment was cruel. They got no pay. They got only meager food. Their housing was poor. It was no better than housing for farm animals. Many of them never learned to read. They never learned to write. Enslaved people had no freedom of movement.

2They could not go where they wished. Sometimes marriage was not allowed. Sometimes they could not raise their own children. A family member might be sold to another enslaver. That sale broke the family apart. Whole families of enslaved people had to work. The work brought no pay. Dreams of Escape. Harriet was born around 1820. Running was not allowed for her. Playing was not allowed for her. Every enslaved child lived with those same limits. She worked all the time. Even then, her enslavers sometimes whipped her. She saw two sisters sold. Harriet was terrified. She feared she would be sold next. A sale would force her to leave her family. The picture shows Harriet Tubman around this age. Harriet's life was harsh. Even so, she had dreams. People known as abolitionists began to speak out. They spoke against a deep injustice. The injustice was owning other human beings. Escape for enslaved people came through help from some abolitionists. Some paths led to Northern states. Some paths led to Canada. Slavery was illegal in those places.

3Harriet dreamed of a free life. She wanted that life in the North, as a free woman. Enslaved people tried daring plans to reach freedom. Some had themselves shipped north inside boxes. One man was Henry Box Brown. He nearly died while escaping. His box was turned upside down. It stayed that way for hours. After twenty-six hours in the box, Brown reached Pennsylvania. Pennsylvania was a free state. He reached freedom there. The picture shows Henry Box Brown. The Crafts were one family among many. Clever disguises helped such families escape. Ellen Craft was enslaved, and her skin was light. She disguised herself as a white man. She made the man seem sick. An enslaved man traveled with her. He was her husband, William Craft. The Crafts rode trains. They also rode steamships. Philadelphia was the city they entered on December 25, 1848. Harriet was still a girl when she tried to help. An enslaved man was trying to escape. The metal weight came from the overseer's throw. He wanted the weight to stop the man.

4The weight hit Harriet in the head. She almost died from the wound. Headaches lasted the rest of her life. Seizures lasted too. The injury caused both. A large scar on the head was left by that weight. Years later, Tubman planned her own escape. She wanted her husband to come along. He was a free Black man. He refused. Tubman stayed determined. She would become a free woman. One day the man who enslaved her died. She knew a sale would follow. People he had enslaved would be sold. It was time to leave. She ran with two of her brothers. Soon after they left, the brothers turned back. Tubman did not turn back. Tubman kept going. Her brothers did not. Help from abolitionists let Tubman escape toward the north. An abolitionist woman opened her home to Tubman. The woman had offered help. The woman gave her food. The woman let her sleep. After that, the woman directed her onward to another safe place. At that house, people sent her onward again. These safe homes formed a secret network.

5People called it the Underground Railroad. On the escape, Tubman walked 100 miles, or 160 km. She walked alone. The land was unknown to her. She traveled at night. She hid in the daytime. At last she reached the border of Pennsylvania. She was free. Leading Others to Freedom. Reaching freedom filled Tubman with joy. She later described that day. She remembered glory spread over all she could see. In her telling, the sun looked like gold as it passed through the trees. Tubman went to work. She cooked meals. She washed dishes. She cleaned houses. Because she was free, people paid her. Freedom by itself was not enough for her. Saving was what she did with her pay. She planned to use it to help free others. She worked hard so other people could be free too. Tubman returned to the South on nineteen trips. Each trip was perilous. She put her own danger aside. She became a conductor on the Underground Railroad. Escapees went with her, safe rest by safe rest. A station was usually the home of an abolitionist.

6Sometimes a station was a church. Sometimes it was another safe resting place. Some stations had secret rooms. The rooms were for hiding escapees. Some trips used wagons with false bottoms. A false bottom made a secret compartment. The compartment hid the people inside. Tubman led escapees to safe houses. The Fugitive Slave Act became law in the United States in 1850. The act was a law about the Northern states. Those states had to return escapees. Escapees had to go back to their enslavers. Tubman then led her passengers farther. They had to reach Canada before they found freedom. Do You Know? Code words were part of how people on the Underground Railroad spoke. Freight meant escapees. A station was a safe house, or another place to stay. A conductor was a person who helped. That person helped enslaved people escape. A line was a route. The route ran north to freedom. After the Fugitive Slave Act, the nearest safe place was Canada. It was the nearest safe place for escapees. Tubman often wore the clothes of a man.

7The clothes were a disguise. How She Did It. Many people called her a master of disguise. An old woman was a disguise she used at times. Sometimes she dressed as a man. One story says she met a former enslaver. He did not recognize her. She was clever in other ways too. She paid men to tear down wanted posters. The posters described escapees. Newspapers did not print runaway notices on weekends. The notices had to wait for Monday morning. She started escapes on Saturday night. That start gave her a head start. If she saw people who seemed to be hunting escapees, she turned. She walked south. The turn made escape look unlikely. A wish to go back was met with her refusal. She made the group continue forward. The direction was north, toward freedom. Her proud claim was that no passenger was lost. Enslavers were furious. They offered a reward for capturing her. The reward applied if she was dead. It applied if she was alive. The reward was $40,000. For that time, the amount was enormous.

8One day she heard a man read her own wanted poster. The poster said she was not able to read. At once she took out a book. She pretended to read it. The man left. He did not give her a second look. A huge reward hung over her. Even so, no one turned Tubman in. Why might that be? Many memorials honor Harriet Tubman. This is one of them. Many of her relatives got out of the South through her help. Six escaped from the ten brothers and sisters she had. On one difficult journey, she guided her elderly parents. She took them north to Canada. About three hundred enslaved people gained freedom on the trips she led. The path went north. Helping other people gain freedom came before everything else for her. John Brown was a famous abolitionist. He placed Harriet Tubman among the bravest people on this continent. The End of Slavery. The Civil War began on April 12, 1861. The North fought the South. Their fight was about whether people could be held in slavery.

9Nursing was Tubman's work while the war went on. She served the Union Army of the North. She also served as a spy. She scouted warehouses of the Southern army. Those warehouses held weapons. She still traveled into the South. She still led enslaved people north. She led Union soldiers to stores of arms. She led them to stores of ammunition. Do You Know? How many enslaved people got free by the Underground Railroad? No one knows. Many did reach the North. They became free. Some did not. Many escapees were discovered. They were captured. They were sent back to their enslavers. Others died while they tried to flee. The exact count of successes is unknown. Secrecy was terribly important. The year 1865 was when the Civil War came to an end. Slavery ended in that year too. Tubman married again. She lived in Auburn, New York. She sold vegetables from door to door. She had very little money. Even then, that house of hers was opened as a home. Freed Black people who needed help could live there.

10She worked for their aid. She worked for their education. She also fought for women to have the vote. Harriet Tubman died during her nineties. The day was March 10, 1913. She gave her long life to freedom. People will keep her in memory as a true hero. The Harriet Tubman Home took in elderly people. Those people had once been enslaved. Word Meanings. abolitionists: people whose fight was to have slavery made illegal. compartments: separate sections. They are parts of a larger thing. enslaved: kept in slavery. It means a person is owned by someone else. It can also mean a person is made to work for someone else. escapees: people who broke free. meager: very little. perilous: full of danger. plantation: a big farm with a wealthy owner. The owner may be one person or a family. Other people do the work. scouting: exploring an area in order to learn about it. Underground Railroad: safe places and houses linked in secret. It gave enslaved people a way out. The way led north to freedom.

25. Helpers Who Run the Country

1A worker is shown at the Treasury Department. The founding fathers are shown writing the Declaration of Independence. At the start, the United States was already home to many people. The count was more than two million. The men who founded the country saw a limit. One person could not handle a job that large. They invented a system of help. Experts would join the president in running the country. American leaders gave their approval in 1788. The document they approved is the United States Constitution. It explains how the country ought to be run.

2It is a written set of laws. Government is split into three branches. A branch is one part of that government. Lawmaking belongs to the legislative branch. Fairness of the laws belongs to the judicial branch. A chart can lay out the three branches. Carrying out the laws belongs to the executive branch. The president leads that branch. The vice president belongs to it. The cabinet belongs to it too. The Senate sits in the legislative branch. The House of Representatives sits there too. The Supreme Court sits in the judicial branch.

3Other federal courts sit there too. The president must follow the laws. The president must also run the country. Leaders made a plan for helpers. The president would be allowed to choose them. Helpers would make the daily work lighter. Cabinet members may be chosen by the president. The Constitution does not demand that those choices be made. A picture shows George Washington beside the first cabinet. He served as president. Henry Knox is in the group. Knox took the War role. Alexander Hamilton is in the group.

4Hamilton took the Treasury role. Thomas Jefferson took the State role. Edmund Randolph took the role of Attorney General. Approval for this helper plan came in 1788. Four people could be chosen by the president. Each one would help him. The four together were the cabinet. Spending would be one helper's task. Other countries would be another helper's task. Federal law would be another helper's task. That person made sure the states obeyed it. That person made sure the people obeyed it too. The military would be the last helper's task.

5The first person to be president was George Washington. He knew each cabinet choice had to be careful. He picked four helpers. He believed they would serve well. The group had to pull together. They had to follow the directions he gave. Thomas Jefferson served in that first cabinet. Alexander Hamilton served in it too. The two men clashed on many questions. Offices of the State Department fill a large building. The building carries the name Harry S. Truman Federal Building. The nation has grown since the cabinet of George Washington.

6A president today needs still more help. Cabinet departments are more numerous now. Each department holds even more workers. The workers each have a special task. Helpers in the Department of State deal with other countries. They keep offices far from home. Those offices sit in more than 190 countries. The Department of Defense works inside the Pentagon. Its helpers look after the United States Army. They look after the Navy. They look after the Marines. They look after the Air Force. Their work is to protect the country.

7Money is the charge of the Treasury Department. On a single day, it may turn out a huge value in paper money. The value can reach as much as $560 million. The helpers print fresh paper money. They produce coins. They keep watch on the nation's banks. Gold is kept at Fort Knox. The site is in Kentucky. Fort Knox belongs to the Treasury Department. The Department of Justice is a very large workplace. More than 114,000 people work there. Those helpers make sure federal law is obeyed.

8Public lands fall to Interior. Park care is one task for the people who work there. They also handle natural resources that matter. Mapping the country's lands has been part of the work. That map work dates to 1849. The Coast Guard belongs to a different department. The department is Homeland Security. Ocean waters are part of the Coast Guard's patrol. The seas are part of it too. A United States Coast Guard boat is on patrol in New York Harbor. Keeping people safe is the duty of Homeland Security.

9The helpers work so that American borders stay safe. They work so that the American people stay safe. Other departments exist besides these. Every department has its own special task. The president gives the departments their orders. The departments pass information back to the president. Only four helpers sat in the earliest cabinet. The number today is much higher. The count of helpers has changed. The purpose of the work has stayed the same. They go on serving the president. They go on serving the nation's people. A list shows who takes the lead if a president can no longer serve.

10The vice president stands next. After that comes the Speaker of the House. Next in line is the Senate's President Pro Tempore. Department heads come next in the line. The Secretary of State is the first of those heads. The Secretary of the Treasury comes after that. The Secretary of Defense comes after that. More names follow in order. With the cabinet's help, the president runs the country. The same cabinet fixes this order of who leads next.

26. Hot Springs and Wild Animals

1Yellowstone National Park holds many important sites. Montana, Wyoming, and Idaho all come together there. A map of the park can include Grand Canyon of the Yellowstone. It can include the rim of a volcano. It can include a state border. It can include a park entrance. Yellowstone Lake is marked. Old Faithful is marked. Geysers are marked. Hot springs are marked. Mud pots are marked. Bison are marked. Grizzly bears are marked. Wolves are marked. Steamboat Geyser is one of the sights. A hot spring is one of the sights too. Picture hot water shooting from the ground high into the air. Picture green pools that are beautiful. Picture pots of mud that bubble. The smell of the mud is like rotten eggs.

2Picture grizzly bears, bison, and wolves all seen in a single day. Grand Canyon of the Yellowstone draws more visitors than most other sights in the park. The place you pictured is real. Its name is Yellowstone National Park. A national park is special land kept protected. Yellowstone's land is the meeting point of Wyoming, Montana, and Idaho. In 1872 it was named the first national park in the world. Millions of people come every year to look at its wonders. Old Faithful is the geyser that most guests want to watch. A geyser is a hole that opens in the ground. Steam shoots out of the hole.

3Hot water shoots out of the hole too. The name Old Faithful came from how steady it is. A spray happens about every ninety minutes. The spray happens on every day. Water in the spray rises 100 feet. The rise is about 30 meters. Yellowstone has geysers that are larger than this one. No other geyser there is as regular. An old volcano that has collapsed sits under Yellowstone. Three big eruptions have come from it. A span of two million years holds those eruptions. The rim left by the collapse is huge. In places the rim stretches farther than 45 miles across. That stretch is about 72 kilometers. People still keep a close watch on Yellowstone.

4The volcano does not look ready to erupt again any time soon. A rare hot spot lies beneath Yellowstone. Heat from Earth's core makes the underground rocks super-hot. Rain seeps downward and meets the hot rocks. The rain water heats up. Hot water climbs through cracks in the ground. Steam climbs through those cracks too. At the top, the water and the steam erupt. About three hundred geysers are found in the park. Old Faithful is one of that number. The hot spot also made hot pools filled with bubbling mud. It also made thousands of hot springs full of color. Yellowstone holds the third largest hot spring on Earth. Mud pots take shape in places close to volcanoes.

5The bison is North America's biggest animal on land. Thousands of bison make Yellowstone their home. Visitors see animals in lots of shapes and sizes. Bison are the biggest animals inside the park. A single bison can weigh as much as 2,000 pounds. That weight is about 907 kilograms. These giants wear brown fur. The head on a bison is big. A hump rises from the back. In the summer, bison may roll around in the dust. Dust helps them battle flies. Dust also helps them lose old fur. Grizzly bears are the next animals in size. One bear can weigh as much as 800 pounds. That weight is about 363 kilograms.

6A grizzly is fast when you consider how big it is. Dens under the ground are where grizzly bears sleep. The winter sleep goes on for many months. They wake when spring arrives. They wake up hungry and ready for food. Yellowstone visitors used to offer food to the grizzly bears. That feeding is against the rules now. People can be hurt by it. The bears can be hurt by it too. Wild wolves numbering forty-one were brought into Yellowstone. The move happened from 1995 through 1997. Some of the wolves came from Canada. Some of the wolves came from Montana. Wolves are part of life in the park. Settlers forced the wolves out of Yellowstone.

7The removal was more than 100 years in the past. Workers from the national parks later returned the wolves. The wolves living in the park today number about one hundred. More than 350 further kinds of animals live in the park. Elk roam free across it. Deer roam free across it. Golden eagles roam free across it. Beavers roam free across it. Foxes roam free across it. For these wild animals, Yellowstone is a safe haven. Visitors may enjoy the sight of them. Getting too close is something visitors should never do. Native peoples who lived on this land included the Bannocks. Among national parks in America, Yellowstone is one of the favorites.

8Native peoples used the land in the years before it was a park. They hunted on the land. They fished on the land. They gathered plants on the land. Geysers held an important place in their faith. Settlers painted scenes from the area. They also traveled the land and explored it. The paintings helped convince the United States. The exploring helped convince the country too. Yellowstone was then made the first national park of the country. Explorers made their way through Yellowstone. Wonders were written down in reports. Wonders were saved in photos. Wonders were saved in paintings. Later, land where Native peoples had made their homes was taken by the United States. Yellowstone was formed out of that land.

9New laws said the land could not be owned by any person. The park was kept so that many people could use it. It was kept so the animals would be cared for. This decision gave birth to the national park idea. More than fifty national parks now exist inside the United States. Many other national parks exist in countries around the world. Some of those parks guard natural wonders. Grand Canyon National Park is a park that guards a natural wonder. Other parks guard places people need to remember. Battlefields belong in that second group. Gettysburg National Park keeps the memory of an important battle. Grand Canyon National Park contains a canyon that is among the largest on Earth.

10That same canyon is among the most beautiful. Cool lake water is where a child dips a set of toes. About three million people move through Yellowstone each year. Those travelers come from lands all over the world. Some come in order to fish. Some come in order to hike. Some come in order to camp. Some come in order to watch animals. Some come in order to see the hot springs. The wonders here are rarely seen in any other place. Many people help protect this special area. Many groups help protect it too. Yellowstone will go on being a beautiful place for a visit.

27. Ice at the Bottom of the World

1This book names Antarctica the seventh continent. It tells how freezing the land is. It tells about life on Antarctica and around it. It tells how the land is a playground for scientists. It ends with a conclusion and a word list. A list gives each continent a number. Number 7 is Antarctica. Number 4 is Africa. Number 1 is North America. Number 5 is Asia. Number 2 is South America. Number 6 is Australia. Number 3 is Europe. Give a globe a spin. Six continents are easy to spot on a spinning globe. Africa, Asia, and Australia are three of them. The remaining three are Europe, North America, and South America. Humans have had homes on these lands across many thousands of years. Look next along the lower part of the globe. That lower place is where continent number seven appears. That continent is Antarctica. A picture shows a ship stuck in ice. The crew is trying to free the ship.

2The explorer was the British sailor Ernest Shackleton. The month was February 1915. Antarctica lies far from where anyone lives. Rough seas surround it. Nobody knew for a long time that the continent existed. Stormy waters around Antarctica have been sailed by many explorers. They have done so throughout history. Even so, people did not actually discover Antarctica until around 1820. No one person is given credit for the discovery. Sightings by a few men are claimed. They sighted it within days of each other. A second disagreement among historians is about a different first. Who first walked its land is something historians still dispute. A map of Antarctica shows three oceans. They are the South Atlantic Ocean, the South Pacific Ocean, and the Indian Ocean. The map marks Vinson Massif. Its height is 4,897 meters. That height is 16,066 feet. It is the tallest mountain on Antarctica. The map marks the South Pole.

3The South Pole is Earth's southernmost point. The map marks the Ross Ice Shelf. The shelf is a floating area of ice. The ice is the size of France. The last two hundred years have made the picture much clearer. Extremes are the mark of this place called Antarctica. It is the coldest continent. It is the windiest continent. Less rain and less snow fall here than on any other continent. Wildlife here is also among the most amazing in the world. Some pictures of Antarctica were taken at midnight. They show the Sun during the Antarctic summer. In that season the Sun stays above the horizon the whole time. Away from the Sun is how the southern half is turned. Winter in Antarctica falls in June and July. For a couple of weeks then, the Sun never rises. Summer in Antarctica falls in December and January. For a period then, the Sun never sets.

4Antarctica's location shapes the weather. Cold, ice, and wind are the usual weather. In summer, the temperature most often reaches just above freezing. That mark is 0 degrees Celsius, or 32 degrees Fahrenheit. Winter readings stay almost always under a colder line. That mark is minus 18 degrees Celsius, or 0 degrees Fahrenheit. Place and date both belong to the coldest temperature ever recorded on Earth. The date was July 1983. The place was an Antarctic science station. It got down to minus 89 degrees Celsius. That is minus 128 degrees Fahrenheit. A snowstorm can hide the view. Sky, mountain, and sea can be hard to tell apart. This extreme cold explains the ice. Ice in thick sheets lies over Antarctica. At its deepest, the ice is 4 kilometers thick. That depth is 2.5 miles. All that ice makes another fact surprising. No continent is drier than Antarctica. Yearly rain and snow together stay under 5 centimeters.

5That amount is 2 inches. Water covers most of Earth. Most of the water holds salt. Frozen water holds most of the fresh water on Earth. It is frozen in Antarctica's ice. Snow does not melt away during the cold Antarctic summers. Because of that, ice has been building up. The buildup has lasted thousands of years. Icebergs are big chunks that break from the ice. They break off and float out to sea. People also call them ice mountains. Emperor penguins live in Antarctica in huge numbers. More than a million of them live there. The bitter weather has a hard effect on life. It has made Antarctica a dangerous habitat. Life finds it hard to survive there. The continent has no trees. Only low plants grow there. Moss is one of those low plants. A single native insect lives on the continent. That insect is the tiny Antarctic midge. Almost all plant life on Antarctica has one address.

6Almost all animal life has the same address. That place is near the coasts or in the sea. The emperor penguin is one creature of the icy water. It is the largest penguin in the world. Height for this bird is more than 1.2 meters. That height is 4 feet. Leopard seals are giant. They spend most of their time alone. A picture shows one leopard seal resting. It yawns on a chunk of ice. Leopard seals are fast. They are fierce predators. They hunt penguins. They hunt other prey. The hunt happens in the open water. Among seals, the leopard seal has a size rank. Among seals, this kind ranks second in size in the world. A leopard seal can grow more than 3.3 meters long. That length is 11 feet. It can weigh up to 590 kilograms. That weight is 1,300 pounds. Caring for a baby penguin is a large task for the male emperor penguin.

7It stands on the ice. It holds an egg between its feet and its body. It does this for two months. The male does not eat for this entire period. It loses almost half its weight. Sometimes the chick hatches early. The father remains if the chick comes out before the mother is back. He stays with the chick. Food is what she brings when she returns. The chick gets fish from her. The father can finally leave. He goes to the sea to eat. Blizzards often trap scientists indoors. The station is Australia's Mawson Station. The scientists pass the time in a few ways. They use the indoor climbing wall. They watch movies. They read. Protection of the wildlife and the land has taken hard work from people worldwide. They protect Antarctica's wildlife. They protect its land. Thirty countries have research bases on Antarctica. The number of bases is about seventy.

8Some bases are open year-round. Others are open only in summer. Antarctica is not under the control of any one country. Many countries put their names on an agreement in 1961. The agreement is called a treaty. The treaty makes sure no wars happen in Antarctica. It also makes sure scientists can study there in peace. Wildlife study is the reason some scientists come. They study life on land. They study life in the water. For example, some scientists keep records about penguins. They record what penguins eat. They record how penguins behave. Other work looks deep into the ice. Scientists drill into the ice for a weather record. They want to know what the weather was like long ago. Antarctica's ice is studied by other scientists in further ways. They measure how the ice changes. Long tubes of ice are drilled out and pulled up. The tubes are called cores. Bubbles of air are trapped in these cores.

9The bubbles can inform scientists about old air. Old air in Antarctica is what the bubbles can reveal. Antarctica has giant ice shelves. An ice shelf is a floating piece of ice attached to land. Some of those shelves have been dropping into the ocean. Scientists are studying melting ice. They study the melting in Antarctica. They study melting in other places too. The melting may cause problems around the world someday. Tourists usually visit in the Antarctic summer. That season runs between November and March. Tourist numbers each year reach into the thousands. They come in addition to the scientists. Most tourists arrive on ships. They come to experience a faraway place. Planet Earth has few places more remote than Antarctica. Clouds of many colors fill the sky over Antarctica. Antarctica remains hard to know. Few lands on Earth are less known than this one. The cold makes exploration difficult. The ice makes exploration difficult.

10The rough seas make exploration difficult. Study is difficult for the same reasons. However, the land has uncommon beauty. It offers chances to study an extreme habitat. Continents are the main sections of land on Earth. The list includes Africa, South America, Asia, and Europe. It also includes North America, Australia, and Antarctica. Extremes are conditions far beyond what is normal. They are also beyond what is expected. A globe is a ball-shaped map of the world. A habitat is the natural setting of a plant or an animal. Predators are animals that hunt other animals. They eat the animals they hunt. Prey are animals that are hunted. They are eaten by other animals. Protect means to guard. It means to keep something from harm or danger. Research is a detailed study of a subject. The study is especially to discover new information. It can also find facts. Survive means to stay alive.

28. Ice on the Move

1Glaciers look like massive rivers made of ice. The Earth's surface is always changing. Wind wears mountains down. Water wears mountains down. Heat wears mountains down. Stone can crack from these forces. Glaciers bring some of the changes that stand out the most. Ice and rock form these giant pieces. Glaciers cross the land at a slow pace. Cliffs are carved as they pass. Valleys are carved as they pass. Prairies are carved as they pass. Lakes appear where glaciers melt. Rivers appear there too. Hills of soil and boulders appear there too. Continental glaciers are one common kind. Valley glaciers are the other common kind.

2Continental glaciers take shape at Earth's north pole. They also take shape at the south pole. Vast areas of land lie beneath these huge ice sheets. The ice can grow so thick that mountain tops are all that stick out. Continental glaciers bury much of Greenland. They bury much of Antarctica as well. Almost the whole of Antarctica lies under one huge continental glacier. Valley glaciers begin on high mountains. Those mountains climb past the snow line. Above that line, snow stays on the ground through every season. A tropical climate can still grow valley glaciers when the peaks are tall enough.

3A long, narrow valley is the path a valley glacier follows. The glacier moves as a river of ice would move. Austria's Pasterze Glacier is a valley glacier. Glaciers can grow on high mountains that never lose their snow. Some places stay below freezing for most months of the year. Snow stacks up much faster than melting can clear it. Every new layer presses the snow that lies under it. The lower snow gets squashed, which is also called compressed. Compressed snowflakes turn into ice crystals. Those crystals are called firn. More falling snow presses the firn crystals together.

4In time the firn becomes one huge slab of ice. A glacier is the name for that slab. The change can be shown in three steps. The steps are snow, firn, and solid ice. First the snow stacks up. Next its weight squashes whatever is underneath. Last of all, the pressed snow is solid ice. A glacier in Alaska flows down its slope. Movement can begin once the ice is about 30 meters thick. That thickness equals about 100 feet. A building of 12 stories is about the same height. Gravity tugs the heavy glacier down the slope. The name of this pulling force is gravity.

5The pull is aimed at the center of the Earth. Objects fall because of this force. Objects roll down slopes because of this force. Flow is the name for how a glacier moves. Ice on top is heavy. That heavy ice compresses the ice beneath it. Tight pressure can melt ice even when the ice sits above the snow line. Melted ice is slippery. The slip lets the glacier slide. At times the upper ice moves ahead of the lower ice. Cracks open in the ice. Crevasse is the name for a crack in glacier ice.

6A crevasse may reach hundreds of meters down. A hiker may stand at the edge of one deep crevasse. The coasts are where continental glaciers head. Downhill is where valley glaciers head. Continental glaciers take shape in the middle of continents. From that middle, the ice flows out toward the coasts. Valley glaciers travel down mountain slopes. In a single day, a fast glacier can move 20 meters. That distance is about 70 feet. Motion that quick is very rare. Most glaciers cover only a short distance in a day. The distance may be only a few centimeters. A few centimeters measures less than 1 inch.

7The daily distance can also reach 30 centimeters. That measure is about 1 foot. Rocks in Ohio still show deep scars cut by glaciers. A sliding glacier pushes rocks and soil into piles ahead of it. Moraines is the name for those piles. Some rocks and soil get trapped inside the ice. Trapped rocks scrape the land under the glacier. Huge scars are left by the scraping. Some glaciers scrape a whole mountainside clean. Valley glaciers cut deep valleys that have a U shape. Steep cliffs stand along those valleys. Once a glacier passes below the snow line, its bottom starts to melt.

8Meltwater from the bottom forms a river. Certain glaciers cut valleys that run onward until they meet the sea. Seawater then fills the valleys. A valley filled this way is called a fjord. Continental glaciers leave the land flat. The sides of a fjord are very steep. Icebergs are chunks that break from a glacier and drop into the sea. At the ocean, a glacier crumbles apart. It slides down into the water. Now and then a big chunk of ice falls free. Any piece that breaks away is an iceberg. Some icebergs floating in the Antarctic Ocean reach nearly 80 kilometers in length.

9A length of 80 kilometers is about 50 miles. Recede is another word for melt. Some glaciers recede until they disappear. Warmer weather can make a glacier recede. Less snow can make it recede too. A moraine stays in place after the glacier recedes. The leftover moraine is a long ridge built of rocks and soil. Till is the name of this soil. Till can be useful ground for growing food. Huge boulders may drop out of a glacier as it melts. Lakes can appear where ice melts on the land. Ponds can appear there too.

10Glaciers dropped the big boulders seen in some places. One glacier has already receded and left its moraine behind. Glaciers help give the Earth shapes that are beautiful. They help give the Earth shapes that are interesting. Many glaciers have receded across the last 100 years. The Earth's temperature may be on the rise. Scientists watch glaciers to learn what temperature changes do to them. These enormous rivers of ice have a lot to teach about our planet. Glacier locations around the world can be shown on a map. White is the color used to mark those places.
