30. Itchy Guests on Human Heads

1This book begins with the parts inside it. One part asks which people get head lice. One part tells the right name for the insect. One part tells how head lice reach a new person. One part follows the life of a head louse. One part tells how to send the bugs away. One part tells the truth about head lice. The last part is a list of word meanings. Head lice like to feed on heads. They feed on the heads of children. They feed on the heads of adults too. These insects suck blood. They choose a child's head more often than an adult's head. They can cause a great amount of trouble. People have a special word when they see more than one mouse. They do not say mouses. They say mice. The bloodsucking insect in this book works the same way. One insect is a louse. Two or more insects are lice. Head lice have bothered people for many centuries. They have been around for a very long time. Even so, many people do not understand what they are.

2Many people do not understand where they come from. A filthy home is what some people blame. Other people believe lice come only to hair that is washed too little. In fact, lice do not care how clean a home is. They do not care how clean a school is. How often you wash your hair does not bring them. How often you brush your hair does not bring them. The length of your hair does not bring them either. People with a lot of money still get these insects. They affect people who do not have a lot of money. City life does not keep these insects away. Country life does not keep them away either. Many kinds of people around the globe get them. The two kinds can appear to be a matching pair. The two kinds are quite different. Body lice spread dangerous sickness. Head lice do not spread sickness. Body lice live in clothing. They live especially in the spots where clothing touches the skin. Head lice live on human heads.

3They live in human hair. About one day is all an adult body louse lasts when people are absent. A young body louse dies even more quickly. A few days without a person may still leave a head louse alive. Body lice are most common when people cannot keep clothes clean. They are also most common when people cannot keep their bodies clean. Head lice can show up on a clean body. They can show up when clothes are dirty. They can also show up when clothes are clean. More than one kind of louse lives on people. Every type feeds on human blood. The two kinds are nearly identical in looks. Head lice are slightly smaller. An adult measures about 1/8 inch from the head end to the tail end. That length is about 2 to 3 millimeters. A pencil point matches that size. Such a small size makes a head louse tough to spot. The same small size makes it tougher to catch. A picture sets a match head next to a head louse. The picture lets you compare their size.

4Head lice are insects, like all insects. They have six legs. They have three body parts. Strong claws grow on those legs. The claws help the louse hold tight to human hair. That tight hold makes the insect tricky to remove. A close view shows the claws many times larger than life. Gripping one human hair is the job of those claws. Those claws leave the louse with no way to jump. Direct contact from one head to another is the usual way they spread. The other person already has head lice. People often touch heads while they talk. Young children do this a lot while they play. That contact makes children easy targets for these pests. Cats can get lice. Dogs can get lice too. Pet lice do not belong to the kind that wants human hair. Cats cannot give their lice to us. Dogs cannot give their lice to us. We cannot give our lice to cats. We cannot give our lice to dogs. A vet should see any pet that gets lice.

5The vet can give treatment. Shared hats are a known way for the insects to move between people. Someone who already has the insects may share a hat. Sometimes that person shares a comb. Sometimes that person shares some other item. That shared object can carry a louse along. The ride can carry the louse to a new person. This kind of spread rarely happens. These insects last only a short time off a human body. A louse may crawl onto something like a comb. It usually dies before it reaches another person. Head lice have pointed mouths. Each mouth is shaped like a straw. The mouth is for piercing through skin. It is also for sucking up blood. Their bites do not hurt. A small bit of saliva is pushed in during feeding. Saliva is spit. The spit helps keep blood from clotting. Blood that stays liquid is easier to drink. Tan is the color of most adults. They may be gray instead. A blood meal makes the color look darker. The saliva is what makes the bites itchy.

6Bite saliva can leave a person sensitized. Sensitized skin is allergic to the bites. The bitten area gets redness. The bitten area also gets itchiness. The life of a head louse starts with an egg. Head lice eggs are called nits. Nits are tiny. Hairs are where adult females stick each nit. They use a special glue. The glue makes the nits hard to remove. Most nits sit close to the scalp. Many are found near the ears. Many are found at the back of the head. Hatching takes anywhere from seven days to ten days. A nit must stay warm if it is going to hatch. They stay warm by staying close to the scalp. A nit more than 1/4 inch away from the scalp usually dies. That distance is 6 millimeters. A picture shows a nit stuck to a hair. The nit stays there until it is ready to hatch. Young head lice are called nymphs. Nymphs look like small adults. Food is required within twenty-four hours after hatching. If they do not feed in that time, they do not stay alive.

7A head louse has three growing stages. The stages are the egg, or nit, then the young louse, then the adult louse. The eggs take 7 to 10 days to hatch. Life cycle means the full set of stages in one louse life. The life cycle breaks if they are killed before full growth. Pulling them off before full growth also works. Full-grown insects are able to lay more eggs. A nymph turns into an adult in about ten to twelve days. An incomplete metamorphosis is part of that growth. Other insects share this trait of a hard outside. The hard outside helps protect their bodies. The outer layer is shed when growth makes them too big. Then they grow a new outer layer. A ticklish feeling is usually the earliest clue. It feels like something is moving in your hair. An itchy feeling can also come from the bites. Sores can form when itchy places get scratched. Other people can sometimes see nits when the insects are present. Finding a nit probably means the insects are present.

8However, nits can stick to hair even when they are dead. They can also stick after they hatch. Living insects are the best evidence for a diagnosis. Spotting living lice is difficult. Adult head lice do not like light. They can crawl quickly to avoid light. A lice comb is sometimes used to help people look. That comb has very fine teeth. A mother may comb her daughter's hair with that comb. A lice comb has long teeth. The teeth are strong. The teeth are closely spaced. Those teeth remove lice from human hair. They remove nits from human hair too. Special shampoo with chemicals is a common way to kill them. About nine days later, the care usually must be repeated. The second treatment deals with any newly hatched nits. Doctors sometimes order products built from other chemicals. Those chemicals are used to kill the lice. A machine was invented by scientists working at the University of Utah. The machine is like a hair dryer. It kills lice. It kills nits.

9It uses hot air. Hot air helps kids avoid the use of chemicals. Hot air helps parents avoid chemicals too. Students of that same school have shown the machine while it runs. The machine lifts up hair. Hot air comes out next and clears the lice away. Some head lice are called super lice. Super lice are not bigger than other lice. They are not more thirsty for blood than other lice. They are harder to kill. That is because the bugs have changed. Earlier killing methods depended on certain chemicals. Those chemicals no longer hurt them. The change is something many people now report. Many people find that those chemical shampoos now fail. Olive oil is chosen by some people so they can skip chemicals. They use the oil for several weeks. Bedtime is when the oil is put on. They sleep in a shower cap. Head lice are not a sign of unwashed hair. They are not a sign of dirty houses. Anyone can get them. They are just a sign that you are human. They are also a sign that you need treatment.

10Treatment gets rid of your itchy guests. So tell other people the facts. Do not spread the lice. Clotting means a liquid changes into a solid state. The word usually refers to blood. Diagnose means to identify an illness or a problem. Hatch means to come out of an egg. An egg, or something like it, holds an animal inside while the animal grows. Incomplete metamorphosis is an animal's growth through three stages. The stages go from egg to nymph to adult. Pushing something into an animal body is the meaning of inject. A needle can do this. Fangs can do this too. Lice is the plural of louse. Lice are small insects with no wings. They live on other animals. They drink the blood of those animals. Nits are the eggs of lice. Nits are the youngest stage of lice. Sensitized means made sensitive. It can also mean made physically responsive to something. Treatment is medical care given for an illness. It can also be care given for another problem.

31. Jobs That Dogs Can Do

1This book starts with an introduction. Next it tells about service dogs and therapy dogs. Then you will read about search-and-rescue dogs. After that come detection dogs. A closing part and a word list finish the book. Pictures show a hunting dog, sled dogs, and herding dogs. Some dogs do more than live as pets. Those dogs hold jobs. Some dogs work on farms. Farm dogs guard animals. Farm dogs also herd animals. Other dogs pull sleds. Some dogs help people hunt. People and dogs have worked as a team for thousands of years. Why are dogs such good workers? They learn fast. They hear better than people do. They smell better than people do. Because of that, dogs can do work people cannot do. Some of the jobs may surprise you. Many dogs work beside people and help them feel better. The name for these dogs is therapy dog.

2A therapy dog comforts a person. The dog also helps that person stay calm. Sometimes the dogs visit sick people in hospitals. A visit can cheer a patient. A therapy dog needs a friendly personality. Training teaches the dog to stay calm in a new place. The dog must stay calm around new smells. It must stay calm around new people. Studies show that petting a dog can help you. One result is lower stress. Other dogs help people who have disabilities. People call these helpers service dogs. A service dog may have one of many jobs. A guide dog helps a person who does not see well. Training stops the person from walking into traffic. The dog also leads the owner around objects in the way. Then the owner is less likely to trip or fall. One photo shows a guide dog in China leading the owner off a subway train.

3Guide dogs can be trained for big adventures. A guide dog named Tennille hiked in wild country with her owner. No guide dog before Tennille had been trained for wilderness hikes. Her owner is Trevor Thomas. Thomas is blind. Tennille helped him climb over large rocks. She also helped him go around those rocks. She searched for signs along the trail. She brought Thomas up to each sign. Thomas read the words by touching them with his fingers. Thomas hopes other blind people will try a hike like his. He and Tennille want to encourage them. A service dog helps with many tasks of each day. One task is opening a door by pulling a rope. Another task is pushing a button. Some owners do not hear well. Certain service dogs help those owners. The dog listens for a sound such as a doorbell.

4Then the dog touches the owner. The dog moves toward the sound. Some service dogs help the owner get around. They pull wheelchairs. They pick up objects that fall. They press buttons. They open doors. Tyler McCready can have blood sugar that drops too low. His dog, Sinatra, warns him. Sinatra touches Tyler's arm. That touch tells Tyler to take medicine. Some service dogs help by using the nose. Dogs can help a person whose blood sugar is low. The dog smells a change in the blood. It alerts the owner to eat food. It can also alert the owner to take medicine. Other service dogs smell a seizure before it starts. A seizure is a sudden attack. It can cause spasms in the body. It can cause other problems too. The dog warns the person before the attack. Children with food allergies have helpers of another kind.

5Some of those children cannot eat peanuts. The dog smells peanuts hidden in food. Some dogs search for people who are lost. They also search for people who are hurt or trapped. Their name is search-and-rescue dog. People often shorten that name to SAR. Many SAR dogs use a strong nose to find someone who needs help. Trainers teach the search in two ways. The first way is tracking. The dog puts its nose on the ground. It follows a scent it finds there. A tracking dog can follow a missing person while the scent is still fresh. The team must work fast. If they wait, the scent fades. One SAR dog helps police follow a boy. The boy had left his house during the night. The second way uses air, not the ground. These dogs are called air-scent dogs. They sniff the air.

6They often search a wide area. With only the nose, they can find a skier under deep snow. The snow can be 5 meters deep. That depth is 16 feet. They can find a hiker who is lost. They can find a person inside a building that has collapsed. In China, a firefighter and a rescue dog search a place where an accident happened. In England, a Newfoundland practices a rescue in the water. Some SAR dogs work in water instead of on land. A water rescue dog can be trained to pull a drowning person to safety. The dog can find a person under the water. It can pull a small boat back to land. These dogs must be strong swimmers. That is why big dogs such as Newfoundlands do this job well. A Newfoundland has waterproof fur. Its toes are webbed. It loves to swim.

7It is much stronger than a human lifeguard. One Newfoundland weighs 68 kilograms. That weight is 150 pounds. In one rescue, a dog of that size can bring ten people to a safe place. Some dogs work like detectives. People call them detection dogs. They use smell to find objects that are hidden. Police use these dogs often. After a building burns, police may use an arson dog. People may not be sure the fire was started on purpose. The arson dog sniffs for chemicals. Those chemicals may be what started the fire. One picture shows an arson dog searching a burned building. A dog checks bags that arrive in the United Kingdom. It sniffs for items the law does not allow. Detection dogs also work at airports. They work at border crossings. They look for objects that must not enter a country. They look for objects that must not go on a plane.

8Fresh fruit usually cannot cross a border unless someone gives permission. Live animals are usually kept out in the same way. The dogs can find these hidden things inside bags. Police also use detection dogs when they look for criminals. Some detection dogs work far from crime. Trainers teach some dogs to hunt for termites. The dogs search houses and other buildings. Termites hurt a building because they eat wood. People have trouble finding them. A termite dog can smell where they are. Someday, detection dogs may help doctors. Some dogs seem able to smell cancer in a person. Scientists study those dogs. They want to know if doctors could get a quick warning about cancer from these dogs. The help would be for patients. One dog shows this skill by sniffing breath samples. The samples come from people.

9The dog is checking for lung cancer. Dogs can learn odd jobs quickly. In Michigan, a dog named Piper shared airport work with the person who owned him. Piper's task was chasing birds that were wild. He kept the birds off the runways where planes roll. Another group of dogs worked in San Francisco. Their workplace was a baseball park beside the ocean. The dogs jumped into the water. They brought back balls that players hit into the water. That program has ended. For a while, fans loved to collect those balls. People called the balls Splash Hit balls. Piper was the dog who worked at the airport. Pictures show a service dog, a police dog, and a search-and-rescue dog. Dogs share many kinds of work with people. When human work changes, the jobs we give dogs change too. A dog may sniff out cancer cells.

10A dog may pull a swimmer to a safe place. In ways like these, dogs help humans. Strength makes a dog a wonderful worker. A friendly nature helps. Intelligence helps. Strong senses help too. Here are word meanings from the book. To alert someone is to warn that person or give that person news. A chemical is a substance that has certain properties. Chemicals can join with other substances and make something new. To comfort someone is to give hope and help in a hard time. It can also mean to soothe. Detection means finding that something is there. It can also mean solving a mystery. A disability is a physical condition or a mental condition. It limits what a person is able to do. A spasm is a movement the person cannot control. The movement is not natural. It starts when a muscle suddenly gets tight.

32. John Muir and the Wild Land

1John Muir went into nature to draw near a place he called the heart of the world. Long ago, settlers started colonizing western land. Today that region is the west of the United States. Many of those settlers talked of conquering nature. Indigenous peoples there had managed the land. They had guarded it for thousands of years. Settlers wanted the land cleared. They wanted it used for their own needs. Muir was not like most settlers. He wanted nature kept safe. He wanted pieces of land saved as national parks. Muir could write with great skill. He could speak with great skill too. He used both skills to stir other people. He hoped they would care about conservation. The house where John Muir was born stands in Scotland. Today that house is a museum. The museum tells of his life. It also tells of his work. His birthday was April 21, 1838. Scotland was his birth country. He was the first son in the family. Eight children made up the family.

2At age eleven, John moved with his family. Wisconsin, inside the United States, was the new home. A farm was his father's next project. The father was a very strict man. Farm labor filled long hours of the children's days. Each day, the children received only one small meal. Their health grew poor because of this. John did not go to school in those years. Learning still mattered to him. He got up early so he would have time to read. Each morning he was up at 1:00 a.m. He taught himself many subjects. Any free hour in the day went to exploring. He walked the woods near the farm. He walked the fields too. In his teen years, some of that early time went to inventions. One invention was a special alarm clock. When the set time came, the device shoved him from the bed. The clock was joined to a desk. The desk was fixed to a bed that could fall. At the set time, a lamp lit.

3Then John slid onto the floor. As a young adult, Muir felt restless. He wanted to follow the work he loved most. At twenty-two, he brought his inventions to a fair. The fair was a state fair in Madison. People loved the inventions. The alarm clock drew the biggest interest. A judge gave him a prize of cash. That money brought him a new kind of freedom. He had never had that independence before. He chose not to return home. Madison became the place he stayed. College began for him that fall. Jeanne Carr grew close to Muir as a friend. She was also a teacher to him. She was a writer. She studied plants. Ezra Carr grew close to him in the same way. Ezra was a professor of science. A picture shows Muir in his younger years. His main studies were math. Science was a main study too. Plant science was part of that work. In 1863, he left Madison behind. He set off for a school he named himself.

4He called it the University of the Wilderness. He walked the route. The walk took him through parts of Canada. It also took him through parts of the Midwest. Odd jobs paid for the travel. In 1866, he took work as a mechanic. The following year, an accident at work took his sight for a short time. He was afraid the blindness would last. Sight came back after one month. He understood he was not living the life he loved most. He quit the job. Nature would be his life from then on. A map shows the route Muir took toward Florida in 1867. He started from Indianapolis. Some miles were by train. Some miles were on foot. Some miles were by boat. He crossed Ohio. He crossed Illinois. He crossed Indiana. He crossed West Virginia. He crossed Kentucky. He crossed Virginia. He crossed Tennessee. He crossed North Carolina. He crossed South Carolina. He crossed Georgia. He crossed Alabama. He crossed Mississippi. Cedar Keys was on the route.

5The Atlantic Ocean lay near that coast. Florida was the end of the line. He set out on September 1, 1867. The hike covered about 1,000 miles. That is 1,609 km. Florida was the place he hiked toward. The coast took a little more than seven weeks to reach. The next March, he arrived in San Francisco. He asked a person for directions. He wanted the nearest way out of the city. The person asked for the goal of the trip. Muir said he wanted any place that was wild. On foot, he aimed for the Sierra Nevada of California. The beauty there amazed him. That first stay was short. He came back the next year. Shepherd work filled a short stretch of time. A sawmill became his next job. The mill stood near Yosemite Falls. The name Yosemite is said yoh-SEH-muh-tee. A map of the Sierra Nevada region can show the area. California is on that map. San Francisco is on it. The Pacific Ocean is on it. Ahwahnee Valley is on it.

6People also call that place Yosemite Valley. Free hours went to walking the land. The writing of Ralph Waldo Emerson meant a great deal to him. Emerson often wrote about a bond with nature. In the early 1870s, Muir offered a new idea. He said moving glaciers had shaped Yosemite Valley. Glacier study drew him far into the mountains. He searched there for proof. A glacier article from him came out in October 1871. Later, others proved the idea. More people began to notice him. Lovers of nature came to his cabin. They came to meet him. Emerson made that visit. Settlers had given the area the name Yosemite. The land already carried another name. Ahwahnee Valley means a place like a gaping mouth. The name Ahwahnee is said ah-WAH-nee. Several Indigenous tribes call it home. The Ahwahneechee are one of those tribes. The Miwok are another. The Paiute people are another. Settlers used force to remove many Indigenous peoples. Those settlers were the ones who named it Yosemite.

7El Capitan is a feature of Yosemite Valley. Yosemite Falls is a feature of the valley too. Oakland, California, became Muir's home in 1873. There he wrote magazine articles about nature. He hoped readers would care about nature as deeply as he did. Muir married Louisa Wanda Strentzel in April 1880. People called her Louie. He went to live on her family's fruit ranch. Ranch chores left him unhappy. Louie stood by him. Her support let him travel again. Louie Wanda Strentzel lived from 1847 to 1905. Her parents were immigrants from Poland. Every subject she studied brought her a perfect score. Music was a gift of hers. A life as a concert pianist was open to her. Quiet days at the family's farm were the life she picked. She backed her husband's love of nature. She did not feel that love herself. Two daughters were born to them. The girls were named Wanda and Helen. A photo shows John Muir with his wife and the two girls.

8After a trip to Yosemite in 1889, Muir printed two articles. Both pieces of writing described how beautiful the area was. Citizens pressed the government to guard Yosemite. One year later, Yosemite was named a national park. It was the third national park in the United States. The Sierra Club started in 1892. Muir helped found it. The club worked to preserve the Sierra Nevada. Muir was the club's first president. Today the Sierra Club is a leading group for conservation. President Theodore Roosevelt requested a meeting with Muir in 1903. He wanted to understand the West's wild places more clearly. He wanted to grasp what those areas needed. Muir led him on a camping trip inside Yosemite. During the nights outside, Muir made a request. He asked the president to save wild lands as parks. Because of that trip, more of Yosemite joined the national park. Roosevelt also saved many other wild places. A photo shows both men standing on Glacier Point. In later years, Muir gave more time to writing.

9Travel stories filled his writing. A fight of twelve years also became his work. The fight tried to save a valley in Yosemite from a dam. The dam would have flooded the valley. People worked to stop the project. Muir sent a letter to members of the government. Even so, the project won approval in 1913. The fight made more people aware of the need to protect natural land. It also helped bring about a new agency. The National Park Service began in 1916. People call it the NPS. The NPS built stronger management. The management covered every national park in the United States. John Muir's death came on December 24, 1914. The National Park Service began only after he had died. His work still helped that agency become real. The word wilderness can suggest land with nobody living on it. It can suggest land that nobody cares for. Many places Muir called wilderness were not empty. Indigenous peoples had lived there for a long time. They had protected those places.

10Muir did not always see how they cared for the land. One example is the controlled burn, a fire that is planned. Indigenous peoples had used these fires for thousands of years. The fires kept the land healthy. Muir believed every fire should be stopped. He thought that was the only way to guard the land. Scientists today know those planned fires help the land stay healthy. John Muir's name is on many wild places. His name is on many groups as well. Muir Woods National Monument is one of those places. Keeping natural spaces safe mattered deeply to him. His work helped create national parks. Those parks still protect beautiful land. Today the Sierra Club works on many kinds of environmental problems. Those problems show up across the whole country. Indigenous peoples are partners in the work. They protect land together. They protect animals together. They want the places people love to stay safe. They want those places to stay healthy in the years ahead.

33. Kites, From Toys to Tools

1This book opens with an introduction. Next comes the history of kites. Then you will read about unusual jobs for kites. After that, you can build a kite of your own. A word list comes last. A sky crowded with kites is one of the brightest, liveliest sights there is. The kites are full of color. They look as if they are dancing. Each kite seems to want your eyes on it. Dragons with long tails fly up high. Stars that spin fly up high. Cartoon characters that people love fly up high too. A kite is more than a plain toy now. New materials have changed kites. New ways of engineering have changed them too. Today's kites are bigger. They are lighter. They are stronger. Many of them move like acrobats high above the ground.

2One kite has the shape of an eagle. It is much like kites people made thousands of years ago. The first toy kites took their name from a bird called a kite. That bird is large and graceful. Its wings stretch very wide. Many of the oldest kites were made in bird shapes. People kept testing new ways to build kites. Bit by bit, the kites grew more elaborate. They gained special features. They gained special ways of flying. Chinese history holds very early notes about kite flying. The oldest written kite story is more than 2,200 years old. The story is about General Han Hsin of China and a problem he faced. He needed the length of a tunnel under the walls of his enemy.

3A kite helped him measure that length. Chinese legends also tell about two thinkers, Mozi and Lu Ban. They built a kite that carried Lu Ban off the ground. Some kites changed history. Ben Franklin was a famous American diplomat and inventor. He loved to test ideas. He used a kite to test one idea. He believed lightning was electric current. During a storm, Franklin and his son put a metal key onto the string. They sent the kite up into the storm clouds. Franklin was right about what happened next. Electricity from the clouds moved down the wet string. It reached the metal key. He moved his hand toward the key. Electricity jumped from the key. The jump gave him a shock. Do not try this test yourself.

4Franklin tried the test again later. That time, he was badly hurt. One kite lifts tools that measure the weather. Kites have been used in surprising ways. Fishing is one use. Fish like to chase objects in the water. Some fishers want to reach fish that stay far from the beach. They tie a shiny hook and bait onto a kite's long tail. The kite drags that bait along the top of the water. A fish sees the bait. The fish bites. Then the fisher reels the fish in. Weather study is another use. For many years, scientists sent up a special kite shaped like a box. The kite carried weather tools high into the air. The tools helped them chart how fast the wind blew. The tools also recorded how warm or cold the air was.

5A kite can help a cargo ship use less fuel. The same kite can help the ship make less air pollution. Real wind power is another use. Cargo ships on long trips burn a great deal of fuel. They also pollute the air. One shipping company in Germany uses special kites to ease both problems. The large kite helps pull the ship along. Because of that pull, the engines burn less fuel each day. Less fuel burned means less air pollution. Not every spy is a person with special training. For years, armies tied small cameras to kites. The cameras were powerful. The kites flew above enemy places and watched them. Armies also used kites when soldiers practiced shooting.

6Those kites could twist in the air. They could dive. They could slip away from bullets the way a fighter plane does. In World War I, the French army sent up kites it called war kites. The kites watched the enemy. A kite that sails lightly overhead can still make people smile. A little practice is enough for almost anyone to build a kite. Almost anyone can learn to fly one. Later, you may want to try harder kite skills. Stunt kites use two strings or more. You might also try kitesurfing. In that sport, a large power kite pulls a surfboard. Kitesurfing is exciting. It is strong exercise too. It builds muscles in the middle of the body. It builds muscles in the lower body too. To make a kite, you need two sticks that are light and smooth.

7One stick is 3 feet long, or 90 centimeters. The other stick is 2 feet long, or 60 centimeters. You need a large plastic trash bag that you have cut open. You need a long strip of light cloth or light plastic for a tail. You need scissors. You need strong tape. You need a very long string. Step 1. Lay the sticks so they cross. Put tape around the place where the sticks cross. Step 2. Cut a piece of string that is 4 feet long, or 120 centimeters. Tape one end of that string to one end of the long stick. Tape the other end of the string to the other end of the long stick. People give this string the name bridle.

8Take the rest of your string. Knot that end at the bridle's center. Step 3. Set the crossed sticks on the open plastic bag. Cut the plastic into a diamond around the sticks. Leave extra plastic past each stick end. Step 4. Fold each plastic corner over the end of a stick. Tape each corner so it holds tight. Step 5. Tape the cloth strip or the plastic strip to the bottom point. That strip is the tail. Fly the kite only in an open place that is safe. Stay far from power lines. Stay away from trees. Stay away from roads. Stay away from airports. Do not fly during rain. Do not fly if you hear thunder. Look at the ground first so you will not trip.

9When you test your first kite, choose a day with a breeze. Carry the kite to a place that is flat and open. A nearby beach will work. Look around before you start. Make sure no power line can catch the kite. Make sure no large tree can catch it. Make sure nothing on the ground can trip you. Are you ready? Hold the kite up by the bridle. Run toward the wind. When you feel a tug, let the kite go. Let a little string slide out. Do this slowly. Stop for a moment when you feel the wind take the kite. Then let out more string. Keep going until the kite is high in the sky. Here are word meanings from the book.

10A bridle is a kind of harness. Strings or ropes on it help guide movement. A diplomat is a person who speaks for one government to another government. Elaborate means made of many parts and many details. A fishhook is a curved hook made of metal. Fishers attach it where a fishing line ends. An instrument is a tool that makes a measurement or records one. An inventor is a person who creates a new device or a new process. A legend is an old story that many people know. Nobody can prove that the story is true. A shock is a sharp jolt. It happens when electric current touches a body. Temperature tells how hot or cold something is. A thermometer is the tool that measures it.

34. Leading the Country Day by Day

1This book opens with a short introduction. Then it explains the executive branch. Next it tells what that branch does. A short closing comes after that. A list of hard words comes at the end. A portrait shows George Washington. He was the earliest president of the United States. For more than 225 years, a president has been at work every day. On every day of every year, the job matters. No one in the United States holds more power than the president. The work of the president is the executive branch in action. In 1788, American leaders put a system of laws in place. They called that system the Constitution. It was meant to help govern the young country. People still follow that system today.

2The system divides the United States government into three branches, or parts. The president leads the executive branch. The legislative branch makes the laws. That branch speaks as the voice of the American people. The judicial branch keeps watch on the justice system. It does that work through the courts. A chart names all three branches. The judicial branch includes the Supreme Court. It also includes other federal courts. The legislative branch includes the Senate. It also includes the House of Representatives. The executive branch includes the president. It includes the vice president. It includes the cabinet. At times the branches pull in the same direction. At other times, one branch pushes against another. The system grants the president a long list of powers.

3The president does not rule as a king. Voters are the ones who choose the president. A new election arrives every four years. A person may serve as president for two terms. Service beyond two terms is not allowed. Four terms as president belonged to Franklin Delano Roosevelt. His service ran from 1933 to 1945. He died in 1945. After his death, the legislative branch reset the limit. The new limit was two terms. A president must be thirty-five years old or older. Birth in the United States is required of the president. A life of fourteen years or more inside the United States is required too. In 1961, John F. Kennedy ate breakfast with his daughter, Caroline. Voters had elected him at the age of forty-three.

4That age made him the youngest person elected president. In 1978, Jimmy Carter walks beside Anwar Sadat. The two men talk as they walk. Anwar Sadat was president of Egypt. The president holds many powers. One power lets the president send troops of the United States anywhere in the world. The president also sits down with other leaders. Together they work on many kinds of problems. Some of those leaders come from other parts of the world. Some of them are leaders inside the United States. Many of them sit on the president's cabinet. Fifteen departments make up the cabinet. Helping to run the country is work for every department. The Department of Energy is one example.

5Enough power for the whole country to keep moving is its aim. It plans for power in the present. It also plans for power in later years. The department helps choose the kinds of energy Americans use. Oil pumps bring up oil. That oil serves cars and heating. Solar panels change energy from the Sun into electricity. In 1974, Gerald Ford took the place of Richard Nixon. Ford had been vice president, and then he became president. The vice president is part of the cabinet. The vice president is expected to step in if the president cannot go on. Voters choose the vice president in the same election as the president. The president picks the other members of the cabinet. The president may also dismiss a cabinet member at any time.

6The president also takes part in making laws. Those laws cover many subjects. One law might set rules for running schools. Saving animals could be the topic of a second law. That kind of law might also be about saving land. A third law might set rules that banks must follow. Every law begins as an idea, no matter the subject. After the idea is written down, people call it a bill. In 1972, Richard Nixon signed a law that guards certain wild animals. The bald eagle was one animal on that list. More than two hundred kinds of animals have been saved by the law so far. A bill goes to the president after the legislative branch passes it.

7A signature from the president turns the bill into law. The president may veto a bill. A veto means the president does not agree with the bill. A veto keeps the bill from turning into law. A vote by two-thirds of the legislative branch can still turn it into law. A vote that strong seldom happens. The president also takes part in the judicial branch. The link is that the president chooses judges. Those judges then help shape the law of the United States. The legislative branch must agree to those judges before they serve. In 1981, Ronald Reagan chose Sandra Day O'Connor. She became the first woman to sit on the Supreme Court. That court is the highest one in the judicial branch.

8In 1863, Abraham Lincoln wrote an order. The order freed many people who were enslaved. His honor is the Lincoln Memorial in Washington, D.C. The executive branch is not the branch that makes laws. Even so, a president can sometimes write orders. People call those orders executive actions. An executive action tells the government what to do. An executive action can change the lives of Americans. Under the Constitution, each branch can check the other two. A check is a way to stop another branch. Checks and balances form an important part of the Constitution. The system helps stop any one branch from holding too much power. The legislative branch can check the executive branch. It controls the budget.

9Taking a president out of office is within its power. A check on the legislative branch can come from the executive branch. It can veto a bill and block that bill from becoming law. Congress can overcome a veto when it has enough votes. The executive branch checks the judicial branch when it picks judges. The judicial branch checks the executive branch in return. It checks that the president's orders follow the Constitution. The legislative branch can check the judicial branch. Judges can be taken out of office by this branch. The judicial branch checks that laws from Congress follow the Constitution. In 2007, George W. Bush shakes hands with Nancy Pelosi. Nancy Pelosi was Speaker of the House. The Speaker holds the most power in the House of Representatives.

10Each day, the president and the cabinet make choices that matter. Those choices touch every American. Even so, they are only one branch. All three branches are needed for the system to work. After many years, the system still works. Approve means to agree in an official way. Advisers to a president, or to another leader of a country, form a cabinet. The Constitution is the written set of rules and ideas behind the laws. An election is the act of picking a person for office by voting. Executive describes the branch that leads a nation and carries out its laws. Veto means to refuse a bill so the bill does not become law.

35. Machines Made for Flight

1This book opens with an introduction. Then it tells how flight began. Next come airplanes. Helicopters follow. Blimps and balloons come after that. Rockets and spaceships are next. Then the book shows other ways to fly. An index closes the book. Close your eyes. Picture yourself flying the way a bird flies. Where would you go? What would you see? Imagine that you had never seen an airplane. Imagine that you had never heard of one. Could you picture what flight would feel like? Let us meet some fantastic machines. They are the machines that make flight possible.

2People have wanted to fly for as long as anyone can remember. They watched birds in the air. They dreamed that flying would feel wonderful. A few people had useful ideas. They asked what lets an object move through air. One man built a small toy propeller. The toy could spin its way through the air. In the 1400s, the artist Leonardo da Vinci drew plans. The plans showed a helicopter. The machine might have worked. He never built it. People kept testing ideas. Some tied wings to their arms and flapped.

3A few tied their bodies to large kites. Nearly all of them crashed again and again. They still wanted to fly. The history of flight takes a big step in 1783. Two brothers in France invented the hot-air balloon. The first balloon passengers were one sheep, one rooster, and one duck. George Cayley made the earliest glider that a person could ride. Later, an engineer made a better glider. He printed his ideas in 1889. A few years after that, his ideas and notes shaped many plans. Those ideas and notes inspired many designs by the Wright brothers.

4A sketch shows one early glider by Cayley. A drawing shows the helicopter from Leonardo da Vinci. One set of wings flapped when the rider moved both arms and both legs. Orville Wright and Wilbur Wright studied those early ideas. They also studied why most of the ideas failed. After many tests, they built an airplane with an engine. Their plane was the first successful one with an engine. The plane held only one person. The brothers took turns trying it. December 17, 1903, was Orville's day. He stayed in the air for one whole minute.

5He traveled 260 meters. That distance is 850 feet. People could fly. Airplanes have changed a great deal since those first flights. The flying machine most people notice first is a large passenger plane. Have you taken a plane on a vacation? A plane of that kind can hold about 450 people plus their bags. It can cross the country. It can go around the world. One commercial plane carries hundreds of travelers around the world each day. In a famous picture, Wilbur Wright watches Orville. Orville is making the flight that made history.

6A Navy jet fighter leaves the deck of an aircraft carrier. Gases from the jet draw long cloud streaks in the sky. Many slower planes use propeller engines. Many older planes use them too. Each blade of a propeller works like a wing. Spinning makes air suction at each blade. Fan blades work in a similar way. The suction pulls the plane forward. Stunt pilots fly small propeller planes. They perform tricks at air shows. Farmers use the same kind of plane to spray crops. One plane can carry 132,000 kilograms of cargo.

7That weight is 291,000 pounds. Helicopters are another flying machine you see often. A helicopter can take off in almost any place. It can land in almost any place. Most helicopters have two rotors. Lift comes from the large rotor above the body. The pilot uses a smaller tail rotor to steer. A plane cannot fly backward. A helicopter can. A helicopter can spin in place. It can hover, staying still in the air. One helicopter comes down on a building's top. Some planes are built for one special job.

8Mail planes bring packages during the night. Military cargo planes carry trucks. They carry supplies. They carry troops. Some planes are set up like offices. People can keep working while they fly to a business meeting. Troops climb aboard a military helicopter. A large helicopter fights a wildfire. A big bucket lets it drop a heavy load of water on the fire. A helicopter ambulance carries people who are sick. It also carries people who are hurt. Bright balloons are scattered across the sky. Riders in a hot-air balloon sit in a basket.

9A rocket is a very powerful engine. It pushes hot gas out behind it. That push is called thrust. In 1957, the Soviet Union shocked the world. A rocket from that country launched the first satellite. The satellite was named Sputnik. Sputnik caught the imagination of everyone. People began to think about travel in space. They began to think about life as astronauts. Since that launch, rockets have taken people to the Moon. Rockets have also helped build a station in space. To leave Earth, a space shuttle sits on a rocket. It brings astronauts to the International Space Station.

10It brings supplies there too. On the way home, wings let the shuttle land the way an airplane lands. Rockets lift the space shuttle off the ground. One picture shows a space shuttle circling Earth. Astronauts reached the Moon aboard the Saturn V rocket. A hang glider passes over a beach. A modern glider sails through the air. The index names Orville Wright and Wilbur Wright. It names the space shuttle. It names the rotor. It names the propeller. It names Leonardo da Vinci. It names jet engines.

36. Magnets at Work All Around You

1A crane in a junkyard carries a giant magnet. Scrap metal rises in a pile under that magnet. Nature never made a magnet able to hoist loads so heavy. Strength here is set by electricity. Turn the electricity on. The magnet then grabs the metal. Cut the electricity. The metal then falls. The crane holds the magnet high over the scrap. Hidden magnetism is the first topic. Magnets in many places come next. Useful words come at the end. A magnet is metal with a special trait. Other metals are drawn to it. Search your home and see if you can spot one. Hundreds may be hiding in that home. Most stay out of sight.

2A telephone can hold some. A blender can hold some. A hair dryer can hold some. Computers contain many magnets. Magnetism is the source of most of the electricity these machines use. Household objects often depend on magnets. A force called magnetism cannot be seen. Only some metals feel its pull. Iron is one metal that responds. Copper does not get pulled. Wood does not get pulled either. The force travels inside the magnet. One end is where it goes in. The other end is where it comes out. Poles are the name for those ends. North is one pole. South is the other pole. Leaving happens at the north pole. Returning happens at the south pole.

3A bar magnet in a photo wears a coat of metal bits. Those bits hang on the end marked N. They hang on the end marked S too. N stands for north. S stands for south. The bits make the invisible pull easier to picture. The magnet in the photo has a bar shape. Bits gather at the two ends. Eyes still cannot catch the force. They only catch what the force does to metal. Poles that do not match will draw together every time. Two magnets in your hands can show you the force. Point a north end toward a south end and you feel the tug. Pushing two north ends together is a different test.

4The push meets real resistance. North rejects north. South rejects south. Poles that match always shove each other away. Modern life is full of jobs for magnets. Millions of them can sit on a single credit card. Their size is tiny. A grain of powder is as big as they get. Each one aims its own way. A code is what those aims create. Computers are able to read the code. Trouble starts if a strong magnet comes near the card. The code can be scrambled. The code can be wiped away. In a shop, someone slides a credit card through a reader. The store computer then reads what the card holds.

5Tiny magnets, millions of them, are what build that code. River water turns the generators at a dam. Magnets spin because of that water. Electricity is the result. Making electricity is one job for magnets. Using electricity is another job. Generators supply almost every bit of electricity you use. A generator is a machine with a clear task. Magnets inside it spin. Coils of wire surround that spin. An electric current appears in the wire because of the magnetism. An electric motor can be seen from the outside. The inside is different. Flowing current turns one inside part into a magnet as well. Copper wire is the path for that current.

6Motors do the generator's job backward. Electric current is one thing they use. Magnets are the other. An axle starts to spin. A fan can be what the axle turns. A cutting blade can be what it turns. A wheel can be what it turns. Moving parts like these power a fan. They power a blender. They power a remote-control car. High-speed trains can ride above a track on magnets. Travel feels like flight. Speed is part of the ride. Smoothness is part of it too. One train in China skips ordinary wheels. A cushion made by magnets holds it up. Train and track have that cushion between them. About 267 miles per hour is its fastest pace.

7About 430 km per hour is the same pace. A doctor can see inside a person with a magnet of great power. Cancer can show up in the view. Other illness can show up too. No cut has to be made. Brain pictures from an MRI come in dozens. Detail fills those pictures. Doctors study them. Earth's magnetic field is the comparison. The MRI field is greater than Earth's by a factor of about 10,000. The floating train does not roll on wheels. Doctors can look at the pictures one by one. Each picture adds a view of the body's inside. The magnet does that looking. No surgery is needed for the view.

8Kids get help from magnets in many activities they enjoy. Fun is one side of magnets. Real usefulness is the other side. A television you watch is using magnets. A light you switch on is using magnets. A magnet can be made at home. Gather a nail made of iron. The nail should be large. Gather a magnet that is strong. Gather paper clips. Several will do. Grip the nail at one end only. Draw the nail along the magnet. Keep the slide going one way only. Repeat that slide many times. The nail, the magnet, and the clips are the whole kit. The nail is the piece that moves. The magnet stays as the surface under it.

9One direction means you do not rub backward. Every pass copies the same motion. Many passes are the whole of this step. A picture shows kids busy with things they enjoy. Magnets play a part in that fun. A light coming on is one daily use. A television is another daily use. Stop after 20 to 30 slides. Bring the nail against the paper clips. Will the clips rise with the nail? Count how many it can lift together. More slides along the magnet will build the effect. The nail's magnetic force can grow that way. Try for every clip in one lift. Attract is a word for a pull toward something.

10A code sends messages. Its letters, symbols, or signals carry a special meaning. Current is electricity moving along one path. Electricity, as people use the word here, means current used for power. A force can move an object. Strength is one way to describe it. Energy is another way. Generators change motion into electricity. They are machines. Invisible describes what eyes cannot catch. Magnetism pushes some metals. Magnetism pulls some metals. It is a force. Repel is a word for a push away. Clips that rise prove the stroking worked. Further strokes can lift a higher number. All of the clips at one time is the mark to aim for.

37. Michelle Obama: Work That Lifts Others

1In 2011, Michelle Obama poses in the White House kitchen garden. Bo, the family dog, is with her. This book follows a life spent in service. It tells of her childhood in Chicago. It tells of college and of law school. It tells of her work in Chicago. It tells of her years as First Lady. It tells of her push to help others succeed. A list of hard words comes at the end. Around the world, Michelle Obama is among the most famous women. She used to be First Lady of the United States. Her husband, Barack, was the forty-fourth president. All through her life, she has worked so other people can succeed. In 2008, she appears on a television show. Even before she was First Lady, she led work in her home city. That city was Chicago. 2As First Lady, she found fresh ways to help. She stood with people who serve in the American military. She worked so American children could get a better education. She also tried to help children move their bodies more. She tried to help them eat food that is healthy. Michelle's birthplace was Chicago. The year of her birth was 1964. She raised Michelle there. She also raised Michelle's older brother, Craig. The Robinson family comes from enslaved African Americans. Those ancestors lived in the South. Michelle's grandfather was Fraser Robinson Jr. In the 1930s, he left South Carolina. He moved to Chicago because he wanted a better life. A yearbook photo shows young Michelle Robinson. The photo is from elementary school in the South Shore neighborhood. The family lived in a small apartment in the south of Chicago.

3The family stayed close. They shared dinners as a family. They played board games. Money was limited. Fraser worked hard. Marian worked hard too. They urged the children to do their best at school. By age four, Michelle already knew how to read. Craig could read by the age of four as well. Each of them skipped second grade. At her elementary school, Michelle entered a gifted class. Classes at a college close to home were part of her studies too. Later, she was picked for Whitney Young High School. Whitney Young was the first magnet school in Chicago. The school got students ready for college. Each day, a city bus ride of one hour took her to school. She took classes that were hard. The student council had her as its treasurer. In 2018, she went back to Whitney Young High School. She came to visit the students.

4Michelle got a very strong education. Her dreams were large. After high school, she wanted to follow Craig to Princeton University. Few universities in the United States stand higher than Princeton. She told some teachers about that dream. They said her aim was too high. They did not think Princeton would take her. Michelle made up her mind to show those teachers they were wrong. At a magnet school in Florida, a dance teacher guides the students. Magnet schools are one kind of public school in the United States. They teach special classes that other public schools do not offer. Some, such as Whitney Young, prepare students for college. Other magnet schools teach special science classes. Some focus on music. Some focus on math. Some focus on languages. Some focus on the arts. Some magnet schools ask students to pass a test. Other magnet schools pick students by drawing names. Hard work in high school brought results. Princeton said yes.

5There, Michelle studied the ways people work and live side by side. African American studies was another subject in her class list. She learned the history of black people in the United States. She learned about their culture too. Princeton felt new to her. Many classmates grew up in rich families. Few other African American students were on campus then. She felt that classmates saw her race before anything else. She felt that teachers did the same. Her final project described that feeling. She wrote that she seemed like a guest on campus. She wrote that she seemed not to fit there. She kept up the hard study anyway. In 1985, she finished with top grades. At Princeton she served as treasurer for her class. She finished in the top ten percent of that class. A photo can show a building at Harvard Law School. After Princeton, Michelle went on to Harvard Law School. Few law schools in the United States stand higher than this one.

6At Harvard, she worked so the student body would be more diverse. She urged black students to make Harvard their school. She also spoke about the teachers. She said the school needed more black professors. In 1988, she finished and earned a law degree. Back in Chicago, she began work as a lawyer. In the summer of 1989, her boss gave her a job to do. She worried that a date would look bad. The worry came because they worked together. Later, she said yes. They married in Chicago in 1992. Their daughter Malia was born in 1998. Their daughter Natasha was born in 2001. Most people call Natasha by the name Sasha. A wedding photo shows Michelle and Barack together.

7In 2009, Michelle watches a White House event. Her old boss speaks at that event. He was Richard Daley, mayor of Chicago. Michelle soon saw that a big law firm was the wrong place for her. In 1991, she left her job as a lawyer. She became a city planner for Mayor Richard Daley. Politics did not interest Michelle. Politics did interest her husband. For a few years, she led a program for young people. The program trained them to lead in their own communities. She later called this work her favorite job. She loved to see what her trainees did next. Students at the University of Chicago became the next people she advised. Volunteer jobs in the city were what she helped them find.

8After that, she worked at a hospital. There she led programs for volunteers. She also led programs that taught people about health. In 2004, she held a post at the University of Chicago Hospitals. Vice President for Community and External Affairs was the title on her job. In 2011, the First Lady leads a group of children. The children try to set a world record for jumping jacks. Through the 2000s, Michelle played a big part in Barack's political wins. She helped him win a seat in the Senate in 2004. She gave many speeches for him. The speeches supported his races for president. Those races were in 2008 and in 2012. Both races ended in a win. As First Lady, she could help on an even larger scale. Kids and schooling mattered most to her. In 2010, she began a program called Let's Move! Schools in the program served food that was healthy.

9The program also pushed kids to exercise. She worked so students would keep learning after high school. In 2015, she and Barack began another program. They called it Let Girls Learn. It backed training for teachers of girls. She also wanted to stand with military families. She joined Jill Biden in that work. The vice president's wife was Jill Biden. 10She has tried to help them succeed. She believes hard work is what brought her own success. The same kind of hard work is what she hopes children everywhere will give. In 2013, she spoke to high school students in Washington, D.C. She told them that a path like hers could be theirs too. Advise means to offer a suggestion or to give notice. Encouraged means urged someone, in a kind way, to act. First lady means the wife of a president or of another leader. Law is the study or the work of rules for a government or a society. 1Places linked to Gandhi's life and work appear on a map. India is marked. Pakistan is marked. Both sit in Asia. South Africa is marked in Africa. The Indian Ocean is marked too. 5Ending the protest was his answer. A 1915 photo marks the return to India. Days later, a crowd gathered. Thousands came. Peace was the mood of that gathering. Shooting started without warning. 39. Moths and Butterflies Up Close

1Table of Contents. Introduction 4. What Are Butterflies and Moths? 5. Life Cycle 8. Life as an Adult 13. Conclusion 18. Glossary 19. Index 20. Introduction. A butterfly can be beautiful. A moth can be beautiful. Gardens are grown to bring butterflies. Zoos and city gardens draw crowds for them. No garden is grown to bring moths. Many people call moths pests. How do the two match? How do they differ? What marks an insect as a butterfly? What marks an insect as a moth? Body, life cycle, and behavior hold answers. No butterfly is larger than the Queen Alexandra's birdwing. Wings can reach 30 cm (12 in) tip to tip. Papua New Guinea is its home. What Are Butterflies and Moths? Butterflies and moths are insects.

2They are invertebrates with no backbone, like every insect species. Exoskeleton is their hard skin over soft insides. Six legs, three body parts, two antennae, plus two compound eyes. Hair-like scales cover the wings, block soaking, and hold heat. Too cold, a butterfly cannot fly until sun warms it. Labels: legs, body, tube-like mouth, eye, antenna, wings, scales. Shared antenna, head, thorax, abdomen, and wings make mix-ups easy. Many small eyes in each compound eye see all around. Antennae smell and sense movement. A long tube mouth sucks nectar. Over 165,000 species (kinds) exist, mostly moths, plus a believed 100,000 not studied or identified. Thorax: two wing pairs, three leg pairs, breathing holes. Abdomen: digestive organs, reproductive organs, breathing holes. They differ in many ways. A chart can name the insect you find.

3Butterflies are busy by day, mostly bright, skinny, and without fur. Most antennae end in knobs, and at rest the wings stay up. Each hind wing has an enlarged lobe so the wings can overlap. Moths are busy by night, mostly dull, fat, and furry. Antennae are plain or feathery, and at rest the wings lie flat at the sides. A tiny hook or bristle links forewing and hindwing during flight. Life Cycle. After a male and female mate, the female lays eggs on or near leaves, twigs, and flowers. The young then have food. Lobster moth egg. White butterfly eggs. A hatching swallowtail caterpillar leaves the egg as the larval stage opens. Butterflies and moths both have this stage. They chew, and one life may include food of 27,000 times the body weight.

4Egg case first, then the leaf or flower, then the remaining plant. Eating and growing are the work. Tight skin splits for new skin and a bigger body. Molting happens four or five times. Full size takes about two weeks. Danger comes from lizards, birds, bats, plus small mammals. Spines and hairs can spoil the taste. Colors and patterns can make them seem more dangerous. Some hide, blend in, or wait until night or until danger passes. Red horns on a swallowtail let out a strong odor that drives enemies off. Food plants can spoil the taste for birds and other enemies. Bright colors warn others away. Owl butterfly caterpillar. A pupa cannot move, so it cannot flee.

5Many chrysalises hide as a dead leaf or as bark. Poisonous bright ones signal bad food. A Puss moth cocoon matches bark and is missed. In the pupal stage a grown caterpillar slowly becomes an adult, seeming dead outside while changing inside. The back end sticks to a twig or a leaf or other support. Soft skin from the last molt hardens into a chrysalis. Moths rest underground, on the ground, or in a protective cocoon. Within hours, new mouth parts, legs, muscles, and tiny wings form. Weeks may pass. Metamorphosis is egg, larva, pupa, then adult. The adult breaks out with damp crumpled wings. Those wings must open, dry, and harden before flight. Growth ends at adult size. Photo: adult, pupa, and larva.

6Most adults live two weeks to two months, but a monarch can live nine to twelve months. Butterfly bush and lantana bring beautiful butterflies. A monarch drinks at butterfly bush. Honey and mashed banana on a trunk bring night moths. Use a flashlight. Adults vary in shape, size, and color. Butterflies may be bright, white, or light in color. Moths may be dull, but the luna moth has amazing colors or patterns. Flying birds and bats are enemies, so adults need care like caterpillars. Patterns help them blend in, and some copy leaves while others copy flowers. Eye spots can scare an enemy. Bright color warns that a monarch tastes bad. Buckeye wings stretch 4.2-7 cm (1.7-2.8 in). Range: North America, southern Canada at the north end, southern Mexico at the south end.

7Large eyespots in black, blue, and yellow look like a staring animal. A wing shake flashes them and may give one or two seconds to escape. The luna moth spans 7.5-11.3 cm (3-4.4 in). Range: eastern and plains parts of the United States and Canada. Pastel green wings have reddish-brown edges. Odd hind wings hide the resting moth among leaves. Only a few moths, this one included, skip adult food after a lifetime of eating as caterpillars. Adults seek a mate and lay eggs. A hollow tube, straw-like and curling at rest, takes in sweet flower nectar. They lack jaws, teeth, and solid food. Good sight and poor smell lead butterflies to land on bright flower clusters and sip each blossom.

8Butterfly flowers and moth flowers often differ in shape. Night moths find light colored sweet flowers by moonlight. Hovering is more common than landing. Petals make feeding easy. Legs, feet, antennae, mouth parts, and other spots hold sense organs that find food, mates, and egg plants. The map shows North America, the Pacific Ocean, resting monarchs, and migration routes. Before winter some migrate from cold homes to warm ones, and the monarch is best known. North American monarchs fly south when late summer or fall cools. West of the Rocky Mountains, several California coast sites are used. East of the Rockies, Mexico can be 4,800 kilometers away, which is 2,982 mi. Millions winter in trees, then go north in spring for egg plants. No one knows the method.

9Science and hobby collecting taught much, but millions of beautiful insects died. Watching flight and feeding is kinder, more interesting, and full of matches and differences. Photo them, or net them with no touching, then release. Books and the Internet show a favorite species. A butterfly garden can be built or visited, then noted in a journal. Hand oil hurts wings, and a soft touch can break a piece or rub off scales. Enjoy them freely. Few insects amaze more. Glossary. antennae 5. Head feelers for touch and smell. invertebrates 5. Animals with no backbone. larval 9. Stage right after hatching. caterpillars 9. Butterfly larvae and moth larvae. chrysalis 11. Stiff butterfly pupa case. metamorphosis 12. 10migrate 17. Place to place, cold to warm. exoskeleton 5. Outside hard shell on insects and relatives. molting 9. Shedding the whole skin. insects 5.
