40. New Year Around the World

1Red clothes are what a Chinese boy wears to celebrate the New Year. Berries are part of his celebration too. Two children in Japan greet the New Year side by side. Around the world, New Year celebrations fall at different times of year. The ways of celebrating are many. Parties are the choice of some people. Other people keep traditions that feel more unusual. Amazing fireworks go up at midnight in many cities. New Year's resolutions are promises many people make. A resolution can be a promise to stop a bad habit. It can be a promise to start a good habit. Cutting back on junk food might be one resolution. Being a better friend might be another. Ethiopia is a country in Africa. Eritrea is a country in Africa too.

2People there use a calendar that holds thirteen months. Enkutatash is the name of their New Year celebration. September 11 is the usual day for it. The new year signals that the rainy season has ended. Torches are held close together for the celebration. Grouped that way, the flames are meant to look like sunshine. A meal is shared by families and friends. Coffee is drunk. Gifts are shared. Daisies may be given by children. Paintings may be made by children. A girl in Ethiopia gathers daisies for the New Year. New Year's Eve may be only a small party. Family and friends may be the whole guest list. The United States also holds a giant gathering. About a million people meet in Times Square.

3That square is in New York City. The crowd counts the year's final seconds together. Lights cover a giant ball. The ball drops from a tall building. Cheering follows. Celebration follows too. Fireworks at midnight appear in many cities. New York City is among them. Inside the Times Square crowd, a young girl celebrates with her father. Thousands of flowers form the floats in a California parade. The parade is the Rose Parade, tied to the Rose Bowl. New Year's Day in the United States brings special games of football. California's Rose Bowl game is the most famous of them. The Rose Parade draws a large crowd before the game begins. A Polar Bear plunge is how many Canadians meet the New Year. They leap into freezing water on New Year's Day.

4The leap is one way to begin the year feeling fresh. People in the southern United States eat a dish named Hoppin' John. Rice and black-eyed peas make up the dish. Some believe a plain meal on New Year's Day may help the rest of the year. Spain is where the custom of eating grapes on New Year's Eve began. Central and South America keep a custom of twelve grapes that night. Each grape stands for a month in the year to come. Eating every grape is believed to bring good luck. The grapes are eaten in the year's final twelve seconds. Cleaning the house before midnight is another widely kept custom. Room for good luck is the aim of the cleaning.

5The old is swept away. A fresh year is welcomed in. Women in Uruguay toss water from a window. New Year luck is what they hope to bring. At midnight, some people sweep coins indoors. Riches in the new year are the hope behind the coins. In many countries, water thrown from a window says goodbye to bad luck. The act works as a symbol. Washing the old away is what it stands for. Welcoming a fresh start is what it stands for too. January gets its name from Janus. Janus was a god of ancient Rome. Doors, gateways, and beginnings were his charge. Two faces are how he is usually shown. One face looks back on the old year. One face looks ahead into the New Year.

6Russia is where a statue of Janus was made. Old broken dishes get smashed in Denmark. The pieces land on doorsteps of family and friends. Good luck for the New Year is the aim of the smash. Danish homes save broken dishes through the whole year. New Year's Eve is the night those dishes break. People in Finland melt tin as a way to guess the year ahead. Love or marriage is the meaning of a heart shape. Good luck is the meaning of a circle shape. A special cake is baked by people in Greece. A coin hides inside the cake. Luck in the coming year should belong to the person who gets that slice. Scotland also treats a clean house by midnight as important. After dark, people in Scotland carry torches.

7Long poles tipped with flame are swung to mark the New Year. A song is sung at midnight. The song remembers old friends. It sends the old year away. At a New Year event in Scotland, a man swings a ball of fire with care. A man from Scotland lifts a torch on New Year's Eve. The flame on it is large. Round objects in the Philippines stand for coins. They stand for good luck as well. Polka-dot clothes are worn by Filipino people as an invitation to luck. Twelve kinds of round fruit are eaten. Homes in the Philippines are cleaned before New Year's Eve. Windows are opened at midnight. An open window is an invitation for good luck to come in.

8Two girls in Cambodia pour water across a statue of Buddha. Late March or the middle of April is New Year time in parts of Southeast Asia. The dry season ends then. The days are very hot. The days are very dusty too. Water festivals belong to this time of year. Dusty statues of Buddha are washed with water as people prepare. That water is saved. Priests pour it over people's shoulders. Good luck is the reason for the pour. Songkran is the name of the water festival in Thailand. The usual dates run from April 13 through April 15. Water is sprayed on crowds by parade floats. Children chase each other. Water blasters are used by some. Water pails are used by others. New Year's Day in Thailand might easily reach 38 degrees Celsius.

9About 100 degrees Fahrenheit is the same heat. A water fight is a perfect celebration when the day is this hot. A boy blows a shofar, which is a horn from a ram. He honors the Jewish New Year. He does this at a school in the city of Los Angeles. The city is in California. Buffalo are herded in a parade in Indonesia. The parade marks the Islamic New Year. Some cultures do not keep the New Year on one fixed date. A lunar calendar sets the date of the Islamic New Year. The Moon's cycles are what a lunar calendar follows. A lunar year runs about eleven days shorter than a solar year. A solar year has 365 days.

10Earth's travel around the Sun is what the solar year follows. Both calendars are used for the Chinese New Year. Both calendars are used for the Jewish New Year too. Red clothes appear at a Chinese New Year parade in London, England. A dragon is held up high. Music is part of New Year joy for children in many lands. Dancing is part of that joy too. Many New Year traditions show up in more than one country. A fresh start is what most traditions help you make. Music and dancing turn that start into a fun celebration. Cleaning the house is among the most popular welcomes. Midnight fireworks are another popular welcome. Each person can pick a way to greet the New Year.

41. Paths That Help Animals Cross Roads

1Every year, Christmas Island in Australia shuts some roads. Red crabs can then cross without being hit. Danger on a road opens this book. Banff as a strong example comes next. Small animals get a turn after that. A question about cost follows. More reasons come after the question. A close and a word list finish the book. Road crossing is full of danger for a turtle. Imagine the turtle is you. A freeway hems you in on one side. Six lanes make up that freeway. Food waits across the pavement. Space to wander waits there too. A partner might be waiting as well. Metal giants roar past at high speed. Your own pace can never match theirs. A safe crossing is the puzzle. Being crushed is the fate to avoid.

2Roads put many animals in danger every day. Turtles are only one example. Crossing works out for some. It fails for others. People can build a way across that keeps animals safe. Critter crossings are the name for those ways. Ecopassages is the other name. Types differ from place to place. Staying alive is the shared aim for animals. Staying alive is the shared aim for humans too. Cars strike many moose each year. Death is the result for those moose. People can die in the same crashes. Four lanes make up the Trans-Canada Highway. Banff National Park has that highway through its middle. Canada is the country of the park. The park sits in North America. Its mountains are among the most beautiful there.

3The highway cuts through the park. Summer traffic can be huge. A single day may see more than 25,000 vehicles. Crashes used to pile up on that road. Animals and vehicles met in those crashes. Hundreds of them happened each year. Parks Canada then added crossings for animals. Underpasses were part of the build. Overpasses were part of it too. The total passed thirty. Future plans add still more. Millions of dollars was the cost. A hard question came with the cost. What if the animals did not use them? A young bobcat walks an underpass made for animals. Tracking uses more than one method. Cameras watch some of the crossings. A photo snaps each time an animal goes through. Other spots use pads for tracks. Soft dirt forms some pads.

4Sand forms others. Feet leave marks as animals go by. People read those marks. A rake then clears the pad. Overpasses carry many animals over the highway. A chart totals large mammals at these crossings. Twenty-three crossings are in the count. Banff National Park is the place. November 7, 1996 is the start date. March 31, 2009 is the end date. Overpass counts come first. Grizzly bears show 317. Black bears show 58. Wolves show 597. Cougars show 41. Coyotes show 319. Moose show 84. Deer show 10,377. Elk show 1,388. Underpass counts come next. Grizzly bears show 10. Black bears show 44. Wolves show 172. Cougars show 66. Coyotes show 66. Moose show 1. Deer show 636. Elk show 418. A black bear came once in that first year.

5A mountain lion came once in that first year. Animals now travel over the highway. They also travel under it. By 2011 the passes had a huge total. Animals had used them more than 220,000 times. Safety has returned for the spotted salamander, an endangered animal. Migration can happen safely now. Mating can happen safely now. Tunnels for this salamander began in Massachusetts. They were the first of their kind in the United States. Each year the animals leave the forest. A busy street lies on their route. Water is where they go to mate. Two slim tunnels pass beneath the road. Migration stays safe inside those tunnels. Florida built a wall with a lip on top. Small animals stay off the road because of the lip.

6A passage below lets them get across. Thousands of lives were the goal in Florida. Snakes were among those lives. Frogs were among those lives. Other small animals were included too. Walls rose along both sides of a road called U.S. 441. Height of each wall is 42 inches. The same height is 107 cm. A lip crowns each wall. Width of the lip is 6 inches. The same width is 15 cm. Climbing past that lip is beyond these small animals. The road surface stays empty of them. Tunnels below finish the trip to the far side. Millions of dollars is what these crossings take to build. Some people call that price too high. Look at crashes across the United States. A year brings more than one million of them.

7Vehicles and animals are what collide. Human deaths follow those crashes. The yearly toll is over 200 people. Bills from the crashes reach billions of dollars. Repairs take a share of the money. Cleanup takes a share. Hospital care takes a share. Extra animal crossings would cut the crash count. Banff offers a clear result. Vehicle deaths of animals fell there. The drop was more than 80 percent. Money is saved when collisions shrink. Human lives are saved too. Lives of many other animals are saved as well. A camera just outside an underpass catches a scene. A deer is in that scene. A camera inside an underpass catches another scene. A grizzly bear is crossing in that scene. Extinction is another threat these paths can block.

8Grizzly bears in Banff are very few. Only a few adult females, if lost to traffic, could have finished these bears inside the park. Passages removed that threat. A safe highway crossing is now open to the grizzlies. Other endangered animals gain even more from a safe path. A whole species may be kept alive. Food is another gift of ecopassages for large animals. Mountain lions travel far in a year while they hunt. Bears travel far too. Prey is what they are seeking. Hundreds of miles can be the distance. Habitats get sliced up by roads. Free movement can stop because of those slices. Food on the far side stays out of reach for some species. Survival grows less likely for them.

9Busy roads often stand in a mountain lion's way. The lion still has to hunt. A rare bighorn sheep rests beside her lamb. Mates are another need. Members of one species can be split apart by roads. Babies cannot come if a mate cannot be found. A species can vanish from a place when babies stop. The vanishing can be quick. Extinction of the species is possible in some cases. Be the turtle once more. Metal giants still roar past. An animal crossing exists this time. Your eyes drop. A tunnel comes into view. It runs under the road. You head toward that tunnel at a slow pace. One more day of safety is yours. Time on a road can be very long for some turtles.

10Two lanes may be all the road has. More than seven hours can pass before the far side is reached. The turtle in the picture has made it. Safety is waiting on that far side. A collision is a hard strike of one object into another. Endangered means dying out is a real danger. Extinction is what follows when a whole group is gone. Animals can be that group. Plants can be that group. A habitat is the natural home of a plant or an animal. To migrate is to shift from one region or habitat to another. A set time each year is when the shift happens. A vehicle is a nonliving thing used for moving. People can be the cargo. Goods can be the cargo.

42. Pictures That Carry a Message

1A red hand shows on this street sign. The hand tells people Don't Walk. A person who sees the sign can know the right action. Pictures by themselves can make a traffic signal clear. People used pictures for a very long time. Picture use came before written words. Picture languages have told stories. Picture languages have also given information. A picture can make a message clear to many people. Egyptian hieroglyphs are a famous kind of picture language. Mayan glyphs are a famous kind too. Those two are among the best-known picture languages. Some old Egyptian picture signs were set on cloth. People who travel in Egypt today see hieroglyphs in many places. Famous ruins hold lots of picture symbols. Workers carved the symbols on tall monuments. They carved them on temple walls too.

2Egyptians kept this writing for almost three thousand years. The span runs from 3100 BCE to 332 CE. Egyptians believed written language was sacred. They believed the gods gave language as a gift. The ruler had a duty to use that gift. Scribes who served the king shared the duty. People called the king a pharaoh. The work of writing was hard. A scribe's study list was huge. The number of symbols was over seven hundred. People called the symbols hieroglyphs. Sons of scribes went to a special scribe school. School began at age six or seven. Becoming a scribe took several years. A young scribe had to learn the right use of each glyph. A young scribe also had to learn the right place for each glyph.

3The word hieroglyph comes from Greek. The Greek word is hieroglyphikos. That word means sacred carving. Egyptian hieroglyphs came in two types. One type stood for a whole word. A duck can be shown as one whole word. Walk can be a whole word too. Sun can be a whole word too. The other type stood for sounds. Sound glyphs worked like alphabet letters. Other glyphs stood for several letters at once. The sound sh is one example. This sound type included twenty-four glyphs. A modern scroll can show the Egyptian hieroglyphic alphabet. The scroll pairs each character with its letter sound. Hieroglyphs cover the walls of ancient Egyptian temples. A message could run in rows. A message could also run in columns. Reading moved from the right toward the left.

4A special mark existed for words about the king. Marks for the pharaoh sat inside a set shape. Marks for his family sat inside that shape too. The shape might be a rectangle. The shape might be an oval. People read that shape as the flat Earth. It also stood for the Sun moving over the Earth. That shape displayed the pharaoh's power over all. One stone carving carries a pharaoh's name. The oldest hieroglyphs date to around 3100 BCE. People carved glyphs on tiny clay tags. Later, people carved hieroglyphs into stone. Those stone marks shared important news. The carving was often done on a large stone slab. People called the slab a stela. Tombs also received carved hieroglyphs. Cutting glyphs into stone took a great deal of work.

5Later, scribes picked up reed pens and ink. The glyphs went onto paper of a special kind. Pen and ink made the work easier than carving. Scribes could record information at a faster pace. Stone carvings of hieroglyphs grew less common. One stela belongs to an Egyptian pharaoh. People found the Cup of Immortality in a pharaoh's tomb. Real hieroglyphs can be viewed with no trip to Egypt. Big-city museums put Egyptian writing on display. In some cities, a short walk is enough. Paris, France, keeps an Egyptian obelisk in a public square. The stone is three thousand years old. Egypt gave this obelisk to France in 1833. People call it the Egyptian Luxor Obelisk. It stands in Paris, France. An obelisk is a stone pillar with four sides. The pillar narrows to a pyramid shape at the top.

6A wall in Palenque, Mexico, carries Mayan glyphs. Palenque is a Mayan city that has been preserved. Use of Egyptian hieroglyphs was falling. At the same time, another picture-writing system was growing. The Maya are a people. They lived in southern Mexico. They lived in northern Central America. They still live in those lands today. Only 300 BCE is the date of the oldest Mayan glyphs. This people believed writing was a gift from the gods. Egyptians had held the same belief. Writing was sacred to the Maya. Only scribes could learn to read. Only scribes could learn to write. Only royalty could learn as well. Mayan glyphs were complex. They were also beautiful. About 800 Mayan glyphs were in use. Egyptian hieroglyphs had been split into two groups.

7The Maya also sorted their glyphs into two groups. Most Mayan glyphs stood for a whole word. About 150 glyphs worked as prefixes. Those same glyphs could work as suffixes. The Maya had no alphabet of letter glyphs. Mayan numbers are built from three symbols. One symbol has a shell shape. One symbol is a dot. One symbol is a bar. Writers made Mayan words by setting glyphs inside a square. A cluster of glyphs worked like the words of a sentence. Two side-by-side columns held the squares. A reader went from left to right. The reader then moved down in a zigzag. Calendar glyphs appear on carved stone at a Mayan temple in Mexico. Scribes took pride in the design of each square.

8They wanted the work to stand out. They set the glyphs in a way that looked pleasing. Each message had its own beauty. Two scribes could write the same message in different ways. That difference made the reading a bit hard. Pottery shows the god Itzamna. Itzamna was the Mayan god of creation. Tribal kings depended on Mayan scribes. Egyptian scribes had been important to a pharaoh in a similar way. Scribes made books for use in religious ceremonies. Mayan writing often recorded a win in battle. Glyphs were carved into the stone walls of monuments. Temples received the same kind of carving. The messages told of the ruler's successes. Rulers used these displays to show power. A picture can show a Mayan scribe at work. Many Mayan writings dealt with timekeeping.

9The Maya watched the stars. Their calendar held 365 days. The year was split into eighteen months. Each of those months had 20 days. Five days were left at the end. People called those leftover days wayeb. People believed the wayeb days were unlucky. Scribes had the job of posting these messages where the public could see them. The job was easy when the king had won a battle. The job was also safe in that case. The job was not easy when the king had lost. The winners took from the losing scribes any power to write. Strong examples of Mayan glyphs still stand at sites in the Yucatan Peninsula. Mayan glyphs tell stories, as Egyptian hieroglyphs do. The stories are about people from long ago. The picture language of the ancient Maya is no longer used.

10The picture language of the ancient Egyptians is no longer used. Other ancient picture languages are still used today. Some Asian languages use thousands of pictographic symbols. Chinese is one of those languages. Japanese is one of them. Korean is one of them. Picture languages exist in modern times too. Plain block glyphs show up on signs for travelers who cross borders. The symbols point travelers to the place for bags. The symbols also point them to a bus. Travelers may come from any part of the world. A glyph sign is easier than a sign written in many languages. One picture can do the work of a dozen words. Picture signs help people travel with ease all over the world.

43. Reefs Built by Coral

1A reef gives animals many good places to hide. A moray eel is one animal that hides there. This book opens with an introduction. Next it explains what coral is. Then it describes a busy community under the water. It tells where coral reefs grow. It explains dangers that threaten the reefs. It also tells how people try to protect them. A word list comes at the end. Many kinds of coral live on a reef. Fish live there. Other ocean animals live there too. Tropical oceans on Earth hold places of wonder and beauty. People know these places as coral reefs. Each reef is a huge community under the sea. Thousands of living things make it home. By day and by night, animals hunt. They eat. They try not to be eaten. They need one another for food. They need one another for shelter. They need one another for safety. Almost one-fourth of ocean plants and ocean animals live near coral reefs. Fish make the reef home. Sea turtles do too. Sea stars do too. Many other animals stay there.

2Seabirds come to visit. Dolphins come to visit too. At a quick look, coral can seem like rock with a strange shape. It can also seem like a plant. Corals are really tiny animals. The name for one is polyp. You say that name PAH-lips. A pea is bigger than most polyps. One coral branch can hold thousands of polyps. A coral mound can hold thousands too. Each polyp has a body like a tube. The mouth has tiny tentacles. A polyp works like a machine made for eating. At night, the tentacles open. They catch animals that are very small. Hundreds of coral kinds live in the ocean. Most fit into one of two groups. Hard corals are also called stony corals. They build hard skeletons. Over many years, millions of those skeletons pile up. The pile becomes a reef. A polyp settles on a surface. It stays in that same spot for its whole life. Elkhorn coral is one hard coral. A real elk grows antlers. It does not grow horns. Most stony corals live with algae.

3You say that name AL-jee. Algae are simple relatives of plants. They make food for the corals. They make food for many reef fish too. A soft coral can bend when water moves. Soft corals often look like trees. They can look like bushes. They can look like other plants. Algae live inside some soft corals. They do not live inside every soft coral. A sea fan is a soft coral. Its shape helps it catch tiny bits of food. In the Red Sea of Egypt, golden butterflyfish swim past soft corals. Red bigeye fish pass by in a school as well. Many people picture bright fish when they picture a coral reef. They are right to do that. Thousands of fish kinds live on reefs. The reef gives them food. It also shields them from animals that want to eat them. Shrimp find meals at a reef. Lobsters find meals there. Crabs do too. A sea star catches small animals. Those animals hide among the coral branches. A sea anemone looks like a flower. It waits until the next meal drifts by.

4A coral reef supports thousands of kinds of life. Reefs matter in other ways too. They help guard a coast from storms. They help guard a coast from floods. They hold soil so it does not wash away. That hold protects the coast from erosion. Reefs feed animals that supply a large share of the world's seafood. Reefs give those animals shelter too. Reefs help many countries earn money. They help those countries feed people. People use reef plants in medicines. They use reef animals in medicines too. A reef in the Caribbean Sea helps keep the land from harm. Scientists have already made medicines from reef life. More medicines are likely to come. Corals make a substance that treats asthma. The same substance treats arthritis. Medicines for several kinds of cancer come from reefs too. Scientists also study reef animals for new treatments. They look for help with infections. They look for help with skin problems. A map of the coral reef zone shows North America, Asia, and Europe. It shows the Atlantic Ocean and the Pacific Ocean.

5It shows Africa and the equator. It shows South America, the Indian Ocean, and Australia. It shows Antarctica too. The map marks reefs, land, and ocean. NOAA made the map in 2017. Most coral reefs lie near the equator. Warm tropical seas near land are where most reefs grow. Reefs also grow where warm currents leave the tropics. Florida is one of those places. Coral grows best when the water is warm. The best range is 21 to 29 degrees Celsius. That is 70 to 84 degrees Fahrenheit. Sunlight is what algae use when they make food. For that reason, most corals live near the surface. Reefs come in three kinds. A fringing reef sits close to shore. It usually follows the natural edge of the land. That edge is called the fringe. A barrier reef has a wide, deep stretch of water between the reef and the shore. The largest barrier reef on Earth is the Great Barrier Reef. It lies in Australia. It stretches 2,300 kilometers. That length is 1,429 miles.

6The Great Barrier Reef is six thousand to eight thousand years old. It is built from more than three thousand reefs and islands. Fish of nearly two thousand kinds live in that reef. An atoll is the third kind of reef. Its shape is a circle. It forms around the rim of an old island that is sinking. It can form around an island volcano. In time, the island sinks. Or the volcano breaks apart. Then a pool of water called a lagoon fills the middle. Look in the Indian Ocean for the country called the Maldives. Twenty-six atolls make up that country. Pollution can kill a coral reef quickly. Water that is too warm can kill a reef quickly too. Reefs are fragile. Many forces can damage them. A huge tsunami wave has the power to break a reef. You say tsunami as soo-NAH-mee. Even so, people cause most of the harm. Many scientists think pollution helps cause coral diseases. A disease can destroy a whole reef in a few weeks. Warmer seas from global warming play a part too.

7Global warming is a climb in how warm Earth's air and oceans are, on average. The rise is big enough to change weather patterns that last a long time. Today, human actions cause most of this warming. One action is clearing land so people can farm. Another is burning coal, oil, and gas. Those actions make a great deal of carbon dioxide gas. The gas mixes into the air that surrounds Earth. It holds heat near Earth's surface. Global warming brings many problems. More bad storms are one problem. Danger to natural communities is another. Scientists expect the problems to grow. Those problems will grow unless people cut way back on the actions that release so much of that gas. Bleaching is another problem. It also starts when ocean water gets too warm. Inside the polyps, the algae change. The change harms the polyps. The polyps push the algae out so they can stay safe. Most of their food comes from the algae. Without that food, the polyps start to starve. They lose their color too.

8They look bleached. Reefs in many parts of the world are turning white because of bleaching. A coral can sometimes heal after bleaching. If the cause keeps going, the corals die. The reef dies with them. Bleaching now touches three-fourths of coral reefs. Human actions have put too much carbon dioxide into Earth's oceans. The extra gas makes the water more acidic. Because of that, corals cannot take in one chemical they need. That chemical keeps the skeleton healthy. Without it, the skeleton dissolves. People harm reefs in still more ways. Some fishing uses poisons. Some fishing uses explosives. Those methods have wiped out more than half of the reefs in the Philippines. Building along a coast causes soil to erode. It causes pollution too. People also break chunks off a reef. Sometimes the break is an accident. Sometimes it is on purpose. Boaters have damaged the elkhorn coral in the picture. Many countries have started to protect reefs nearby. In some places, the law says you may not take fish. You may not take plants.

9You may not take coral. People must stay away from many fragile reefs. Boats must stay away too. Laws like these are only a beginning. Scientists raise healthy coral pieces in nurseries. Some pieces were saved after a boat hit the reef. Some were saved before a building project on the coast. Some pieces hang from bars. They may set the pieces in baskets instead. Later, they move the pieces onto hurt reefs. The new pieces help those reefs grow healthy again. Each year, divers who volunteer help clean reefs. In Indonesia, people plant mangrove trees. The trees help protect coral reefs close by. People have already destroyed half of the coral reefs on Earth. Most reefs that are left are in trouble. Scientists are working to help. We can help too. You can help even if you live far from any ocean. You can help slow global warming. Using less energy helps. Recycling helps. Walking more helps. Riding a bike more helps. If we help, reefs can remain a beautiful wonder of nature. They can still be here many years from now.

10How could you help? Here are word meanings from the book. Bleaching means something loses its color. White coral whose algae have died is one example. Erosion is the natural carrying away of rock or soil. Water, wind, or ice can do the carrying. Fragile means easy to damage or break. Delicate means the same thing. Carbon dioxide is a gas you cannot see. It is created when coal, gas, or similar fuels burn or break apart. A skeleton is a hard frame. It supports and protects the body of some animals. Tropical describes the region around the equator. That region has a climate that is hot and humid. A coral reef is an underwater ridge in warm seawater. The ridge is built from outer skeletons. Those skeletons come from tiny ocean animals called polyps. A tsunami is a set of huge ocean waves that destroy things. An underwater earthquake can start one. A landslide or a volcanic eruption can start one too. To dissolve is to break down, often after touching a liquid.

44. Rolling Through the Nation

1This book opens with an introduction. The next part tells about early travel. Then the pages explain how people build cars. Another part names companies that make cars. Later pages tell problems that automobiles cause. The book then looks at cars in the years ahead. A list of word meanings comes at the end. Cars and trucks fill the world. Their number is more than 600 million. Even a tiny town in a tiny country has them. The United States alone holds more than 200 million cars and trucks. Automobiles make up most of those vehicles. This nation gets around on wheels. Crowded traffic fills the streets of New York City. People have always had to get from place to place. Walking was the first way to travel. People walked even when the distance was long. Later, people tamed animals so they could ride. They taught some animals to pull wagons.

2Horses helped on those trips. Camels helped too. Elephants helped people travel. Those same animals carried loads. Picture a ride to school on an elephant. Animal travel beat walking. Those trips were still slow. In India, elephants still carry people. They still carry goods there too. A camel taxi works in India as well. It takes riders to the place they choose. Inventors wanted a wheeled machine for carrying people. They wanted the machine to carry people faster. They planned an engine to run it. Some engines used steam. Other engines used electricity. Neither kind worked well enough. In the late 1880s, people built engines that ran on gasoline. People also call that fuel gas. Those engines were small. They were powerful. They let travelers go faster. They also let travelers go farther. Most cars today use gas engines.

3One early steam vehicle has a place for steering. It has a seat for the driver. It has a steam engine. It rolls on three wheels. That steam vehicle was built in 1770. Did people say car before they said automobile? People first used car in 1301. The word named a Celtic war chariot. A man from France first used automobile in 1883. He used it to name electric cars. How do people use the two words now? In 1896, Henry Ford sat in the first car he had built. The earliest cars looked nothing like cars on the road today. They looked like carriages with the horses gone. People called them horseless carriages. At first, workers made each car by hand. One car took a long time. A handmade car cost a lot of money. Not every person could pay that price.

4People searched for a faster way to manufacture cars. They also wanted a lower cost. In 1896, one early company finished only 13 cars during the whole year. By 1899, a company could finish over 2,000 cars. The Model T count from Henry Ford's company passed 15 million. That work lasted almost twenty years. The Ford Motor Company began in 1903 under Henry Ford. He started it in Detroit, Michigan. Detroit soon became the main center for making cars. Its famous car had the name Model T. Ford wanted a lower price on each car. Then more people could buy one. He had to manufacture a larger number of cars. He also had to make them faster. Ford studied every way to speed the work. The assembly line was his invention in 1913. The new way made each car faster. It also made each car cheaper.

5Soon, other companies built cars the same way. Buying a car became possible for almost everyone. In 1929, the roads held more than 3.5 million cars. So many cars were moving that people had to add more roads. Later cars include a 1940 Chrysler Royal Sedan. A 1957 Chevrolet Bel Air is another. So is a 1965 Ford Thunderbird. Along an assembly line, builders add one part and then the next until the car is whole. Machines pull cars that are not finished yet. Unfinished cars travel in a row from one end of the factory to the other. Workers add parts while each car moves along. The car is finished at the last stop. For years, people did every job on the line. Now robots, a kind of machine, often handle those jobs. These robots are not the robots you see in movies.

6They are special machines. They move the way an arm and a hand move. A robot can keep working and does not grow tired. Pictures show one of the first assembly lines. They also show an assembly line from today. For many years, three large companies have built cars inside the United States. Those companies are General Motors, Chrysler, and Ford. After cars first reached the streets, American companies led the world in production. In the 1960s and the 1970s, Toyota began to sell a different kind of car. Toyota is a company from Japan. The cars were built to last a longer time. They broke down less often than most cars made in America. American makers still sold the most cars then. In 2007, that pattern changed. Toyota sold the largest number of cars and trucks of any company. No company from another country had done that before.

7Japan's Toyota is not alone in building cars beyond the United States. Volkswagen is brought in from Germany. BMW is imported from Germany too. Among foreign sellers, Japan leads in how many cars reach the United States. South Korea has sent very high numbers as well. A 2007 chart lists U.S. carmakers and brands. Chrysler makes the Chrysler, the Dodge, and the Jeep. Ford makes the Ford, the Lincoln, the Mercury, the Mazda, and the Volvo. General Motors appears with Cadillac and Chevrolet. The same U.S. group includes Pontiac and Hummer. It also includes Saturn, Buick, Saab, and GMC. A 2007 chart also lists foreign carmakers and brands. BMW makes the BMW, the MINI, and the Rolls-Royce. Volkswagen makes the Audi, the Bentley, and the Bugatti. Volkswagen also makes the Lamborghini, the Skoda, and the SEAT. Honda makes the Honda and the Acura.

8Nissan makes the Nissan and the Infiniti. Other foreign names on the chart are Toyota, Daihatsu, and Scion. Hyundai, Hino, Lexus, and Kia are on that chart too. Automobiles help people. They can create serious trouble too. Exhaust is burning gas that leaves through a car's tailpipe. That gas enters the air. Unhealthy chemicals then mix into the air people breathe. Those chemicals can make people sick. Car companies work each year to lower the chemicals coming from their cars. Have you ever been stuck in a traffic jam? In a jam, the cars sit still. You cannot go anywhere. Too many cars are on the roads today. That crowd causes problems. Remember that about 200 million cars are in the United States. That total is almost as high as the number of people. Cars have increased in number faster than people have.

9Years ago, most cars did a poor job of keeping people safe in a crash. Today, special parts help protect riders. Cars have seat belts. Babies ride in safety seats. Safety seats are also for small children. Newer cars have large airbags. In a crash, a big airbag inflates. Inflate means the bag fills with air. What new idea could keep riders safer still? Car companies are also testing new kinds of engines. One kind of car is called a hybrid. A hybrid runs on a gas engine together with an electric motor. A hybrid uses less gas. Less gas burned could mean cleaner air. Carmakers check airbags with crash test dummies. The dummies look like humans. A company called ZAP built the electric cars in the picture. Those cars help cut bad chemicals in the air. This nation will always travel from one place to another.

10Will the trips always roll on wheels? How will future cars look? How will future cars work? Maybe your ideas will shape what comes next. Here are word meanings from the book. An automobile is a road vehicle that has four wheels and an engine. A chemical is a substance that a chemical process makes or uses. Distance is how much space lies between two places. An engine uses moving parts and power so that something moves. Expensive means the price is high. Foreign describes something from another country or another language. Imported goods are items bought after they come from another country or state. An inventor creates, plans, or builds something nobody had made yet. To manufacture is to make finished goods, often in a factory. Production means putting resources together so a product can be sold.

45. Salmon and the Chain of Living Things

1Contents. A Chain of Life. Eggs. Alevins and Fry. Smolts. Adults. Spawners. A Closing. Word Meanings. Index. A Chain of Life. Picture a chain. Each link is joined to the next link. Break one link, and every other link is affected. Salmon are links inside many food chains. In each stage of life, a salmon eats. In each stage, other living things may eat the salmon. Follow the food chain of an adult salmon. The chain begins with plankton. Plankton are tiny living organisms. Small fish eat the plankton. Herring are small fish of this kind. Salmon then eat the herring. A human may eat a salmon. A bear may eat one. A killer whale may eat one. A seal may eat one. An eagle may eat one. Harm to one living thing spreads through a food chain. Harm also reaches the rest of the living things linked there.

2People sometimes catch fish so they can eat. Bears catch fish for eating too. A large share of a salmon's life is spent in streams. A large share is spent in rivers too. Salmon reproduce only at the end of life. Salmon are travelers. Life begins in a stream or a river. Then they migrate far out into the ocean. At last they return to that very same place. They return in order to spawn. Spawn means to reproduce. Salmon are transformers. Body shape changes many times in one life. Color changes many times too. This book explains the salmon life cycle. It explains links between salmon and other animals. It explains links between salmon and their environment. Eggs. A salmon begins as a tiny egg. The egg is laid in gravel. That gravel lies in a riverbed or a streambed. Salmon need cold water before they can lay eggs.

3They need clean water too. They need it so the eggs can be fertilized. Water that is too warm may kill the salmon. Death can come before the eggs are laid. Salmon take oxygen out of the water. Polluted water can be low in oxygen. Then a salmon cannot breathe. The salmon will die. Water is a salmon's environment. If the water is harmed, the salmon are harmed. The eggs they lay are harmed too. A close view shows a salmon egg. An eye is visible in the egg. Thousands of eggs may come from one salmon during one laying. A food sac is fixed to a newly hatched salmon. The sac gives the young fish what it needs for growth. These fish are salmon fry. Alevins and Fry. Hatching comes after a wait of about two weeks. The tiny salmon that come out are called alevins.

4Alevin is said AL-uh-vins. Their food hangs on the body inside a sac. Once that sac is empty, stripes grow. Fish with those stripes are called fry. Fry eat plankton. If plankton are too few, the fry starve. Plankton must have clean water. They must have cool water too. Plankton form one link in the food chain. If that link is upset, the fry are upset too. The picture shows plankton under a microscope. They have been magnified many times. Salmon color shifts from brown to silver. Silver coloring hides them in the ocean. Smolts. An amazing journey starts as fry change. Brown becomes shiny silver. The fish turn into smolts. A smolt is the next stage in the life cycle. Smolts eat insects. Too few insects leave the smolts short of energy. Then they cannot finish migrating to the sea. In a food chain, insects form some of the links.

5If insects are upset, the salmon are upset too. The smolts travel downstream. They come to the zone where river water meets the ocean. That zone is called an estuary. In an estuary, fresh water mixes with salt water. The mix is called brackish water. Smolts need an estuary with the right mix. The estuary prepares their bodies for the ocean. Water in the ocean is salty. If that mix is wrong, those body changes cannot happen. Then the smolts cannot survive in the sea. Labels on the picture say fresh water. They say brackish water. They say salt water. The shore is the meeting line of river water and sea water. Chemicals have polluted some estuaries. Fish absorb those chemicals. The chemicals can harm a fish. They can kill a fish. A smolt may absorb pollution into its body. A bird may eat that smolt afterward.

6The bird becomes poisoned. The food chain includes smolts. If smolts are upset, the birds are upset too. Water is tied to the fish. Fish are tied to the plankton. Plankton are tied to the insects. Insects are tied to the birds. A fish may hold chemicals in its body. A bird could get sick after eating that fish. Red salmon, adult male. Red salmon, adult female. Do You Know? Some scientists think salmon learn a scent by heart. The scent belongs to their own river. They follow the scent to find home. Adults. Inside the estuary, smolts develop into adult salmon. Then the adults scatter into the open ocean. Time in the ocean lasts from one year up to seven years. They feed on smaller fish. They travel thousands of miles. When it is time to reproduce, they make a long swim back.

7They return to the river where they hatched. These salmon will become spawners. Red salmon, breeder male. Red salmon, breeder female. Spawners. As adults return to fresh water, the shiny silver color fades. Males change to bright red. They change to green as well. A hooked nose grows. Large teeth grow too. Females turn a dull red. Males push against powerful currents. Females push against them too. The swim goes upstream. Spawners do not eat on this voyage. They burn fat stored in the body. The fat was stored from ocean feeding. The trip upstream is dangerous. Bears favor spawners as food. Eagles favor them too. Bears can be seen fishing for salmon. They fish along the banks of rivers. They fish along the banks of estuaries. Bears must carry a large store of fat in the body. Fat helps them last through winter. The skin of a salmon holds lots of fat.

8If salmon are plentiful, bears eat only the skin. The skin is nutritious. They leave the rest of the fish. A bear is catching a salmon. Bald eagles like to nest near salmon streams. A nearby stream keeps food close. Eagles love salmon too. An eagle may catch a salmon itself. Or it may eat what a bear left behind. Humans eat salmon as well. One link of a food chain is the salmon. If salmon are upset, bears are upset. Eagles are upset. Humans are upset too. Humans have built dams in many rivers. A dam keeps spawners from getting home. Then they cannot spawn. When the number of eggs drops, the number of new salmon drops too. Birds get less food. Bears get less food. Humans get less food. Spawners live with water as part of their surroundings. If water is upset, salmon are upset.

9Bears are upset. Birds are upset. Humans are upset too. Most salmon spawn only one time in life. Nutrients from salmon bodies enter the stream. The nutrients help the life cycle begin again. Once spawners reach home, the females lay eggs. The males fertilize the eggs. About a week later, the salmon die. Their bodies are not wasted. Birds eat the bodies. Bears eat the bodies. Other animals eat the bodies too. Nutrients in the spawners' flesh feed plankton. Nutrients in their bones feed plankton too. A Closing. Salmon are amazing. Life starts in a riverbed or a streambed. The fish travel to an ocean. They return to the place where they were born. At each stage of the journey, the body transforms. Many food chains depend on salmon as key links. At each stage of life, a salmon sits in food chains that are not the same.

10What happens to salmon affects other living things in those chains. Very large growth comes to some salmon types. They can reach 135 pounds. That is about 61 kg. Word Meanings. alevins: baby salmon with a food sac on the underbelly. brackish: fresh water mixed with salt water. estuary: the river zone where fresh water mixes into the ocean's salt water. fertilize: to join male cells with female cells. The joining can create a new animal. It can create a new plant. food chains: plants and animals linked by what they eat. fry: young river salmon. interconnected: connected to many things in many ways. plankton: tiny animals. A microscope is needed to see them. smolts: young salmon that leave a river and head for the ocean. spawn: to reproduce and to lay eggs. Index. eggs. fresh water. red salmon. reproduce. streambed.

46. Shirley Chisholm Steps Forward

1In 1968, Chisholm spoke to people at a housing project. The city was New York, New York. Shirley Chisholm's history-making speech came on January 25, 1972. She stood in a church in Brooklyn. She told the people there that she was a candidate. The prize she sought was the Democratic nomination. The office was the presidency. The country was the United States of America. Loud cheers filled the church. She went on with the speech. She said she was not a candidate for Black America alone. She is Black. She feels pride in that. She said she was not a candidate for the women's movement alone. She is a woman. She feels equal pride in that. She said she stood as the candidate of America's people. She said her presence stood for a new era. That era belonged to American political history. She was the first woman to seek that Democratic nomination for president. No Black candidate before her had sought the nomination of any major party.

2American politics entered a new time. A photo shows her on the campaign trail in 1972. A photo from 1930 shows a line of people waiting for food. The city is New York, New York. The time is the Great Depression. Shirley Chisholm's birthplace was Brooklyn, New York. She was born in 1924. Her mother had come from Barbados. Barbados is a Caribbean island. Her father had come from Guyana. South America was her father's home region. During part of childhood, Shirley was sent away from Brooklyn. Her sisters were sent too. They lived with their grandmother on Barbados. The Great Depression was the setting for that stay. Money was scarce across the United States in that period. The parents remained in Brooklyn. They worked through the hard years. Later they brought the children home. The Caribbean Sea holds Barbados. The island is small. Close to three hundred thousand people live there. Bridgetown is a place on the island.

3The island lies near North America. It also lies near South America. Shirley liked the farm her grandmother kept. Caring for the animals became one of her lessons. The sisters gave food to the chickens. They collected eggs. They watched so the sheep would not run off. Shirley could read before her fifth birthday. A few years after that, Brooklyn became her home for good. New York was still a struggle for her parents. They still did all they could for the children. Shirley did well as a student. Hard study filled her school years. Strong grades in high school opened a door. The door led to Brooklyn College. Even in college, helping people was the aim she named. Teaching became the work she chose. Her skill at speaking was well known. Debates were a place she did especially well. One teacher heard her speak. What he saw impressed him deeply. He said politics should be her path.

4At the time, sexist views shut many doors. Racist views shut doors too. Those views kept many Black women out of politics. Black students were barred from social clubs at Brooklyn College. The clubs ran many social activities at the school. Parties were one activity. Dances were another. Other events filled the calendar too. She chose to build a social club of her own. Over time, the clubs that had been all white changed their rules. Black students were then allowed to join. A photo shows her with the Brooklyn College social club. Another picture shows Brooklyn, New York, around 1946. She finished college in 1946. Her next work was teaching at a nursery school in Brooklyn. The community showed her many things that were not fair. She wondered why so much discrimination existed. She wondered why medical care was so hard to afford. She wondered why good schools were so hard for parents to find. She saw that every party leader was white.

5Most of the people around her were people of color. Not one Black politician served Brooklyn. Her mind was set. She would do whatever she could to improve life in Brooklyn. A 1968 photo shows people who served in the New York State government. Chisholm joined groups that were already at work. Those groups wanted social change. She knew the neighborhood needed that change. In the early 1960s, she started attending meetings. The meetings belonged to the Democratic Party. Every neighbor needed someone who would speak up. Problems would stay unheard if the voices stayed silent. She decided the change would have to come from her. The year 1964 was her first race for a government seat. That race was for New York's state legislature. The district already voted Democratic. The win came with ease. She was the only Black woman serving in that legislature. A 1968 photo shows her talking with voters. The place is Brooklyn, New York.

6A smaller photo shows the oath of office. The oath took place in 1969 in Washington, D.C. In office, she tried to send help to people with the greatest need. One bill she wrote offered scholarships. Students of color were the ones who would receive them. Another bill aimed to help workers. The workers were people who had lost jobs. Four years after the state race, she chose a bigger contest. The year was 1968. The contest was a seat in the U.S. Congress. Anger met the decision. Some of the anger came from her own party. Congress had never had a Black woman in it. Critics said a Black woman could not run for Congress. She paid those words no mind. She campaigned with all her strength. The election went to her. Once in Congress, she defended the causes she believed in. Racial equality was one cause. Gender equality was another. She worked to improve daily life in communities with low incomes.

7People began to call her Fighting Shirley. The name fit a politician who was tough. It fit a politician who was determined. A goal she believed in was a goal she would not drop. Fifteen years of her work went toward a national holiday. The day would honor Doctor Martin Luther King Jr. In 1983, that holiday became law. Healthy families were another aim. She helped start a program for food and learning. The name is Women, Infants, and Children. People also call it WIC. Families in need received food from the program. They also received lessons about nutrition. Millions of American families have been helped by it. The help has gone on since the program started. A photo shows her holding a petition. The petition asked that January 15 become a national holiday. The day would honor Doctor Martin Luther King Jr. National troubles bothered her in Congress. Local troubles in Brooklyn had bothered her earlier.

8She felt too many people were being passed over. She wanted her voice to be theirs. Her decision in 1972 was to run for president. Anger rose once more. Critics said a Black woman could not run for the presidency. Again, she refused to listen. A young volunteer joined the trail of her presidential campaign. Putting on a campaign can cost a great deal. Travel was a cost most candidates had to pay. Ads were a cost. Helpers were a cost. She would have to carry her message with far less money. In 1972, she stood with other people who were running for president. The setting was a show on television. Television debates were one path for a message to reach the public. The organizers would not let her join those debates. They said she had no real chance to win. She pushed to be included. At last she received time to speak. The speech went out on television. Some people who heard her found strong reasons to give her a vote.

9She spoke about needs that many politicians of the time ignored. People in need were part of that message. Older people were part of it. People of color were part of it. Veterans were part of it. Farm workers were part of it. A poster from the 1972 campaign carried her message. She described a vision of a new America. In the country she described, every person would have equality. Thousands of loyal volunteers joined because of that vision. Many of the volunteers were young. They came to rallies. They hung posters. They walked from door to door. They spoke with voters. She did not win the nomination for president. Even so, she made a statement with real power. Every person has a right to join in politics. A person's background does not take that right away. She went back to Congress after the race. Her years there lasted until 1983.

10A 1980 photo shows her beside other Black members of Congress. A 1970 photo shows Chisholm beside her staff. The office is in Washington, D.C. She did not expect the nomination to be hers. A book she wrote explains why she ran. The book was published in 1973. The title is The Good Fight. She wrote that someone had to go first. She said the country tells people that anyone may run for president. She said that promise had never really been true. She wanted the campaign to inspire other people. A later day was the future she hoped to open. On that day, no aim would sit out of reach. Any person could try for it. In 2020, a new office went to Kamala Harris. Forty-eight years had passed since Chisholm's run. Harris was the first Black woman in the vice president's office. Chisholm's death came in 2005. The work she left will be remembered for generations.

47. Stone Ridges and a Sea of Grass

1A map names North Dakota, Montana, and Minnesota. It also names South Dakota, Wyoming, Iowa, and Nebraska. The map marks the Ben Reifel Visitor Center. It marks the White River Visitor Center. On a map, the park has a strange, thin shape. Three units make up the park. That is why the outline looks odd. Water cut the Badlands. Wind cut them too. Those same forces still work hard there today. This book asks what is good about the Badlands. It describes the park. It describes the rocks and the grass. It describes the animals. It tells how to plan a visit. A list of hard words comes at the end. Grass bends in the breeze. Far off, bison eat. A prairie dog makes a squeak. Then the prairie dog drops into a hole. Past that point, the grass stops. The ground falls open.

2Bare ridges and peaks of rock aim at the sky. The ground looks dry. The ground looks brown. At times the Sun hits at just the right angle. Then the rocks glow with amazing color. The grass fields glow too. Badlands National Park is a strange place. It is also an amazing place. The park lies in the land now called South Dakota. Native American groups of many kinds have lived here for a very long time. They hold this land to be special. An Oglala Lakota man rides across family land. The land is part of the Pine Ridge Indian Reservation. Lakota life near the park goes back more than two hundred years. They call the place mako sica. The name sounds like mah-KOH SHEE-chah. In their language, the words mean land that is bad. Scientists use the word badlands as well.

3For them, it means soft rock that weather has worn away. In 1978, people made this land a national park. About one million people visit in a year. Three areas form the park. Most visitors go to the North Unit. The other two areas lie inside the Pine Ridge Indian Reservation. The Oglala Lakota people live at Pine Ridge. Part of the park sits near Buffalo Gap National Grassland. Part of it wraps around that grassland. Side by side, they guard a very large space. Wild plants stay safe there. Animals stay safe there. The land stays safe there. Prairie grass blankets Buffalo Gap National Grassland. Colored bands of rock in the hills tell how the land formed. Scientists have used those bands to read the park's geological past. A geologist studies rocks, soil, and land. The goal is to learn Earth's history.

4Bare rock in this park makes that job easier. Open layers show the steps that built the land. Each layer needed millions of years to form. At one time, ash settled on the surface. At another time, sand settled. At another time, mud settled. Each layer hardened into rock. Later, water wore the layers down. Wind wore them down as well. The Sharps Formation holds the highest ridges and peaks. Its white base is called Rockyford Ash. Ash from volcanoes made that white base. The layer dates to 25 to 30 million years ago. The Brule Formation marks years when grass and trees covered the land. Those years were 30 to 34 million years ago. The Chadron Formation comes from clay. The clay formed in wet marshes and swamps. Chadron sounds like SHA-druhn. Fossils of alligators have turned up at this level.

5The layer dates to 34 to 37 million years ago. Most of Yellow Mounds is remains that turned into fossils. The remains piled up as a great sea went away. The layer dates to 69 to 75 million years ago. Pierre Shale began as black mud on the floor of that sea. Pierre sounds like PEER. The mud became a rock called shale. Fossils of old sea animals tell what happened in this layer. A scientist who studies geology begins at the bottom. The oldest rocks are usually down there. The story in these layers starts 69 to 75 million years ago. Fossils inside each layer show that the land changed in large ways. A fossil is a plant or an animal remain that turned to rock. Fossils here show a time when the area was like a jungle. Humans were not alive yet.

6At other times, forests stood here. A wide sea covered the land as well. Hunters of fossils have found many wonders in the park. One find is a skull from a saber-toothed cat. The bones include prehistoric mammals. Giant rhinoceroses are among them. Three-toed horses are among them too. In 2010, a Junior Ranger made a find. She was seven years old. Her name was Kylie Ferguson. Her discovery was the fossil of a saber-toothed cat. Rock ridges and valleys are not the whole park. Some ground is mixed-grass prairie. A prairie is wide. A prairie is flat. Grass covers a prairie. Trees are scarce there. Much of the land now called the United States was prairie two hundred years ago. Most of that grass is gone. Farms replaced it. Roads replaced it. Towns replaced it. This park guards the largest mixed-grass prairie inside any national park.

7Plant kinds on that prairie number more than four hundred. By 1890, wild bison were almost all gone. Today, about 1,200 of them live in the park. Many kinds of animals make a home here. The list includes mammals, reptiles, birds, and insects. They have to survive hard weather. Winters are cold. Winter winds are strong. Summers can be dry. Summers can be hot. A storm may hit in any season. In the rocky zones, visitors may see bighorn sheep. Signs beside dusty trails give a warning. The signs say to watch out for rattlesnakes. A rattlesnake has a rattle on its tail. It shakes that rattle to drive off whatever frightens it. A bite from this snake can be dangerous. The snake would rather slip away. It prefers slipping away over biting a person. Most of the wildlife lives on the grasslands.

8Large brown bison graze on the grass. Prairie dogs dig tunnels under the ground. A group of those tunnels is called a town. A town of tunnels can spread over a wide area. At night, a rare black-footed ferret may hunt the prairie dogs. In 1987, scientists began a program to save these ferrets. The program worked in this park. It worked in other places too. Swift fox live on the grasslands. Mule deer live there too. Hawks live in the park. Bluebirds live there. Owls live there. People have recorded more than two hundred kinds of birds. A hawk hunts while it flies overhead. A bluebird darts after insects. Quail run fast through the grass. An owl may nest in a prairie dog tunnel that no one uses. Rangers ask visitors to keep back from the animals.

9The gap should be at least 100 yards. That gap is 91.5 meters. Distance keeps the animals safe. Distance keeps people safe too. Rough, beautiful rock shapes pull visitors in. Each year, more than one million people arrive from around the world. The park is a strong reason for a drive. The drive crosses rolling prairie. Visitors need to get ready first. Places to eat are scarce near the park. Places to shop are scarce. Places to stay are scarce. A wise hiker wears sturdy shoes. A wise hiker carries lots of water. Many people see the park from a car. Others see it from a camper. A twisting road crosses the park. The road runs 31 miles. That distance is 50 kilometers. Many stops along the road are good for photos. Sunlight brings out the colors. Sunlight also brings out the shadows.

10A trip here can feel like a trip into the past. It is easy to picture riders on horseback. They move through grass that stands tall. Ridges of rock tell how this land was made. Valleys tell that story too. Wild animals live here as they have for centuries. The Badlands in South Dakota are beautiful. They are harsh as well. Sunrise washes the park with light. Distance means how much space lies between things or places. A layer is one coat of material above or below another coat. A national park is government land kept safe for its past, its beauty, or its wildlife. Protect means to guard or defend against harm or danger. A ridge is a long, narrow top of a hill. Steep slopes usually run along its sides. Warn means to tell someone that danger is near.

48. Strange Stops Beside the Road

1Billboards for The Thing make some drivers curious. On the drive between Texas and Arizona, the signs are hard to miss. Giant billboards ask what The Thing is. Roadside attractions around the world number in the thousands. The Thing is among them. Roadside attractions are weird. They are wonderful too. They invite people to stop. They invite people to look. The world's largest baseball bat stands in Kentucky. It leans over a building that is five stories tall. The bat is 120 feet tall, which is 37 meters. It weighs more than 68,000 pounds, or 30,844 kilograms.

2The bat is a huge replica of the one Babe Ruth used. A bat that large makes you wonder if giants play baseball. South Dakota is home to the Corn Palace, where corn becomes art. Each year, local grains cover the outside of the building. Local grasses cover the outside too. No bird feeder anywhere is larger than the Corn Palace. When winter starts, birds eat the art. Squirrels eat the art too. Barney Smith stands inside a museum that is also his garage. The museum is one of a kind.

3A spare toilet seat at home can go to him. His Toilet Seat Art Museum is in Texas. The museum's first year was 1992. The seats inside number more than one thousand. He has turned those seats into works of art. A big elephant would need a big drink of water. James Lafferty's elephant, the largest anywhere, went up in 1881. He built it in New Jersey. Lucy is a building six stories tall. Metal covers 12,000 square feet of her, or 3,658 square meters.

4Visitors can still climb inside Lucy today. The year 1915 brought a cotton disaster to Alabama. The cause was a bug known as the boll weevil. Farmers then had to grow other kinds of plants. Peanuts were one of those plants. In the end, the other crops helped the farmers. The farmers honored the weevil by building a monument. The boll weevil is no bigger than a pea. Cotton across the American South was lost to this bug. A homesick soldier started Sign Post Forest. Drivers on the Alaska Highway can find it.

5This forest is not made of trees. Sign Post Forest stands in northwest Canada. More than 100,000 signs make up the forest. People have hung signs there since 1942. People did not make every roadside attraction. The General Sherman Tree stands in Sequoia National Park. By volume, no tree on Earth is larger. People estimate its age at 2,300 to 2,700 years. Stones set in a ring form Stonehenge. The raised stones are 4,000 to 5,000 years old. From London, England, the trip west to it is 90 miles.

6Nebraska has a replica of England's Stonehenge. The replica is called Carhenge. Workers built Carhenge in 1987. They did not use old stones. They used thirty-eight old cars. Some people once wanted that spot to become a junkyard. Yearly visitors now number more than 80,000. An 1890 photo shows London Bridge still crossing the river Thames. London Bridge now stands in Arizona. Many roadside attractions are only replicas. This bridge is the real one. Builders finished the earliest London Bridge in 1831. An American paid $2,460,000 for it in 1968. Taking it apart and moving it to Arizona cost another $7 million.

7The giant handles of this basket weigh almost 150 tons. Heat in the handles keeps ice from forming. A basket company in Ohio has a building shaped like a basket. Workers there do their jobs inside it. The basket is seven stories tall. One of the company's own baskets was the model for the building. When the building was new, a pilot flew a small plane through the handles. Allen Park, Michigan, has the world's largest tire. High Point, North Carolina, has the world's largest chest of drawers.

8Cape Breton Island, Nova Scotia, Canada, has the world's largest violin. Castroville, California, has the world's largest artichoke. Collinsville, Illinois, has the world's largest ketchup bottle. Sugarcreek, Ohio, has the world's largest cuckoo clock. Olivia, Minnesota, has the world's largest ear of corn. Cleveland, Ohio, has the world's largest rubber stamp. A map can lead you to these odd sights. The map shows the United States. The map shows Canada. The map shows AK. The map shows Mexico. The map shows the Atlantic Ocean.

9The map shows the Gulf of Mexico. The Thing is in Arizona. The world's largest baseball bat is in Kentucky. The Corn Palace is in South Dakota. Barney Smith's Toilet Seat Art Museum is in Texas. Lucy the Elephant is in New Jersey. The Boll Weevil Monument is in Alabama. Sign Post Forest is in Canada. Carhenge is in Nebraska. London Bridge is in Arizona. The world's largest basket is in Ohio. Roadside attractions share one trait. They grab people's attention. They make people stop. The Thing sits off the highway in Arizona.

10Learning what it is takes a stop off the highway. Attractions are people, things, or kinds of fun that people enjoy. Billboards are large outdoor signs used for advertising. A monument is a building, statue, or other structure. A monument remembers a person. It can also remember an event. A museum is a building that stores and shows important things. Those things may matter to history, science, or art. A replica is a copy or reproduction of something. A story can mean one level in a building.

49. The Branch That Writes the Laws

1Members of Congress do their work in the Capitol Building. That building stands in Washington, D.C. America's lawmaking branch of government has a special name. People call it Congress. Two groups make up Congress. One group is the Senate. The other group is the House of Representatives. A senator speaks for the American people. A representative speaks for them too. This speaking happens inside the United States government. These members write the nation's laws. They also revise those laws. Other important duties belong to them as well. Leaders adopted the Constitution in 1788. Three parts are how this document splits the national government. Executive duties sit with the president. The Senate belongs to the legislative branch. The House of Representatives belongs to that branch too.

2The judicial branch operates the Supreme Court. It operates the rest of the United States court system too. At times the parts cooperate. At times the parts disagree. A chart can show the split among the three branches. Delaware sends only one person to the House. Lisa Blunt Rochester is that representative. Member counts differ between the Senate and the House. The House of Representatives holds 435 members. Voters choose each House member for a term of two years. The fifty states share the House seats. The share follows the number of people living in the state. No other state has as many people as California. California also sends the most members to the House. A few states send only one.

3Each senator's pay in 2018 was $174,000. The Senate holds one hundred senators. A senator's term lasts six years. Two senators come from each state. Population size does not change that number. Set rules await anyone who hopes for a seat in Congress. Age for the Senate starts at thirty years. Citizenship must go back nine years. The person must live in the state that would be represented. Age for the House starts at twenty-five years. Citizenship for the House must go back seven years. That person must also live in the state to be represented. Many steps stand between an idea and a finished law. Writing a bill is step one. Any person may write a bill. A person who does not work for the government may write one.

4At a town hall, people speak with senators. They speak with representatives too. Some of them even offer ideas for bills. After that, a member of Congress carries the bill in. The bill may enter the Senate. It may enter the House. It may enter both. A small group then meets to discuss it. Senators may form that group. Representatives may form it. The group must decide one thing. Should the full Senate or the full House take up the bill? Research on the bill is part of the group's work. They sit with people who know the subject well. Research leads them to revise the bill. Then the group holds a vote.

5A yes from most of the group lets the bill move ahead. A date is set for the larger talk. The Senate or the House will take that turn. A bill of high importance is discussed at once. A great many bills are never discussed. Representatives may gather to talk about revisions. The health care system of the United States can be the topic. When the date arrives, the full Senate or the full House debates. Members revise the bill again. A vote follows. If most vote yes, the bill travels on. It travels to the other half of Congress. That half debates the bill. That half revises it too. Both halves must approve the very same bill. Passage in both votes sends the bill to the president.

6The president's signature turns the bill into a law. A bill with no signature can still wait ten days. After those ten days, it becomes a law. The president may veto the bill. A veto is a rejection. Talk of the bill usually ends after a veto. Another route remains. A yes from two-thirds of both halves makes the bill a law anyway. In 2018, marchers passed the Capitol Building. They marched in support of laws against guns. Jon Tester is a senator from Montana. During a 2018 parade, he speaks with people from his state. Will Hurd is a representative from Texas. In 2017, he serves a Thanksgiving meal. The meal is at an army base.

7A member of Congress keeps a very full schedule. The job breaks into many pieces. Without a cap, a senator's talk on a bill can run on. A cap on time removes that freedom. Often just a few senators speak on a given bill. The others stay busy. Some of that work happens in small groups. Some of it happens in offices. Lights signal that a vote is near. Bells signal the same moment. The House contains a great many representatives. Rules cap the time a member may speak on a bill. Rules also cap the number of changes. Those caps apply in the House. Nancy Pelosi is a representative from California. She gave the longest House speech on record.

8Her speech ran for more than eight hours. Pramila Jayapal is a representative from Washington. Say the name as pruh-MIH-luh, then JYE-uh-pawl. She explains why she hopes to remain in the House. She wants to keep doing the work. Members of Congress give many hours to money. The money is raised for election campaigns. Some of it pays for ads. Some of it pays for travel. Some of it pays for posters. Some of it pays for stickers. Some of it pays for stamps. The spending helps them get elected. It also helps them keep the job. A representative's term is only two years. For that reason, fund-raising matters a great deal to them. Half of a day may go to seeking people's support.

9Phone calls are part of that effort. Speeches are part of it. Interviews are part of it. They describe the work already done. They describe future plans for the people back home. The Constitution sets up checks and balances. Each branch is able to stop the other two. Stopping another branch is what a check means. This plan sits at the center of the Constitution. Too much power cannot gather in a single branch. Lawmakers can limit the branch that carries out laws. One limit is control of the budget. Another limit is taking a president out of office. The branch that carries out laws can limit the lawmakers. Its tool is a veto on a bill. That veto can keep the bill from becoming law.

10Congress can cancel a veto when the vote count is high enough. Lawmakers can also limit the courts. They may take judges out of office. Courts review the laws that Congress passes. The review asks if those laws match the Constitution. Choosing judges lets the law-carrying branch limit the courts. Courts also review the president.
