52. The Letter That Started a Beard

1In 1860, Grace Bedell sent Abraham Lincoln a letter. The letter said he should grow a beard. People say the reply he wrote is worth more than $1 million today. Email was how people wrote before texting. Telephones came before email. Letters came before telephones. People still write letters. In the 1800s, a letter was the main way to reach a person in another place. Two people might fall in love through letters. Good news might come in a letter. Bad news might come in a letter too. In 1993, a White House volunteer sorted mail for President Bill Clinton. Socks, the First Cat, was getting a lecture nearby. A letter can still reach a person you may never meet. U.S. presidents get thousands of letters each day.

2They get about ten times that many emails. Funny notes show up among the letters. Some of those letters are angry. Some of those letters are sad. The letters help a president know what is happening around the country. Children write to the president at times. A child might share a story. A child might offer an opinion. A child might make a suggestion. This story is about one child. She sent a suggestion to one of the most famous presidents. The last photo of Lincoln without a beard was taken in August 1860. A beard shows in most pictures of Abraham Lincoln. The five-dollar bill shows that beard too. His face did not always have a beard. A clean-shaven face was Abe's look before his run for president.

3Growing a beard was the idea of a girl who was eleven. No one knows if he had thought of a beard before she did. The girl was named Grace Bedell. Her father showed her a picture of Lincoln. The sadness in Lincoln's eyes struck her. Wrinkles along his mouth struck her too. So did his long, thin face. She thought a beard would make him look much better. People often called a beard whiskers in those days. She wrote to him on October 15, 1860. That letter told him her idea. She asked Lincoln whether a daughter of his was her own age. Her letter also described her family. She had four brothers. She wrote that some brothers would vote for him anyway.

4She would try to get the other brothers to vote for him if he grew whiskers. She said that ladies like whiskers. Those ladies, she said, would tease husbands into voting for Lincoln. Then, she wrote, he would be president. Her father was going to vote for him. She said she would vote for him if she were a man. Women struggled for many years before they could vote. The right to vote was not theirs until 1920. The Nineteenth Amendment was ratified that year. By then, Grace's age was 71. The stamp on her letter cost one penny. That stamp would have shown a Native American. The penny did not show Lincoln until 1909. The man who would soon be president wrote back quickly.

5Grace had his letter within days. He greeted her as his dear little miss. He was sorry to say he had no daughters. He wrote that he had three sons. One son was seventeen years of age. One son was nine years of age. One son was seven years of age. A photo from 1861 shows Mary. It also shows Willie. It also shows Robert. It also shows Tad. It also shows Abraham Lincoln. Lincoln asked if people would think whiskers looked silly. He had never worn whiskers. He was fifty-one years old. He wondered if that age was too old for a first beard. Grace's letter made him think about her suggestion. Not long after, he started to grow whiskers. Voters gave the United States its sixteenth president when they picked him.

6He got on a train in Springfield, Illinois. The train was going to Washington, D.C. His beard was full by that time. A picture from February 1861 shows the beard. 7They were eager to see their newly elected president. A large crowd gathered to see him. The crowd also wanted to hear him. Grace Bedell stood in that crowd. Her father stood with her. Lincoln knew he was in Grace's hometown. He decided to talk about his beard. When the talk ended, he made a request. If the girl who wrote the letter was there, he wanted to meet her. Mr. Bedell held Grace by the hand. They worked their way through the tight crowd to Lincoln. Lincoln climbed down. He spoke with Grace. She remembered what he said. He told Gracie to look at his whiskers. He said he had been growing the whiskers for her. He kissed her cheek.

8Cheers rose around them. Then he got back on the train. Lincoln was the first president with a full beard. The fifteen presidents before him did not even have a mustache. During the fifty years that followed, only two presidents had bare faces. William Howard Taft served from 1909 to 1913. Chester A. Arthur served from 1881 to 1885. John Quincy Adams served from 1825 to 1829. Lincoln was first to have a beard. He was not the first president with hair on his face. Several presidents before him had sideburns. Several presidents after him had sideburns. Two presidents had muttonchops. Harry Truman was the most recent president with any facial hair. He served from 1945 to 1953. He grew a goatee only while he was on vacation.

9Fashions come and then they go. No president since William Howard Taft has worn facial hair. Taft served from 1909 to 1913. He wore a mustache. Grace never saw the president again. Clean-shaven means a beard has been shaved off.

10A mustache or other hair may be shaved off too. The word can also mean a person shaved not long ago. Eager means excited and interested. Elected means chosen for government by a vote.
