53. The Making of a Blizzard

1After the Blizzard of 1888, horse carts carried snow out of New York City. Rain came down on a Sunday in March of that year. Rain reached the coast that runs along the northeast of the United States. Night arrived, and the temperature fell fast. Rain changed into ice. Ice then changed into snow. Howling winds were blowing by Monday morning. The snow that was falling got stacked by those winds into huge drifts. People gave the storm the name Great White Hurricane. Three days was how long the storm lasted. Boston ended up buried. New York ended up buried too. New York City had huge snow piles along roads beneath the high train tracks. More than four hundred deaths came with the end of the storm.

2Damage from the Blizzard of 1888 ran into millions of dollars. Ships out on the sea sank. Trains froze where they stood on the rails. Snow as tall as several stories blocked the roads. Summer was well along before the final snow melted. Elevated trains could not run once the 1888 blizzard had passed. New York City officials made a decision because of the storm. The El trains would go below the ground. A subway would be built instead. Workers finished New York's first subway section in 1904. Fierce wind is one mark of a blizzard. Blowing snow is another mark. The United States has had many large snowstorms since 1888.

3Blizzards were only some of those storms. A blizzard needs three things at once. The winds have to be strong. The snow has to blind anyone looking into it. The cold has to be freezing. Steady blizzard winds pass 35 miles each hour. That rate is about 56 kilometers each hour. A gust may reach the force of a hurricane. Such a gust is 75 miles each hour or higher. That rate is about 119 kilometers each hour. Driving grows hard when a whiteout arrives. Strong gales haul falling snow with them. They also lift snow already lying on the ground. A spinning white cloud fills the air. Seeing through the cloud is difficult. Whiteout is the name for this.

4During a whiteout, sight stops at about a quarter of a mile. That distance is about 402 meters. Travel that stays truly safe is almost impossible. The early meaning of blizzard was a violent strike. It could also mean a blow. Cannon shots were described with that word. A small-town paper in Iowa was first to use the word for snow. The 1870s were when that happened. The storm in the paper was a powerful one. One wind-chill chart begins at an air temperature of 0 degrees Fahrenheit. A wind of 5 miles each hour makes it feel like minus 11 degrees Fahrenheit. A wind of 10 miles each hour makes it feel like minus 16 degrees Fahrenheit.

5A wind of 15 miles each hour makes it feel like minus 19 degrees Fahrenheit. A wind of 20 miles each hour makes it feel like minus 22 degrees Fahrenheit. Biting wind makes cold air feel colder than it truly is. Wind chill is created by blowing gales. The body loses heat faster when wind chill is at work. The air seems much colder than it is. Bare skin can freeze from wind chill. Frostbite is the painful condition that follows. Bundling up guards a person from wind chill. Warm clothes keep skin from freezing. Snowstorm clouds can build and then roll over a lake. A snowstorm is how every blizzard begins.

6Warm moist air runs into cold air. Clouds grow from that meeting. Moisture inside the clouds freezes and becomes flakes. Snow is what falls when the air down low is cold enough. Clear sky around a storm has higher air pressure than the storm itself. Air starts moving because of the pressure gap. Wind is that moving air. A wider pressure gap brings a stronger wind. A blizzard tends to grow on one side of a snowstorm. That side is where the pressure gap is the largest. Several feet of snow fell from a massive blizzard in 2016. Virginia was covered by the snow. Washington, D.C., was covered as well. A skier on a long cross-country trip passes the Capitol during that record storm.

7Snow on the ground is enough for a blizzard to be possible anywhere. Some regions get blizzards more often than other regions do. America's eastern shore is one region like that. Cold air from Canada sometimes sweeps south in winter. That cold air from the northeast meets air that is warm and moist. The warm air is moving up out of the south. A storm called a nor'easter forms from the meeting. Several days is a possible length for a nor'easter. A great deal of snow can fall from it. These storms head north beside the East Coast. A fast snowstorm sometimes starts in the western mountains of Canada. It then crosses the plains of America toward the east.

8The coast is its goal. Alberta Clipper is the name of this storm. Deep snow is not what clippers are known for. Strong winds are their mark. Bitter cold is their mark too. Lots of North American blizzards begin as a nor'easter or an Alberta Clipper. A Russian city has a statue buried in snow. A woman walks past it. North America is not the only place with blizzards. Parts of Asia can face powerful blizzards lasting for days. Winter in northeast Russia brings bitter cold. Strong winds come with that cold. The blizzard in December 2014 was the worst the area had faced in years. Winds full of ice laid snow more than a foot deep across the land.

9Before a blizzard, people may line up to buy groceries. Danger rises when wind, snow, and freezing air arrive together. Flesh can freeze from wind chill and a low temperature. Swirling snow hides what is ahead. Accidents can follow from that poor sight. Road crews have trouble clearing highways while snow keeps blowing. A gust of wind may knock a tree down. A gust may also knock a power line down. People may be left in the dark. Staying warm may turn into a struggle. Predicting the time of a blizzard is easier today. Predicting the place is easier today too. Years ago, both tasks were harder.

10People now get time to prepare. Batteries can be set aside. Flashlights can be set aside. Those lights help if the power fails. Extra food can be stored by families. Extra water can be stored too. Other ways to stay warm can be planned. One way is a fireplace. Another way is a woodstove. After the supplies are ready, people should settle indoors. They should wait until the storm passes. Anyone who must step out should wear warm clothes. Even then, the outdoor time should stay short. Inside is the best place while a blizzard is going on. People may go out once the storm is finished. They can then enjoy the leftover snow in safety.

54. The Reasons Our Bodies Sleep

1This book opens with an introduction. Then it asks what sleep is. Then it explains the need for sleep. Then it explains sleep patterns. Then it explains how to get a good night of rest. A word list comes at the end. Many people like a quiet start to the night. They curl up between soft sheets. They slowly drift off into a sound sleep. A good night of sleep is a kind of rest. It comes after a busy day at school, at work, or at play. People need sleep to function well. Most other animals need sleep too. Sleep belongs with food and water. It is necessary to keep minds sharp. It is necessary to keep bodies ready to go. Here is a surprising fact about time. About one third of a life is spent asleep. Think of a person who is seventy-five years old. That person has spent twenty-five years snoozing. Falling asleep happens naturally. It also happens at regular times. Stopping sleep is easy. Often, nothing more than a loud noise is required. The noise wakes a sleeping person. So what is sleep? What makes us fall asleep? For centuries, these questions have been difficult for people. An ancient Greek thinker had one belief about sleep. He believed sleep was a quiet time of rest.

2He believed eating brought that rest on. It sounds odder than the idea really is. A big meal leaves most people ready to nap. Aristotle had his own belief about food and sleep. Warm vapors, in his belief, rose to the head while food was digested. He believed the vapors then cooled. He believed the cooled vapors went to the heart. He believed that path made people feel sleepy. This idea is not true. Even so, the kind of food you eat does matter. It changes how sleepy you feel. Sleepiness can be caused by certain foods. Other foods, like chocolate, have caffeine. A perk-up can follow those other foods. They do not make you wind down. Brain waves are recorded with a special machine. Later years brought a clearer picture of sleep. It came in the early twentieth century. Studies showed a surprise about the brain. Snoozing does not truly turn the brain off. Instead, brain activity changes. The change comes as wakefulness shifts to sleep. A new idea followed. Some activity in the brain continues in sleep. Further study of sleep came after this discovery. Doctors wanted to know the cause of a change. The change was in brain waves. They learned about a substance called adenosine.

3Adenosine affects the brain. It triggers sleep. The body produces adenosine naturally. It makes adenosine as the body uses energy. The amount of this substance grows higher all through the day. It travels to the brain. There, it acts on many body processes. Those processes are active when we are awake. Adenosine slows them down. A chart lines up six states of the brain. State 1 is awake. State 2 is awake with eyes closed. State 3 is falling asleep. State 4 is asleep. State 5 is deep sleep. State 6 is asleep and dreaming. Machines can record brain activity. They show that a sleeping brain can be very busy. During dreaming, it can be just as active as a waking brain. More brain activity shows up as tighter waves. Looser waves occur during relaxation. The chart comes from VEM and Science Source. If the brain does not shut down while we snooze, it still has a job. The job is learning. The brain helps us learn by shuffling memories. At first, the brain stores much of what we learn in one place. That place is called short-term memory. A short time is all that short-term memory holds well. It is not big enough to hold everything we learn forever.

4It is not strong enough either. The brain must move information out of short-term memory. The new place is long-term memory. We are asleep during the time of this memory shuffle. The move makes memory easier. It is easier to remember what we have learned. It is easier to use what we have learned. Scientists can examine a man during a sleep study. They look at his brain and his body. Professional athletes need sleep for two body jobs. Sleep strengthens body tissues. Sleep restores energy levels. Falling asleep is not only a change in brain activity. Body processes change too. Waking hours are when energy is used. Body cells are broken down. The shift in the body comes with sleep. It shifts to a different kind of activity. New cells are built during sleep. This process restores body tissues. It helps those tissues grow. Athletes need sleep for this reason. During training, athletes constantly break down cells. They break down tissues too. Restoring those tissues takes enough rest. Rest also makes the tissues stronger. This is also why new babies sleep so much. Within five months, most infants become twice their birth weight. That rate of growth needs a balance. Building up must be greater than breaking down for that growth.

5We also need sleep for illness. Sleep helps our bodies fight illness. Sleep helps our bodies heal wounds. Sleep strengthens the immune system. The immune system is the cells and tissues that fight infection. Healing is what the body gives its attention to during sleep. It does not spend that time using energy on other activities. Studies show a change in the blood during sleep. During sleep the number of those illness-fighting blood cells goes up. Sick patients are told by doctors to rest in bed for this reason. Sleep problems come in several different kinds. Three of the best-known problems are listed here. Insomnia is difficulty falling asleep. Insomnia can have many different causes. Stress may cause insomnia. Worry may cause insomnia. Bad pain may make it impossible to sleep. Illness can block sleep completely. Narcolepsy sits nearly opposite insomnia. Narcolepsy can bring sleep on with no warning. The sleep can come at any time. Others can feel startled by these attacks of sleep. Weak muscles can strike these patients all at once. The weakness makes them fall to the ground. On the ground, they are unable to move. Among teens, sleepwalking happens fairly often. It is rare in adults. Open eyes are part of how sleepwalkers move around.

6They may be walking. They may be talking. They may even be doing chores. Usually a sleepwalking episode is shorter than ten minutes. Waking a sleepwalker is not dangerous. It is not harmful. Even so, it is best to simply lead the person back to bed. Sleep is not all one kind. Sleep goes through cycles. Researchers want to understand how sleep works. They monitor brain activity in sleeping people. They monitor eye movement. They monitor breathing rates. They monitor heart rates. All of the measurements go into one graph. The graph shows a pattern. The pattern is made of different stages of sleep. Two kinds of sleep exist. One type is rapid eye movement sleep, called REM. The other type is non-rapid eye movement sleep, called NREM. Three stages belong to NREM sleep. Drowsy feelings mark the first stage. They start to doze. They may twitch. They may suddenly jerk awake. They imagine they are falling. This stage does not last more than ten minutes. A sample graph shows five cycles in one night. The cycles are numbered 1, 2, 3, 4, and 5. The hours run from 11 pm through the night. Next the clock shows 12 in the morning, then 1, 2, 3, and 4.

7They continue through 5 am, 6 am, and 7 am. The graph marks awake time. It marks REM sleep. It marks NREM stage 1, NREM stage 2, and NREM stage 3. A sleeping person goes through these stages in multiple cycles each night. Another chart shows time spent in each stage. It shows about how much time an average adult spends each night. One person may spend a different time in each stage than another person does. Age is one of the many things that change the length. The chart comes from The Brain from Top to Bottom. During stage 2 of NREM, muscles do not move much. Awareness of the world outside drops for the sleeper. Shifting of memories begins at this time. Stage 2 of NREM sleep takes half of the night's sleep, or even more. Sometimes more than half of sleep time is spent there. During stage 3 of NREM, sleepers begin to dream. The brain puts newly learned information into a new order. Lasting memories are formed. Breathing rate is lowest during this stage. Heart rate is lowest. Blood pressure is lowest. Brain temperature is lowest. Waking a person is usually very hard in this deep sleep. This stage uses nearly one quarter of the time asleep.

8REM sleep does not match the other sleep stages. During REM sleep, the eyes stay closed. Even so, the eyes move rapidly from side to side. It is as if the sleeper is watching a ball fly back and forth. Brain activity is almost at waking levels. Breathing is almost at waking levels. Heart rate is almost at waking levels. The brain is not resting. The brain is speeding along. The body of the sleeper stays fully still. In this stage the muscles cannot move. Perhaps the reason is the dreams. Wild dreams take place during REM sleep. We dream in REM sleep. We also dream in deep sleep. We dream every night. We do not always remember our dreams. All dreams are like stories. The stories play out in our mind. Some of the dream stories feel unpleasant. Nightmares are scary dreams. They can make sleepers wake up upset. Serious bad dreams sometimes come to young children. Those nightmares are called night terrors. Night terrors can cause children to scream. Children may move about without being fully awake. For centuries, people have wondered about dreams. They have asked whether dreams had secret meanings. Ancient people believed dreams were messages from the gods. Today, some people think dreams come from our imagination.

9Other people think dreams have hidden messages. Many things should happen at bedtime. They should happen when a tired head hits the pillow. When they do not happen, the next day can go wrong. Cranky feelings can come from too little sleep. Work mistakes grow when sleep is missing. Problems become harder to solve. The brain does not struggle alone. Studies show a problem in the body too. Without enough sleep, the body has a harder job. A steady body temperature is harder to hold. Wounds take longer to heal. Naps do not always make up for lost night sleep. Good sleep at night is not easy to replace when it is missing. Even so, a quick nap can help. It can improve mood. It can improve alertness. It can improve performance. A sleep schedule helps even on weekends. Sticking to it can help you fall asleep. It can help you stay asleep all night. Habits that support good sleep matter a great deal. Try to go to bed at the same time each day. Wake at the same hour every day. A body stays on its schedule because of this. Cutting down screen time also helps. Try not to watch television just before bedtime. Try not to use a computer just before bedtime.

10Try not to use a tablet just before bedtime. Try not to use a smartphone just before bedtime. Do not exercise right before going to bed. A simple walk is acceptable before bed. Falling asleep can get harder if you do more than a walk. Heat and water can help if sleep will not start. Take a warm bath. Or take a warm shower. Muscles relax from either choice. Healthy bodies follow from strong sleep habits. They keep the mind sharp. Remember a simple warning. Miss your sleep, and the next day may go badly. Brain waves are patterns of brain activity. They can be measured in electric currents. Function means to act or work as expected. The immune system moves antibodies through the body. It does this to fight infection. Monitor means to observe something over time. It can also mean to check the progress of something. Reorganized means the order of something was changed. It can mean the arrangement was changed. Restores means returns something to its original condition. A schedule is a plan. The plan names the time and the place for each event. Shuffling means moving around. It can also mean rearranging. Triggers means causes something to take place.

55. Tremors, Fire Mountains, and Giant Sea Waves

1Contents. A Start. Far Under the Ground. Earthquakes, a Hard Shake. The Richter Scale. Volcanoes, Huge Blasts. Tsunamis, Waves Gone Wild. A Closing. Word Meanings. Smoke rises from a volcano. The volcano looks peaceful. A Start. The ground is usually peaceful. The ocean is usually peaceful too. Strong forces sit deep inside Earth. Those forces can cause an earthquake without warning. They can cause a volcano. They can cause a tsunami. Tsunami is said tsoo-NOM-ees. This book tells about these events. It also tells about the forces that cause them. Far Under the Ground. Giant pieces of rock form Earth's outer layer. The pieces fit together like a puzzle. These pieces are called plates. Plates form the continents. Plates form the ocean floors. Where plates meet, cracks are common. Gaps are common there too. Those cracks and gaps are called faults. These plates are tectonic plates.

2Dotted lines show where each plate ends. Under the plates, Earth is very hot. The heat is strong enough to melt rock. Melted rock becomes a liquid called magma. Plates rest on top of that liquid magma. Magma keeps moving all the time. As it moves, it pulls the plates with it. Three different things can happen where moving plates meet. Sometimes plates push against each other. Then one plate slides over the other. Or one plate slides under the other. Sometimes plates slide past each other. Then their edges grind together. Sometimes plates move apart. A gap opens between them. Magma comes up through that gap. An earthquake can damage a building. It can even destroy a building. Earthquakes, a Hard Shake. Sometimes plates catch on each other. Then the plates cannot move. Pressure builds up. Energy builds up too.

3At last the rocks give way. The ground shakes. People above the plates feel an earthquake. Some earthquakes are small. A small one does no harm. Sometimes the shaking is very strong. Buildings crumble. Bridges fall. The ground splits into large cracks. This ground was torn open by an earthquake. The picture shows a seismograph. A seismograph measures how strong an earthquake is. Seismograph is said SIZEmoh-graf. A number is assigned to every earthquake. The number comes from the Richter scale. Richter is said RICK-ter. The number matches the strength of the quake. 1. People cannot feel this earthquake. Only a sensitive instrument can detect it. 2. Most people do not feel this quake. Someone in a tall building may notice a slight sway. 3. Many people near the quake's starting place notice the shaking. No damage is done. 4. People at the starting place surely feel it.

4Objects that hang will sway. Water moves in swimming pools. A weak building may be damaged. 5. This quake is felt across a wider area. Weak buildings at the center usually take lots of damage. Weak buildings around the center do too. Strong buildings take some damage. 6. Weak buildings take lots of damage. Strong buildings take some damage. The harm can reach 160 kilometers. That distance is 100 mi. 7. This is a very major earthquake. At the center, most buildings are destroyed. Cracks form in the Earth. Pipes under the ground break. Large landslides can happen. 8. Buildings are destroyed. Bridges are destroyed. Large cracks show in the ground. Large landslides happen. 9. The ground seems to move in waves. A whole river may move. Objects may be flung into the air. Buildings face total destruction.

5Other structures face total destruction too. The Richter scale has no top number. The strongest earthquake ever recorded measured 9.5. Volcanoes, Huge Blasts. A volcano starts deep inside Earth. Magma rises from there. A crack in the surface is the way out. At the surface, the magma cools. It becomes solid rock. The rock often forms a mountain. It can also form an island. On this Hawaiian volcano, liquid rock is runny. It flows in a smooth path. The flow looks like a quiet river. Some volcanoes become plugged. After that, they explode with great violence. Sometimes hot magma cools too early. It cools before it reaches the surface. Hardening turns it into rock that is solid. The rock plugs the crack. It can plug the hole where magma had been seeping out. Hot gases remain in the magma. The gases keep pressing on the plug.

6The pressure grows. Then the volcano explodes. When a mountain explodes, tons of rock roll down its sides. Those falling rocks are called landslides. During a volcanic eruption, clouds of ash rise. Clouds of dust rise too. Ash then falls back to the ground. The fall looks like falling snow. A large cloud of ash can block the sunlight. This picture shows Mount St. Helens, which is in Washington State. The picture shows it before the eruption. The eruption happened in 1980. Another picture shows Mount St. Helens after the eruption. The blast blew these trees over. Some large volcanoes have snow on top. They have ice on top too. All the snow melts at one time from the hot gases. They melt all the ice at one time. Water then rushes down the mountain. The flood is large.

7It is filled with dirt. This kind of flood is called a mudslide. Scientists cannot yet predict an earthquake. Predicting a volcanic eruption is now something they do very well. People in the Philippines received a warning from scientists in 1991. A volcano there was about to erupt. Many people left the area. Safety was reached before that huge volcano erupted. Scientists gather information at a volcanic fissure. Tsunamis, Waves Gone Wild. A tsunami is a huge wave. An earthquake can cause one. A volcano can cause one too. Its height can equal the length of a football field. No other waves on Earth are as large as tsunamis. A large landslide sometimes falls into the sea. An earthquake may send that rock down. A volcano may send it down too. The rock makes huge waves. The ocean floor can also be where an earthquake starts.

8A volcano can happen there too. Any of these events can create a tsunami. Near the sea, steam lifts off a volcano. A tsunami speeds away from its start. It moves out in all directions. An entire ocean can lie in the path of one tsunami. Tsunamis travel very fast. In the open ocean, a tsunami may not stand very high. It stays low above the water's surface. Even so, the wave is very deep. Near land, the ocean floor rises. That rise pushes the wave up. At the shore, the wave is tall. It can destroy almost anything that stands in its path. A tsunami wave changes when it reaches shore. It breaks as a giant wave. The picture shows damage from a tsunami. Most tsunamis stand between one story and ten stories high. That height may not sound very great.

9The power of so much moving water is hard to imagine. Houses can be destroyed even by a small tsunami. It can destroy streets. It can destroy a whole town. Tsunamis are getting easier to predict. Volcanoes have also gotten easier to predict. Scientists have instruments that find volcanoes. Those instruments find earthquakes too. When a large one happens, time often remains. Scientists can warn people. They say a tsunami may be coming. Many people call a tsunami a wall of water. An earthquake destroyed this building. A Closing. The ground shakes when an earthquake is large. Homes are destroyed. Property is destroyed too. In an explosive eruption, liquid rock can cover a whole city. Ash can cover a city. Landslides can cover a city too. Harm from a tsunami can cost billions of dollars. Scientists keep working on prediction.

10They want to predict volcanoes. They want to predict earthquakes. They want to predict tsunamis. Their work could save thousands of lives. Word Meanings. earthquakes: a shaking of Earth's crust. Vibrations under the ground cause it. faults: cracks in Earth's crust. Movement takes place along the cracks. landslides: soil and rock on the move in heavy masses. magma: rock that melted into liquid. It lies beneath Earth's surface. mudslide: a flood of water. Dirt and debris fill the water. plates: large sheets of rock. They form Earth's crust. Richter scale: the scale used to measure earthquake strength. tsunamis: enormous waves in the ocean. An underwater earthquake can cause them. A landslide can cause them. A volcano can cause them too. volcanoes: places on Earth's surface where material spews out. The material can be gas. It can be ash. It can be lava.

56. Two Continents, One Great Port

1This book presents one city that belongs to two worlds. It shows where that city sits. It tells the city's history. It visits a cistern under the ground. It visits a huge covered market. It visits the Maiden's Tower. It visits Galata Tower. It visits Topkapi Palace. It visits the Hagia Sophia. It visits Taksim Square. It closes with a walk through time. A list of hard words comes last. Istanbul is a busy city. Many bridges cross its water. A ship comes into the port of a grand city. Goods on that ship travel from Europe toward Asia. Pieces of this amazing city stand on each continent. The city is Istanbul, in the country of Turkey. More than fifteen million people live in Istanbul. Across the years, other rulers have held the city. The city has also carried other names.

2Beautiful landmarks tell the city's story. Only a few cities in the world stand on two continents. Istanbul is one of them. Turkey's northwest corner is where Istanbul sits. Both sides of a natural waterway hold parts of the city. The waterway is the Strait of Bosphorus. The name sounds like BAHS-fuh-ruhs. The waterway joins two seas. More people live in Istanbul than in any other city in Europe. A map can show this bridge between continents. The map marks Europe, Asia, and Africa. It marks the Black Sea. It marks the Sea of Marmara. It marks Italy and the Aegean Sea. It marks Turkey and the Mediterranean Sea. Istanbul has served as an important port since long ago. It lies on the only trade route that links the Black Sea with the Mediterranean Sea.

3That spot has also helped ideas travel between East and West. Human life in this part of the world dates to 7500 BCE. Around 660 BCE, Byzas brought settlers to the spot. His name sounds like BY-zuhss. Byzas led a group that came from Megara, a city in Greece. Megara sounds like MEH-guh-ruh. The settlers gave their new city the name Byzantion. That name sounds like bih-ZAN-tee-uhn. Later the name became Byzantium. That later name sounds like bih-ZAN-tee-uhm. Constantine the Great ruled the Roman Empire from the city of Constantinople. In 1453, Mehmed the Second took control of Constantinople. Istanbul has served as the capital of four empires. In the early 400s CE, the name became Constantinople. The new name honored Constantine the Great, a ruler of Rome. In 1453, the city became an Ottoman city.

4People then called it Konstantiniyye. Ottoman sounds like AH-tuh-muhn. Konstantiniyye sounds like KOHN-stahn-tin-yeh. From 1930 on, Istanbul has been the name of the city. The city was capital of the Roman Empire from 330 to 395. It was capital of the Eastern Roman Empire from 395 to 1204. People also know that empire as the Byzantine Empire. It was capital of that empire again from 1261 to 1453. It was capital of the Latin Empire from 1204 to 1261. It was capital of the Ottoman Empire from 1453 to 1922. Over the years, many landmarks and city parts were destroyed. People built them again. A visit can take in some of the most interesting landmarks. This huge water tank is known as the Underground Cistern. No one uses the tank now.

5Long ago, it was the city's most important supply of water. The cistern holds 336 posts made of stone. Arches rise between those posts. A lake near the Black Sea supplied the tank with water. Istanbul has several hundred cisterns of great age. Of those old tanks, the Underground Cistern is the largest. The Grand Bazaar ranks among the oldest covered markets in the world. It is a huge market of stone, with a roof over it. The earliest parts went up in 1461. More parts were added as time passed. Today the market has sixty-one streets under cover. It holds more than four thousand shops. Some shops sell clothing. Some shops sell jewelry. Some shops sell rugs. Some shops sell goods made of leather. Daily shoppers at the Grand Bazaar number more than 250,000. The number of workers there each day is more than 20,000.

6In 2014, the Grand Bazaar drew more travelers than any other place in the world. Visitors in that year numbered more than 91 million. The Maiden's Tower has been torn down many times. People have put it back up many times. The tower stands on a small island. The island sits at the south end of the Strait of Bosphorus. About 2,400 years have passed since people built the tower. Long ago, people in the tower watched goods on ships that passed. At a later time, the tower served as a lighthouse. Today the tower is a restaurant. At night, diners see wonderful views of Istanbul and the water. A story gave the Maiden's Tower its name. In the story, a ruler tries to keep his daughter safe.

7He places her in a tower that has water all around it. Galata Tower rises near the middle of the view. The tower first opened for visitors in the 1960s. Builders finished Galata Tower in 1348. Galata sounds like GAH-lah-tah. When it was new, no building in Istanbul stood taller. People first used the tower as a lighthouse. Later the tower held prisoners of war. A later job for the tower was watching for fires. From the tower, visitors look out on beautiful views of Istanbul. Hezarfen Ahmed Celebi lived from 1609 to 1640. His name sounds like heh-ZAR-fen AH-med CHEH-leh-bee. He was a scientist. He was also an inventor. He lived in Istanbul. People believe he was the first to fly with no powered machine. One day in the 1630s, he left from Galata Tower.

8He came down on the far side of the Strait of Bosphorus. Scientists today think he likely built a kind of glider. Topkapi Palace went up in the late 1400s. Topkapi sounds like TOHP-kah-pee. Rulers lived there for almost four hundred years. Later rulers added new parts as the years went on. Those additions left the palace with a mix of styles. In 1924, the palace became a museum. Three main gates lead into Topkapi Palace. Inside are four open areas. Buildings and gardens surround those open areas. High walls ring the outside. For a long time, the palace was home to many rulers. A large dome sits in the middle of the Hagia Sophia. Smaller domes sit around that large one. Hagia Sophia sounds like HAH-gee-uh soh-FEE-uh. Its first form, in the year 360, was a church.

9Fires later forced two rebuilds. The building that stands today went up between 532 and 537. In 1453, a ruler turned the church into a mosque. At a later time, it became a museum. In the museum, people learned the story of the building. Today the building is a mosque once more. Taksim Square was laid out in the 1700s. Taksim sounds like TAHK-sim. All of the city's water lines once met at that square. Shops stand there today. Hotels stand there today. Restaurants stand there today. Parades use the square and the street. New Year celebrations use them too. One end of Taksim Square is the site of Republic Monument. The monument honors the start of the Turkish Republic. Stone forms a high wall around the whole of the oldest city part.

10Istanbul is full of color. Great numbers of people live there. The city carries a rich history. The city joins two continents. Wonderful landmarks fill the city. Every corner of Istanbul holds a story of its own. A visit can feel like a trip back through time. A bazaar is a market made of rows of small shops. A church is a building for the practice of the Christian religion. A cistern is a tank or other container that stores liquid. The liquid is usually water. A landmark may be an important old building or another important place. A landmark may also be an object on land that marks a spot. A mosque is a building for the practice of the Islamic religion. A tower is a building that is tall and narrow.

57. Two Spacecraft on a Long Flight

1A Voyager 1 picture shows Saturn. One moon of Saturn is in that picture too. The 1977 launch of Voyager 2 was in Florida, at Kennedy Space Center. A Titan/Centaur rocket carried that spacecraft up. Two Voyager spacecraft left Earth in 1977. The United States was the country that sent them. Their job was a flyby of Jupiter. Their job was also a flyby of Saturn. Jupiter is a giant planet in our solar system. Saturn is a giant planet in our solar system too. Scientists made the Voyagers tough. Even so, they expected the craft to last only about five years. Both spacecraft are still working. Giant satellite dishes in Australia belong to NASA's Deep Space Network.

2By 2025, the gap from Earth to Voyager 1 was about sixteen billion miles. A radio sends its messages home. That radio has about the power of a small lightbulb. A message needs about seventeen hours to reach Earth. Around the world, giant satellite dishes are used by NASA. Those dishes catch the weak signal. Voyager 2 launched on August 20, 1977. Voyager 1 launched on September 5, 1977. Voyager 2 passed Jupiter on March 5, 1979. Voyager 1 passed Jupiter on July 9, 1979. Voyager 1 passed Saturn on November 12, 1980. Voyager 2 passed Saturn on August 25, 1981.

3Voyager 2 passed Uranus on January 24, 1986. Voyager 2 passed Neptune on August 25, 1989. Planners laid out the trip with great care. Each planet moves around the Sun at its own speed. At times the planets share one side of the Sun. At other times they sit on opposite sides. The late 1970s put the four biggest planets near one another in a line. They sat close enough for one trip to visit them all. Jupiter was one of those four planets. Saturn was one of them. Uranus was one of them. Neptune was one of them. Counting outward from the Sun, Jupiter comes fifth. Among the outer planets, it comes first.

4The first job was to study Jupiter. The first job was also to study Saturn. The craft were to study the larger moons of those planets. They were to study Saturn's rings too. During the flight, the two spacecraft made discoveries about our solar system. Jupiter's Great Red Spot is as big as two or three Earths. Jupiter was the first target. It is the largest planet in the solar system. People had already viewed Jupiter through telescopes. The Voyagers showed it much more clearly. The Great Red Spot, the pictures showed, was a storm of huge size. That storm was spinning. They found a ring around Jupiter.

5Saturn has rings of that kind too. Jupiter's moons became better known with the help these craft gave scientists. Saturn has more rings than any other planet in our solar system. The Voyagers reached Saturn nine months apart. One visit was in 1980. The other visit was in 1981. Their pictures showed new rings. Saturn is the second-largest planet in the solar system. Uranus sits tipped onto its side. Its rings make that tilt easy to see. After Saturn, Voyager 1 turned toward deep space. Voyager 2 kept on toward Uranus. Uranus is a strange planet. Its color is light blue. The planet is tipped onto its side.

6Voyager 2 went past Uranus in early 1986. A gas in Neptune's atmosphere makes the planet a deep blue. Voyager 2 flew on toward Neptune next. It passed the planet. It found five new moons there. Then Voyager 2 started toward deep space, as Voyager 1 had done. Scientists picked what to put on the Golden Record. The Voyagers do more than collect information. They also carry a message. If other living things exist out in space, this message says hello to them. Each spacecraft has a Golden Record on its side. Music is on the record. Art is on the record. People from around the world say hello on it.

7In 1977, Voyager 1 and Voyager 2 launched sixteen days apart. In 1979, the Jupiter flybys found volcanoes on Jupiter's moons. Those flybys also found ice on Jupiter's moons. In 1980, the Saturn flybys found an atmosphere on Titan. Voyager 1 then began its trip out of the solar system. In 1986, Voyager 2 reached Uranus. In 1989, Voyager 2 reached Neptune. Voyager 2 then began its trip out of the solar system. In 1990, the Voyagers got the task of exploring deep space. In 1998, Voyager 1 passed Pioneer 10. No object made by humans sat farther out in space. In 2012, Voyager 1 entered deep space.

8The outside of the record carries a map. That map shows where Earth is. Other living things probably will not find us anytime soon. At least forty thousand years will pass before Voyager 1 draws near another star. The planet visits are finished. The spacecraft now have a new task. They are to explore space past our solar system. The year 2012 was when Voyager 1 left our solar system. That leaving started the new journey. No object made by people had left our solar system before it. A drawing of this region shows deep space. The drawing shows one bubble of strong solar wind from our sun.

9It shows our sun. It shows Voyager 1. It shows a larger bubble of weak solar wind. That weak wind trails behind our sun. The drawing shows Voyager 2. It shows Neptune's orbit. Neptune is the farthest planet from our sun. The Voyager spacecraft took many photographs on the trip. Some of those photographs show moons they passed. Project scientist Dr. Edward Stone says Voyager opened up our solar system. The two spacecraft flew past every outer planet. They flew past many moons as well. Deep space is now in view for the first time, thanks to the Voyagers. A discovery is the act of finding something for the first time.

10It can also mean uncovering something for the first time. Exploring means learning about an area by traveling through it. A person can also explore by traveling over an area. A journey is a long trip. Moons are huge balls of rock. A moon travels around a planet. Planets are large, round objects. A planet travels around a star. Rings are bands of dust and ice. Those bands circle a planet. A solar system is a group of objects in space. Those objects orbit a star. A spacecraft is a vehicle for travel in space. Telescopes are tools that make far-off objects look closer.

58. When Locusts Darken the Sky

1Locusts can spread across a huge stretch of land. In 2004, one swarm moved through the Middle East. The insects ate lawns. They ate palm trees. Dry weather has lasted for weeks on the prairie. Brown color is spreading through the corn. Rain is what the corn needs. A cloud appears far off. Farmers feel hope at the first sight. Soon they understand that something is badly wrong. The sky turns brown. A new sound starts. It matches hail hitting a roof. The cloud is made of insects. Millions land on every surface they can find. Jaws chew through the corn. Jaws chew through the grass. Jaws chew through the trees. Jaws chew through the gardens. Clothes on a line become food too.

2The noise of millions of jaws is loud. It sounds like a grass fire. Then the cloud lifts. It is gone as fast as it came. The farms are left with nothing. A plague of locusts has just passed over them. A locust will eat nearly every green part of a plant. Leaves are food. Stems are food. Flowers are food. Fruit is food. Plagues of locusts appear in many religious books. Across history, swarms of locusts made people afraid. These grasshoppers are large. Their bodies are full of color. Millions seemed to come from an empty sky. Every plant in view was eaten. Farmers lost their crops to the swarm. Grass was eaten too.

3Other animals then had nothing to eat. A swarm big enough could bring famine. Ancient writings speak of locust plagues. Religious writings speak of them too. People often read the swarm as a warning. They also read it as a punishment. In the eighteen hundreds, pioneers moved west across the United States. Locust swarms ruined the crops they had planted. The year 1874 brought locusts over the Great Plains. The insects ate crops across more than 5 million square kilometers. That area is about 2 million square miles. Wool was eaten off sheep that were still alive. Clothing was taken off people's backs. The locusts ate that clothing. Locusts even pushed into kitchen cupboards. Everything inside those cupboards was eaten.

4So many bodies were crushed on the roads that the surface grew too slick for travel. Crushed locusts made train rails slick as well. The ruined crops were worth millions of dollars. Many families left the farms they had just begun. A picture from 1890 shows a farmer in Nebraska. He is in a fight he cannot win. Locusts are the other side of that fight. One of the most frightening things was a mystery. Nobody knew the place locusts came from. In quiet years, a swarm could not be found. A single locust could not be found either. Then millions showed up with no warning. Female locusts put their eggs into the soil. After that, the swarm moved on.

5The following year was quiet again. Those eggs did not produce new locusts. A picture shows the females placing eggs in loose soil. Where had the locusts gone? When would the swarms return? Why had they vanished? Scientists took up the puzzle in the early twentieth century. Boris Uvarov was one scientist who took up the work. His subject was the desert locust. That species does great harm. Its home region is the Middle East. The tests he ran changed what people know about locusts. One picture shows Boris Uvarov. Another picture shows a desert locust lifting into the air. Only a handful of locust species still plague people today. The desert locust is one of that small group.

6Uvarov learned something odd about the eggs. What hatched was not a baby locust. Short horns marked these young grasshoppers. Their size was less than the locust parents' size. Their color was duller than the parents' color. Their actions were quite different too. Traveling swarms were not something they formed. A label can point to the grasshopper form. Short-horned grasshoppers kept to themselves. They stayed away from other grasshoppers. Each one kept to one small patch of ground. The damage they did was never large. That left a hard question about where locusts came from. Uvarov ran more tests. He made the short-horned grasshoppers live packed close together. What happened next was a shock. If the grasshoppers kept knocking into neighbors, their color changed.

7They no longer kept apart. They entered a group. The group grew into a swarm. Then the whole group rose into the air. Grasshoppers that had been harmless were locusts now. More than twelve thousand grasshopper species exist. Only about a dozen of them turn into locusts. Scientists think more species may do this in the future. A grasshopper and a locust can be shown side by side. A body changes when a grasshopper becomes a locust. Behavior is the larger change. A grasshopper lives by itself. Many locusts live with a group. Uvarov showed that some animals are able to change. Surrounding conditions guide the change. Staying alive is the benefit.

8In a drought, the food supply for grasshoppers gets smaller. The grasshoppers go where the food is. Every grasshopper in an area can end up in one crowded spot. A wetland is one kind of spot. When the crowd grows too tight, certain grasshoppers change. They change into locusts. The group flies off to hunt for more food. They set down in one place. They eat all the food in that place. Then the group leaves. Turning into locusts helps these grasshoppers last through a drought. A drought means less food for everyone. If enough grasshoppers press into a small space, some species change. The change makes them locusts. Farmers today can still be harmed by locusts.

9Swarms usually appear in dry regions. North Africa is one of those regions. The Middle East is one of them. Australia is one of them. Uvarov's finding changed the way people fight locusts. Before the finding, poison was sprayed over a wide stretch of land. The spray was meant to stop a swarm. Other living things were hurt by that poison. Insects that help people were hurt. Bees were among them. Some farmers set their crops on fire. The fire wiped out the swarm. The fire also wiped out the crops. At times, even that work was not enough. Poison can kill insects that do harm. The same poison can hurt other living things. Weather radar can pick up insects as they cross an area.

10The insects on the screen may be locusts. Radar can send farmers a warning. The warning says a swarm is on the way. Today, people watch grasshoppers with care. They watch the weather too. They notice the start of a drought. Instruments that read the weather can find a swarm early. They can find it while the swarm is still small. Farmers can place tiny amounts of poison. The poison goes on small patches of land. Used this way, it stops big swarms from forming. Crops worth millions of dollars can be saved. Famines caused by locusts are rare now. A clearer understanding of these insects helps people live beside them.

59. Why Weather Changes on Earth

1San Francisco, California, is known for weather full of fog. This book begins by introducing Earth. Then it explains how scientists measure weather. A field guide to climate comes next. The guide covers latitude. It covers elevation. It covers large bodies of water. Seasons follow the guide. Then the book explores climate patterns on Earth. A word list and an index close the book. Weather can change a great deal from one part of the world to another. Ocean water and air move around the planet. The same ocean water meets the coast of North America and the coast of Asia. A breeze that moves curtains in South America also stirs leaves on trees in Africa. Even so, one region can be unlike another. A heavy coat is never needed in some places. People in other places always keep a heavy coat on. In some places, farmers pray for rain. In other places, rain comes every single day. Let us ask why places on our planet have weather that is so different. How many weather words can you list?

2Hot, cold, warm, and chilly belong on the list. Rainy, sunny, snowy, and foggy belong there too. You could keep adding words. Every one of those words points to two ideas. One idea is temperature. The other idea is precipitation. Temperature is the measure of a place's heat or its cold. Precipitation is water that drops from the sky onto the land. It may fall as rain. It may fall as snow, sleet, or hail. Tracking the weather of a place means measuring two things. One question is how hot it is. The other question is how wet it is. Climate is the usual weather of a place. Scientists look across long periods of time. A map in this book marks Seattle, Washington. It marks the Alps mountain range. It marks Europe, Asia, and North America. It marks San Francisco, California. It marks Africa and the equator. It marks South America and Australia. It marks Sydney, Australia. Green areas on the map have wet climates. Dry climates show up as brown or white on the map.

3Can you find the labeled places when they appear later in this book? People can record weather facts on graphs. Look at the graph of temperature. It shows Sydney, Australia. It covers the first half of the year, a span of six months. Numbers along the left side are degrees of temperature. Now look at the graph of precipitation. The two graphs look much the same. On the precipitation graph, left-side numbers are inches of moisture. Scientists learned that Earth has many climate patterns. They gathered weather records over time. They gathered those records in many places. Sunlight hits Earth in a weaker way near the poles. Sunlight hits Earth in a stronger way near the equator. What shapes the climate patterns of Earth? The story begins with the Sun. The Sun warms the planet. The warmth does not fall evenly on the surface. The equator is a line people imagine. It circles the middle of Earth the way a belt does. Places near that line tend to be much warmer. Places far north or far south tend to be cooler.

4Earth has the shape of a ball. Because of that shape, sunlight meets the ground at different angles. The rays are most direct around the middle of Earth. The North Pole gets the least straight rays. The South Pole gets that same weak angle. Try this test. Put an X in the middle of a sheet of paper. Ask a friend to hold the paper so it faces you. Aim a flashlight at the paper. Make a round patch of light around the X. Ask your friend to tip the paper slowly away from you. As the paper tips, the round patch stretches. It turns into an oval. The same light now covers more of the paper. The X is still inside the light. The X gets less light than it got before. Leave the equator toward either pole, and the same change happens. The curve of Earth leans farther from the Sun. Polar regions stay cold for this reason. They get less direct sunlight than places near the equator.

5A diagram shows lines of longitude and lines of latitude. The diagram marks degrees such as 0 and 10. It also marks 20, 30, and 40. It marks 50, 60, and 70. It marks 80 and 120 as well. It also marks Greenwich, England. Grid lines of latitude and longitude can name any spot on Earth. Latitude degrees measure distance from the equator toward the north or the south. Longitude degrees measure distance from Greenwich, England, toward the east or the west. Latitude is our system for that north-and-south measure. The equator sits at 0 degrees of latitude. The North Pole sits at 90 degrees north. The South Pole sits at 90 degrees south. A smaller latitude number means the place is nearer the equator. A larger latitude number means the place is farther away. Climate near the equator is usually warm. Climate far from the equator is usually cold. Elevation is height above sea level or below it. Elevation can change climate. Picture yourself on the equator. Picture a very high mountain under your feet.

6You would still feel a cold climate. Air gets about three degrees Fahrenheit colder for each 1,000 feet you climb. Mountains change precipitation too. Air holds water vapor. Water vapor is water as a gas. Air tends to travel in set directions around the planet. Those winds are called prevailing winds. When wind meets a mountain chain, the air must rise. The rising air cools. Cool air holds less moisture than warm air. The air then drops that moisture on the windward side. That moisture comes down as rain or as snow. Sleet and hail fall there too. The far side is the leeward side. It stays very dry. A dry area behind a mountain wall is called a rain shadow. A picture of a rain shadow shows dry air and warm, moist air. It also shows forest, sea, and desert. Seattle, Washington, has mild weather in part because of its neighbor. Puget Sound is the large water beside the city. Large bodies of water change temperature. They change precipitation too.

7Water warms more slowly than land does. Water also cools more slowly than land does. A large body of water works like insulation for the land beside it. It keeps that land from heating too fast. It keeps that land from cooling too fast. Winter stays mild in places next to big water. Summers there are mild too. Land with no big water nearby behaves in another way. Winters there are often very cold. Summers there are often very hot. In those places, the air holds too little water vapor. The air cannot work as insulation. Ocean currents matter to climate too. Cold currents travel from pole to equator. Warm currents travel the other way, from equator to pole. Air over a current slowly matches the water's temperature. A prevailing wind may blow from the water onto the land. That wind usually warms the region or cools it. Western Europe sits at a high latitude. A cold climate would seem likely there. A warm current changes the story. The current is the North Atlantic Drift.

8It warms the air above the sea. Western Europe gets a milder climate because of it. The same current brings a wet climate. Warm air can hold more moisture than cool air. Air that moves over Europe carries a heavy load of water vapor. People in this rainy region need raincoats. They need umbrellas too. A map shows this warm current. It labels Europe, the North Atlantic Drift, and North America. It also labels Africa and South America. Earth's tilt is what gives Earth its seasons. A diagram shows the axis of Earth. It shows spring, summer, fall, and winter. In one part, the axis leans away from the Sun. In another part, the axis leans toward the Sun. The equator is marked on the diagram. In one season, sunlight is more direct above the equator. In another season, sunlight is more direct below the equator. Earth travels around the Sun one time each year. Most climates on the equator stay hot the whole year. Direct sunlight is the reason. Away from the equator, climate changes with the seasons.

9The tilt of Earth causes that change. Study the diagram. In January, the Northern Hemisphere leans away from the Sun. Winter comes to that half of Earth. In July, that half leans toward the Sun. Summer comes then. The Southern Hemisphere has the opposite seasons. It leans the other way from the Northern Hemisphere. A green valley sits in the Alps. That mountain range is in Central Europe. You have had a short tour of the forces that shape climate. Choose a spot on a globe. Think about what shapes the climate there. How far is the spot from the equator? Is the elevation high, or is it low? Are mountains close by? Is a large body of water close by? Which season is it there today? If you can answer, you can probably guess the weather. You can guess without leaving home. Here are word meanings from the book. Climate means the weather of an area across a long time. Elevation is how high the land sits above the sea. The equator is an imagined line around Earth, halfway between the poles.

10It marks zero degrees of latitude. Insulation is a material that keeps heat in. It stops warmth from leaking out. It stops cold from coming in. On a globe, latitude lines go from east to west. They measure how far north or south a place is from the equator. Leeward means the side that faces away from the wind. Precipitation is water that falls from clouds. Rain is one form. Snow is another. Sleet is another. Hail is another. Prevailing winds are winds that usually blow the same way. A rain shadow is a dry area. That dry area sits on the leeward side of mountains. A region is a place, a location, or an area. Temperature is the reading of heat or cold in a place. Water vapor is water in gas form. Windward means the side the wind blows from. The index lists regions and seasons. It lists temperature and water. It lists climates and graphs. It lists elevation, latitude, and precipitation.

60. Wild Water and Grass in Florida

1In 1947, part of the Everglades became a national park. This book starts with a place that is strange and wonderful. Then it shows why the region is more than a swamp. Next it tells how the park came to be. Then it tells how people guard the park. After that, it steps into the wild. A list of hard words comes at the end. Have you heard of a gumbo-limbo? Have you heard of a skink? Have you heard of a stinkpot? These names do not belong to cartoon characters. They are plants and animals. Their home is Everglades National Park in Florida. No other place is quite like this strange, wonderful land. A skink is a kind of lizard. A stinkpot is a kind of turtle. A gumbo-limbo is a kind of tree. Everglades National Park sits inside the wider area called the Everglades. Much of South Florida falls inside that wider area.

2The park itself is only a small piece of the region. Inside the United States, no subtropical wilderness is larger than this region. Summers there are hot. Summers there are wet. Winters there are mild. Winters there are dry. A map can show the Everglades region and the national park. Lands near the map include Georgia and Alabama. The map also shows the Atlantic. It shows the Gulf of Mexico. Florida is the state that holds the park. Some people picture the region as nothing but one big wet swamp. Water is plentiful in the Everglades. Those waters link different ecosystems. Each ecosystem supports its own forms of life. Many mammals there live nowhere else. The same is true of many reptiles, birds, and fish. Freshwater cypress swamps lie to the north. The name comes from the cypress trees that grow in them.

3The swamps cover more ground than the whole state of Rhode Island. Many kinds of animals live in those swamps. Panthers live there. Rabbits live there. Turkey live there. Deer live there. Mangrove swamps are swamps of salt water. They lie at the southern tip of the Everglades. Mangrove trees grow in that salt water. Their long roots rise above the water. Cypress trees grow odd shapes called knees. The knees come up from the roots. Part of a knee stays above the water. That part brings air down to the rest of the roots. The other part stays under the water. That part anchors, or supports, the whole root system. No Indigenous people held the Everglades region before the Calusa. They also took care of that land. Their society was complex. Most of the Calusa died from diseases that came from Europe. The Seminole tribe grew from Indigenous groups in three states.

4Those states were Florida, Georgia, and Alabama. In 1835, the military of the United States began a war on the Seminole people. The Seminole people followed their leader, Abiaka. They went into the swampy Everglades. That military did not win against the Seminole people. Today this determined people has its own government. It has its own schools. It has its own police. It also owns hotels and casinos. A photo can show a member of the Seminole tribe. Mangrove roots are long. They keep the trees steady while ocean tides rise and fall. Other living things make homes in those roots. Algae live there. Tiny shellfish live there. Fish hide among the roots. The hiding place keeps them safe from animals that might eat them. Sawgrass marshes lie between the different swamps. Sawgrass grows as a tall plant right in the water. Every blade has sharp teeth, like the teeth of a saw.

5Alligators in the marshes use sawgrass when they build nests. Sharp teeth run along the edge of a sawgrass blade. A female alligator builds her nest above the water. She does this so the eggs stay safe. In a hardwood hammock, trees grow close together. Their cover keeps the ground shady and cool. Areas called hardwood hammocks hold trees that are thick with leaves. The leaves stop sunlight. The ground below stays in shade. So little light gets through that few plants can grow on the ground. Those plants use the trees, rather than the soil, as a place to grow. From there they can reach toward the sun. Four main ecosystems shape much of the Everglades. A sawgrass marsh lies between fresh water and salt water. A mangrove swamp is a saltwater ecosystem. A hardwood hammock is a freshwater ecosystem.

6Cypress swamps are freshwater ecosystems too. Salt water and fresh water both appear across the Everglades. Some spots mix the two. For many years, white settlers stayed away from the Everglades. Then a change came in 1903. The government of the United States gave Florida a huge piece of land. That land measured 20 million acres. The same tract is also given as 8,000,000 square kilometers. Developers wanted the water drained. The government wanted that too. They hoped to turn the land into farms. Developers also hoped to build homes and sell them. Soon the work of drying the land began. Bit by bit, the Everglades was being destroyed. Some people wanted the land protected. One of them was the writer Marjory Stoneman Douglas. In the 1940s, she wrote a book. She called it The Everglades: River of Grass. The book was a great success. It drew eyes to the park project.

7That attention helped save the natural resources of the area. A stamp for the park was made in 1947. That same year, the park itself was created. Stoneman Douglas spent five years learning about the Everglades. She wrote in favor of making the area a national park. In 1934, the government agreed to set land aside for a park. The land measured 1.5 million acres. The same measure is also written as 6,070 square kilometers. The times were hard. No money was on hand to build the park. At last, in 1947, the park was created. Hard work by people such as Stoneman Douglas made that possible. Today, development is kept out of the park. Other troubles remain. Dozens of animal species in the park are in danger. Dozens of plant species are in danger too. Their homes face risk. People have changed the climate. Those changes affect patterns of weather.

8A marsh, unlike a swamp, has no trees. Fire is part of the reason. Florida has many storms with lightning. The lightning often starts brush fires. Fire burns plants and trees that grow in the marsh. Young trees die in the fire. Sawgrass does not die. Its roots grow deep. The wet, rich soil of the marsh keeps those roots safe. After the fire passes, new leaves sprout from the plant. Life in the park is falling out of balance in another way. Species that are not native now live in Florida. Those plants and animals arrived from other places. Some of them were pets that people let go. Some plants grew from seeds that people threw away. Wind carried other seeds into the state. The new species cause trouble. They eat animals that native species need. They also eat plants that native species need.

9Special programs now work to remove unwanted species from the Everglades. In 2009, scientists hold a huge python. They caught it inside the national park. Pythons come from Asia. Pet owners who set them free cause large problems in the Everglades. Many people visit the Everglades today. Some hike trails that pass through the swamps. Some paddle through the marshes. Some camp beneath the hardwood hammocks. A wide mix of life appears along the way. Many visitors like to kayak among the cypress swamps. Bicycles share the road known as Shark Valley Loop Road. The manatee is a native species. The southeastern coast of the United States is part of where it lives. Florida is its main range. The park is a special place. Unusual plants grow there. Unusual animals live there. People travel from around the world to see them in the wild. Visitors also come to enjoy the natural beauty.

10No other park is quite like this one. The park covers 1.5 million acres. That size is also 607,000 hectares. More than 1 million visitors arrive each year. More than 40 types of mammals live in the park. Over 360 types of birds live there. The count of tree types there is about 120. Unique reptile kinds there add up to more than 50. Development means putting up houses, businesses, or roads on empty land. An ecosystem is a community of living things plus the places they live. Mild means not extreme. A national park is land the government owns and protects. The reason may be history, natural landscape, or wildlife. Roots are the underground parts of a plant. They hold the plant in place. From the soil, they draw water and nutrients for the plant. Subtropical describes land along the edge of the tropics.

61. Yanomami Life Deep in the Amazon

1Contents. The Amazon. Living for Today and Tomorrow. Life in the Rainforest. Yanos and Community. Gathering and Gardening. Hunting and Fishing. Yanomami Fashion. Yanomami Land Today. A Closing. Word Meanings. The Amazon. Rainforests are the oldest living ecosystems on the planet. They grow on every continent except Antarctica. More than seventy million years is how long they have existed. Earth's biggest rainforest is the Amazon. It lies in South America. The Amazon and other rainforests like it matter a great deal. On Earth, few habitats hold a wider mix of living things. People live in them. Indigenous tribes live in them. One tribe is the Yanomami. Yanomami is said yah-noh-MAH-mee. Count the plant species and the animal species of the world. These forests hold 80 percent of that total. Rainforests offer habitats that wildlife needs. Without those habitats, much wildlife could decline. Much wildlife could face extinction. Rainforests help clean Earth's air. They help clean Earth's water. They help regulate Earth's climate. People sometimes call rainforests natural air filters. Trees there can take pollution out of the air. They release clean oxygen. Rainforests filter Earth's waterways in a similar way. Life on Earth would not stay as it is if the rainforests were gone. Living for Today and Tomorrow.

2The Yanomami tribe calls the Amazon rainforest home. The tribe is one of the oldest cultures in the world. More than five thousand years is how long Indigenous tribes have been in the Amazon. The Yanomami are one of those tribes. Today that land is called Brazil. It is also called Venezuela. Those countries did not exist yet. The Yanomami were already living on that land. What they know of this rainforest was gathered across thousands of years. Their way of life depends on harmony with the forest. Many species of plants share that home. Many species of animals share it too. Because they rely on the rainforest, they avoid overuse. They do not take more resources than they should. They do not harm the environment. A map shows Yanomami indigenous territory. The map is set in South America. The Atlantic Ocean is labeled. Venezuela is labeled. Guyana is labeled. French Guiana is labeled. Colombia is labeled. Suriname is labeled. Peru is labeled. Brazil is labeled. The Yanomami live in a sustainable way. Sustainable means life today is planned for tomorrow. The plan leaves a healthy rainforest behind. They avoid cutting more trees than survival needs. They avoid picking more fruit than survival needs. They avoid hunting more animals than survival needs.

3They hope their children will still have the trees. They hope their grandchildren will still have the fruit. They hope the animals will still be there. They hope the rainforest ecosystem will stay healthy. Dangers When Rainforests Lose Trees. As rainforests shrink, Earth's air is affected. Earth's water is affected too. Habitats of hundreds of plant species are affected. Habitats of hundreds of animal species are affected. Deforestation means the removal of trees. It is still a problem for the Amazon. It is still a problem for other rainforests. A man cuts trees in the Amazon rainforest. The Amazon is lush through the whole year. Life in the Rainforest. What the Yanomami need has come from the Amazon over thousands of years. They have been able to survive on it. They have not had to trade with other nations. The goods they require come from the forest. Many other Indigenous people in South America hold this same view. In their belief, the spiritual world matters deeply. They use what they know of local plants. That knowledge brings them closer to the spiritual world. They use what they know of local animals too. The forest also meets daily needs. Shelter is one need.

4Food is one need. Clothing is one need. Do You Know? Yanomami can be spelled in more than one way. Other spellings are Yanoama, Yanomama, and Yanomamo. A whole Yanomami village may share one round roof. Yanos and Community. The Yanomami live in big round huts. The huts are called yanos. Wooden poles go into the building. Vines go into it. Thatched leaves go into it. From above, a yano looks like a big circle. A hole is open in the middle. The center is open to the sky. Adults meet in that center. Children play in that center. One yano often holds a whole village. Many families share the same roof. They do not live in separate rooms. A separate area belongs to each family. Family members eat in that area. They sleep in that area. A separate fire pit belongs to each family. The pit is for cooking. Each person has a hammock for sleep. A hammock can be made from vines. It can be made from bark. It can be made from cotton. Gathering and Gardening. This rainforest is familiar ground to the Yanomami. They know which herbs help which illnesses. Plants that are safe to eat are known to them. They know which plants are poisonous.

5They gather wild foods. Fruit is one wild food. Nuts are another. Roots are another. Insects are another. Gardens are also set into the rainforest by them. Tree cutting starts on small patches of land. Then they burn those trees. Next they plant sugar cane. They plant plantains. They plant sweet potatoes. They plant manioc. They plant other crops too. The garden moves every few years. The move keeps the land from harm. The old garden is left to grow. It grows back into rainforest. When food from gathering and gardening is not enough, they leave on a wayumi. A wayumi is their name for a rainforest trek. The entire village travels. The travel can last days. It can last weeks. They search for food as they go. On a wayumi they eat roots. They make hammocks from bark. They sleep under leaves. The leaves keep them dry. Each village has a garden of its own. Each family keeps a small piece inside the village garden. Hunters seek animals using bows and arrows. Hunting and Fishing. Hunters use longbows. They use arrows made of wood. Bird feathers are attached to the arrows. The feathers help the arrows fly straight. Sometimes the arrowheads are dipped in poison.

6Bare feet carry the hunters on rainforest paths. They look for anteaters. They look for tapirs. They look for other animals. When an animal is killed, the meat is shared. The entire village gets a share. The Yanomami catch fish in several ways. River fish are the target when some hunters shoot arrows. Other people pull baskets along the top of the water. They also use the ayori-toto vine. That vine is poisonous. They cut the vine. They slide it through the water. Poison drips from the vine into the water. Nearby fish are stunned. The fish float up to the surface. The Yanomami scoop the fish up. Yanomami Fashion. Clothing choices often depend on where people live. They depend on how people live. They depend on which materials people can get. The Yanomami get what they need from the rainforest. They keep the rainforest's conditions in mind. The rainforest is often hot. It is often rainy. So the Yanomami dress in a light way. Light dress helps them stay cool. It helps them stay mobile. Men often wear loincloths. Women often wear fringed aprons. Children tend to wear little clothing. Some children wear no clothing. That choice is for comfort in the heat.

7Body paint is a common choice for the Yanomami. They use different colors. Red dye comes from seeds. Purple dye comes from palm fruit. Black powder comes from charcoal. Many designs use dots. Many designs use lines. A Yanomami child wears paint on the face. Paint also covers the body. Some Yanomami put on tattoos instead of paint. Their tattoos serve many cultural purposes. A tattoo can link a person to the spiritual world. A tattoo can show a person's accomplishments. A tattoo can mark the change into adulthood. Some Yanomami people pierce the nose. They pierce the lips. They pierce the ears. Small wooden sticks are used as nose rings. They are used as lip rings too. Bird feathers are used as earrings. Other jewelry comes from the rainforest. Necklaces are made of seeds. Armbands are made of fragrant leaves. Sometimes small white feathers crown the head. The feathers are from a hawk. A Yanomami girl in one picture wears jewelry made nearby. Another shows a Yanomami boy looking at his face paint. Yanomami Land Today. Today the Amazon is still home to about 20,000 Yanomami. They live much as their ancestors lived. They still hunt. They still gather food. They still sleep under yanos.

8They still make clothing from rainforest materials. The past fifty years brought a rise in outsiders. These outsiders do not share the Yanomami knowledge of the rainforest. Respect for that forest is not shared by these outsiders. Outsiders travel by motorized boats. They travel by trucks. They travel by airplanes. They come to benefit from the land. The land is fertile. The land is rich in minerals. They mine. They hunt. They fish. They cut down trees. Their methods are not sustainable. Care for the forest is not their habit, which sets them apart from the Yanomami. They do not keep the rainforest healthy. Outsider numbers rose in the 1970s. Garimpeiros were a large part of that outside group. Garimpeiros is a Portuguese word. It means wildcat gold miners. The garimpeiros came onto Yanomami land. They hoped to strike it rich. Finding gold mattered more to them than harm to the rainforest. Some people on Yanomami land are the garimpeiros. Few threats are larger than gold mining. It threatens the Yanomami. It threatens the rainforest. The wish to get rich has increased illegal mining. Gold mining has harmed the rainforest in many ways. Trees are lost. Toxic chemicals cause pollution. Those chemicals enter major waterways. They enter the soil.

9Outsiders bring one kind of harm. Forest fires bring another. Global warming causes those fires. Life for the Yanomami is in great danger. The Amazon rainforest is in great danger too. If this rainforest loss does not stop, suffering will come to the whole world. The Yanomami will suffer along with the world. A healthy climate for the globe gets support from this rainforest. Facing outsiders is a task the Yanomami cannot avoid. Outsiders bring diseases. They bring destruction of the forest. They bring pollution of the forest. That forest supports the Yanomami way of life. Tree cutting in the Amazon rainforest goes on day after day. A young Yanomami woman is weaving a basket. A Closing. Yanomami culture is rich with knowledge of the Amazon rainforest. The knowledge reaches back for centuries. The culture also holds discipline. It holds the skills needed to live in a sustainable way. Many people have never met the Yanomami directly. Even so, their problems have captured attention worldwide. The Yanomami have been shown in books. They have been shown in magazines. They have been shown in movies. They have been shown in art. People who study this culture hope others will learn from it. The lesson is how to prevent more harm.

10The harm falls on the Yanomami. It falls on the Amazon rainforest. Everyone on Earth has a reason to care about the Amazon rainforest. A great share of the oxygen in the world comes from it. It affects the world's climate. Plant life of many kinds depends on it. It supports a large variety of animal life. The Yanomami people make their home in it. Word Meanings. ancestors: family from a time long past. culture: the customs and daily ways of one group. It is also their ways of living. ecosystems: living things sharing a place, and the habitats around them. edible: safe to be eaten. loincloths: small pieces of clothing. They are worn around the hips. manioc: a starchy root that grows on a tree in the tropics. People use the root for food. methods: particular ways to do something. plantains: wild bananas. They grow in the tropics. resources: supplies of a thing that is valuable or very useful. sustainable: able to be used without using a resource all up. The use does not cause permanent damage. tapirs: big animals with hooves. They are related to the horse. They are related to the rhinoceros. trek: a long journey made on foot.
